<meta>This page serves as a modern preface or legal disclaimer for the digitized manuscript, explaining the history of its electronic preservation and the terms of its public use.</meta>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the <term>copyright|Legal ownership of a creative work that restricts how it can be copied or shared</term> to expire and the book to enter the <term>public domain|A status where a work is no longer protected by intellectual property laws and belongs to the community at large</term>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other <term>marginalia|Handwritten notes or marks made in the margins of a book by past readers or librarians</term> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>original: "OCR"; a process of turning images of text into computer-readable text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

->*Blessed Angela of Foligno,*<-
->*after the fresco by Pierantonio Mezzastris:*<-
->*Church of Saint Anna, Foligno.*<-

<note>The image depicted here is a reproduction of a 15th-century fresco portrait. It shows Blessed Angela of Foligno (1248–1309), a prominent Italian mystic and member of the Secular Franciscan Order. She is portrayed with a halo and her hands crossed over her chest, a traditional gesture of prayerful devotion and humility. The artist, Pierantonio Mezzastris, was a notable painter from the Umbrian school, active in the region where Angela lived and died.</note>

<summary>This page contains a portrait of the Franciscan mystic Blessed Angela of Foligno, based on a Renaissance fresco located in the Church of Saint Anna in Foligno.</summary>

# THE BOOK
## of the Divine
## Consolation of
## the Blessed
## <term>Angela Da Foligno</term> <note>Angela of Foligno (1248–1309) was an Italian mystic and Franciscan tertiary. She is often called the "Mistress of Theologians" because of the depth of her recorded spiritual visions.</note>
### 1909 <note>original: "MCMIX"</note>

<note>The "Divine Consolation" (Latin: *Liber de vere fidelium experientia*) is the primary record of Angela's spiritual journey, dictated to her confessor, Brother Arnaldo. It describes her conversion and her thirty "steps" toward mystical union with God.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the formal title page for a 1909 edition of the spiritual writings of the Franciscan mystic Blessed Angela of Foligno.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Divine Consolation, Franciscan mysticism, title page, 1909 edition, Christian literature</keywords>

<margin>B.F.H.</margin> <note>These initials belong to the artist Blanche F. Hunter, who adapted the design from a medieval manuscript held at the British Museum.</note>

<note>The layout of this title page is characteristic of the early 20th-century "Fine Press" movement, which sought to bring the beauty of medieval books to modern printing. The first letter of the word "The" is a <term>historiated initial</term>—a decorative capital letter containing a scene or figure—which depicts Angela of Foligno seated at a desk. Angela (c. 1248–1309) was an Italian Christian mystic and member of the Secular Franciscan Order; her "Consolations" record her intense spiritual visions and dialogues with God as transcribed by her confessor.</note>

## Consolation of
## the Blessed

<margin>B.F.H.</margin> <note>These initials belong to the artist Blanche F. Hunter, who adapted the design from a medieval manuscript held at the British Museum.</note>

<note>The layout of this title page is characteristic of the early 20th-century "Fine Press" movement, which sought to bring the beauty of medieval books to modern printing. The design is inspired by manuscript illumination. Angela of Foligno (c. 1248–1309) was an Italian Christian mystic and member of the Secular Franciscan Order; her "Consolations" record her intense spiritual visions and dialogues with God as transcribed by her confessor, Brother Arnaldo.</note>

# CONTENTS

## TREATISE I

->PAGE<-

**OF THE CONVERSION AND PENITENCE OF THE BLESSED ANGELA OF FOLIGNO AND OF HER MANY AND DIVERS TEMPTATIONS . . . . . I**
<note>Treatise I describes Angela’s radical transformation from a wealthy woman of Foligno to a "Step-daughter of Christ." It recounts thirty "steps" of her conversion, beginning with the fear of damnation and ending in mystical union.</note>

## TREATISE II

->**OF THE EVANGELICAL DOCTRINE SET FORTH BY**<-
->**THE BLESSED ANGELA**<-

<margin>CHAP.</margin>
I. **How it may be known that God hath entered into the soul . . . . . 24**

II. **How the spiritual man is deceived . . . 31**
<note>In this context, "deceived" refers to spiritual pride or false visions that can lead a seeker away from true humility.</note>

III. **How, being lodged within the soul, God worketh alike upon the understanding, the affections, and the will . . . . 36**
<note>The "understanding, affections, and will" were considered the three main powers of the human soul in medieval philosophy.</note>

IV. **How that our perfection doth consist in knowing our own wretchedness and God’s mercy 40**

V. **The necessity of a constant consideration and profound knowledge of Christ Crucified . 42**
<note>As a Franciscan mystic, Angela emphasized "Christ Crucified" as the ultimate mirror for the soul's own suffering and salvation.</note>

<summary>This page lists the contents for the first two sections of the book, covering Angela of Foligno's initial conversion and her subsequent teachings on how God interacts with the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>Table of Contents, Angela of Foligno, conversion, penitence, temptations, evangelical doctrine, spiritual soul, Christ Crucified, medieval mysticism</keywords>

| | <note>The "Book of Life" is a popular medieval metaphor. Rather than a physical book, Angela presents the person of Christ as the ultimate source of knowledge and a "text" to be studied through prayer.</note> | |

| X. | How Christ revealed Himself poor in power . | 56 |

| | <note>The "infallible Guide" refers to Christ. In Angela's theology, the "straight road" is the path of suffering and humility that leads directly to God.</note> | |

# TREATISE III

## OF THE MANY VISIONS AND CONSOLATIONS RECEIVED BY THE BLESSED ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

| | PAGE |
|:---|:---:|
| First Vision and <term>Consolation</term>, in which she saw **God** in His aspect as absolute goodness; from which arises a distaste for this life and the desire to enjoy **God** . . . . . . . . | 157 |
| <note>"Consolation" refers to a spiritual gift or a profound sense of peace and divine presence felt during prayer.</note> | |
| Second Vision, in which she saw **God** in His aspect as beauty; because of this, all created beauty seemed deformed and hideous to her . . . . . . . | 169 |
| Third Vision, in which she saw **God** in His aspect as invincible <term>omnipotence</term> in all things, which gave her the grace to be of benefit both to the present and to future generations. Moreover, she also saw the deep humility of **God** . . . . . . . . | 171 |
| <note>"Omnipotence" refers to God’s all-powerful nature.</note> | |
| Fourth Vision and Consolation, in which she saw **God** in His aspect as supreme wisdom, by which she learned to judge all things without error . . . . . . . . | 173 |

# INTRODUCTION

**W**HAT is the secret and so potent attraction of the Saints? Renan <note>Ernest Renan (1823–1892), a French expert in Semitic languages and a historian of religion, famous for his controversial and secularized accounts of early Christianity.</note> says somewhere that he would have given everything he had to have seen St. Mary of Egypt <note>A legendary 4th-century ascetic who, after a life of dissolution, spent forty-seven years in the desert as a hermit to find spiritual peace.</note> pacing the desert in ecstasy, half-starved and turned to the semblance of Nebuchadnezzar <note>The Babylonian king who, according to the biblical Book of Daniel, was driven mad and lived like a wild beast in the fields.</note>. And Renan liberally discounted the value, not only of Theology, but also of the particular virtue, the loss of which had driven that Saint to such an unusual mode of life. The interest in sanctity evidently survives theological and ethical pre-occupations. Indeed, to-day, the Saint is perhaps an object of higher intrinsic interest to “unbelievers” than to the faithful. For to the faithful he is primarily *useful*, either as being efficacious in various troubles of life or, on a higher plane, as a sort of spiritual agent, obtaining graces for his clients. O wonderful exchange! <note>original: "O admirabile commercium !" — a phrase used in the Catholic liturgy to describe the mystical exchange between God and humanity, particularly through the Incarnation.</note> But, like everything else, this celestial intercourse suffers from the defects of its qualities.

I do not wish to be understood as making light of superstition. The humblest blossom of that luxuriant garden is of infinite value, nor do the roots of our most highly rationalised opinions grow outside it. Nevertheless the important position of the Saint in the Catholic economy <term>economy: derived from the Greek "oikonomia," referring here to the divine plan or the organized system of spiritual life within the Church.</term>

INTRODUCTION
page xx

does tend to conceal his real personality from his worshippers. He inevitably tends to be considered more as a means to an end, than as an object intrinsically worthy of contemplation. In these circumstances the actual historical value of his personality is apt to be obscured by legend and fancy. Legend, of course, if at all contemporaneous, is of the highest value as illustrating his effect on those with whom he came in contact. We could ill spare in the life of St. Francis the Wolf of Gubbio <note>A famous legend in which St. Francis of Assisi tamed a fierce wolf that was terrorizing the town of Gubbio, negotiating a peace between the townspeople and the animal.</note>. Modern devotional fancy is less illuminative. It throws no light upon the character of St. Anthony of Padua to learn that centuries after his death he recovered some papers lost by that devout man King Charles II <note>Charles II of England (reigned 1660–1685). St. Anthony of Padua is traditionally invoked as the patron saint for the recovery of lost items.</note>. What then is it that constitutes the intrinsic interest of the Saint when his supernatural value has gone?

One reason, I think, for this interest is that the Saint represents, in a quite unique manner, the satisfaction of a desire which all men more or less obscurely feel. Ever since man emerged from amid the labyrinth of irrational forces, which, until his appearance, determined the evolution of life on the planet, he has sought for power. Power at first over the hostile or indifferent nature which surrounded him, over the stream, the spark of fire, the wild bear: then over his fellow-men, and, at length, when he began to turn his gaze inwards, over himself. It is noticeable that all the really primitive myths <term>divinised</term> <note>Treating or worshiping something as a god or as divine.</note> various aspects of power, celebrated the triumph of force. As the social arts began to develop, and, among them, of sheer

# INTRODUCTION

necessity and morality, men began to attribute moral qualities to the force which they felt around them, above them, and within them. "Since we were unable to make that which is just strong, we have made that which is strong just," <note>original: "N’ayant pas pu faire que le juste soit fort, nous avons fait que le fort soit juste." This quote is from the French philosopher and mathematician Blaise Pascal (1623–1662), found in his work *Pensées*. It suggests that society labels power as "just" because it cannot always give power to "justice" itself.</note> says Pascal. This attribution, however, no less than morality itself, was an afterthought unconsciously conceived in the interest of his self-preservation—for, without morality of some sort, man would soon have disappeared before the wolf and the bear. And, by giving the ultimate sanction of force to his social rule of thumb, he naïvely betrayed his intuition that that ultimate force was the more fundamental reality. 

Now the Saint represents the achievement of this longing for power, carried to the highest and most difficult point, that of complete self-mastery. For while, to the primitive savage, the conquest of external foes of one sort and another is evidently the most pressing need of his position, a need recurring so constantly as to exhaust his store of energy, and veil from his attention other possible achievements, as life gets more secure and his attention is directed inwards, man becomes aware of those other dangerous foes of his own household, the appetites and caprices of his inherited brute nature. Turning to battle with these, he dimly but surely perceives one aspect of the ideal of sainthood. For the Saints of all religions, from the most elementary to the most highly developed, are <term>ascetics</term> <note>From the Greek word *askēsis*, meaning "exercise" or "training." In this context, it refers to the practice of self-discipline and abstaining from physical pleasures to achieve a higher spiritual state.</note>. They live sparely and chastely, and the mortification of the twin appetites of hunger and love represent to the ordinary man the acme of self-control.

->xxii<-

# INTRODUCTION

The Saint is also more than the ascetic. Asceticism is a refusal, a limitation, a constraint—in a word, a negation; and the Saint is eminently positive. Moreover, although negative qualities might impose on the imagination and create interest, they would not inspire the personal devotion that is invariably felt for him. The positive quality of the Saint is love, expressing itself in joy. Thus he is a fascinating combination of the familiar and the unfamiliar, for, if few of us are ascetics, we have most of us some experience of love. But the Saint drinks from the <term>Castalian spring</term> <note>In Greek mythology, the Castalian spring on Mount Parnassus was sacred to the Muses; here it represents a source of pure spiritual inspiration.</note> of his interior life while we hand about the Divinity to each other in treacherous and remarkably “earthen vessels.” <note>A biblical reference to 2 Corinthians 4:7, where human beings are described as fragile clay jars that hold the "treasure" of God's glory.</note> His love is not as ours at the mercy of circumstances, it is not susceptible of betrayal or death, for he has found his treasure beyond time and space, where neither satiety nor caprice can corrupt, nor can rivals break through and steal.

There have been all kinds of Saints, from <term>St. Paul, the first hermit</term>, <note>Paul of Thebes (c. 227–341 AD), traditionally regarded as the first Christian monk to live in the desert.</note> to <term>Father Damian</term>, <note>St. Damien of Molokai (1840–1889), the priest who lived in a Hawaiian leper colony to care for the sick.</note> but the “active” Saints seem less interesting than the “contemplative.” Certainly the <term>Fathers of the Desert</term> <note>The early Christian monks and hermits who lived in the Egyptian wilderness starting in the 3rd century.</note> are very seductive. They lived in caves or on the tops of pillars, supporting their existences on roots and brackish water, while they exhaled their souls in a hymn of timeless ecstasy like <term>Shelley’s skylark</term>. <note>A reference to Percy Bysshe Shelley's poem "To a Skylark," which portrays the bird as a "blithe spirit" singing with pure, unburdened joy.</note> Solitude was to them as water to the fish, and they preferred the society of beasts to that of men. St. Paul had his attendant lions, and <term>St. Anthony Abbot</term> <note>Anthony the Great (c. 251–356 AD), a prominent leader of the Desert Fathers known for his spiritual battles against demonic temptations.</note> spent twenty years in a tomb with serpents who turned into evil

<summary>This page argues that a Saint is defined by the positive quality of love and joy rather than the mere negation of asceticism. It highlights the allure of contemplative Saints, particularly the Fathers of the Desert, who found spiritual ecstasy in extreme solitude.</summary>
<keywords>Sainthood, asceticism vs positivity, contemplative life, Fathers of the Desert, St. Anthony Abbot, spiritual joy, love</keywords>

spirits by night <note>This refers to the "spiritual combat" or visions of demons described by the early Desert Fathers during their nocturnal vigils in the wilderness.</note>, and, in these unusual circumstances, became one of the most important personalities of his generation. The external conditions of their existence were often grotesque, almost always terrible. Their inner spirit was the most precious thing humanity possesses. For when everything else has failed a man, he arrives, if his courage holds out, at the joys of the spirit. The forces manifested in his experience, which succeeded in the long run in eluding his dominating grasp, answer submissively to the call of his mind in contemplation. Like fabled <term>Adam</term> naming the docile beasts <note>In the biblical Book of Genesis, Adam is given the task of naming the animals, symbolizing human intelligence and its authority to define and understand the world.</note>, he sits in their midst, assigning to each its post and due perspective in the panorama of life. For he has arrived, if without the help of <term>metaphysical analysis</term> <note>The formal philosophical study of the fundamental nature of reality and existence.</note>, by experience, at the conviction that nothing is real but thought which is the first—and perhaps the last—word of <term>philosophy</term>. <term>Mystics</term> and contemplatives of East and West of all creeds and rites have borne substantial witness to this truth. This is no doubt the reason why alike to the popular imagination as in the treatises of theologians the <term>contemplative life</term> <note>A way of living focused on prayer, meditation, and spiritual reflection rather than external labor.</note> is extolled at the expense of <term>active virtue</term> <note>The practice of doing good deeds and fulfilling moral duties through physical or social action.</note>. For, to all of us, there come moments when we are aware of a <term>psychological need</term>, more profound, more urgent, than the desire for action. Before certain works of art, or occasional aspects of nature, or it may be at the exquisite climax of some mood of intimate personal emotion, a delicious paralysis steals over the will: we feel that we have done enough. In the calm that follows the whirlwind and earthquake of <term>volition</term> <note>The faculty or power of using one's will to make a decision.</note> now, it

## INTRODUCTION

Blessed Angela of Foligno was a true daughter of this ancient line. Born in 1248, she entered the family of St. Francis as a <term>Tertiary Hermit</term> <note>A member of a "Third Order" (for laypeople rather than monks or nuns) who lived a life of prayer and solitude while remaining in the world.</note>, and became, through the spiritual autobiography, which she dedicated to her confessor <term>Fra Arnaldo</term> <note>Brother Arnold, the Franciscan friar who served as Angela's scribe and priest, recording her mystical experiences.</note>, one of its most striking illustrations. St. Francis and his "Knights of the Round Table"¹ seem from the first to have struck a new note in medieval religion. The <term>Monastic Order</term> <note>Organized religious communities, such as monks or nuns, who live together under a specific set of rules and vows.</note>, whether <term>Benedictine</term> <note>An ancient order of monks following the Rule of Saint Benedict, known for their balance of prayer, work, and study.</note> or <term>Cistercian</term> <note>A stricter reform branch of the Benedictines that emphasized poverty and manual labor, often building monasteries in remote areas.</note>, wrapped in the aloofness of its splendid cloisters, frequently governed by abbots who were great feudal lords, represented, it might be said, the aristocratic principle in spirituality. The aims of the monks were lofty, unintelligible no doubt to the <term>villeins</term> <note>Common laborers or peasants in the medieval feudal system who were bound to their lord's land.</note> of the soil, who were their dependents ; they fed and educated their humble neighbours, but their own life remained exclusive, a thing apart. St. Francis brought the spiritual life down to the people, the <term>common people</term> <note>original: "popolo minuto" — a term for the lower-class residents of medieval Italian cities, such as artisans and laborers.</note>, and he did so—here was his splendid originality—without lowering the values of what he brought within their reach. The religious democracy that he created remained an aristocracy of the soul. His logic was amazing because so simple. In most men, thought and action move on different planes ; with

---

¹ "These are my brothers, the knights of the Round Table." <note>original Latin: "Isti sunt fratres mei milites Tabulæ rotondæ."</note> *Mirror of Perfection* <note>original: "Spec. Perfec." (Speculum Perfectionis), a famous 14th-century collection of legends about the life of Saint Francis.</note>, p. 72.

<summary>This page introduces Blessed Angela of Foligno as a key figure in the Franciscan mystical tradition, contrasting Saint Francis's "spiritual democracy" for the common people with the more aristocratic and secluded monastic orders of the time.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

to them action implies at least some degree of compromise; but when St. Francis, stripping himself of his father’s cloak, and flying naked to the Bishop’s arms, proclaimed the divine royalty of poverty, that most unusual phenomenon was seen—a man’s thought and action in perfect harmony. Something of that divine simplicity, of that exquisite unison of thought and will, is what constitutes the <term>Franciscan</term> <note>Relating to the religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi, which emphasized living in poverty and preaching among the common people.</note> spirit, distinguishing all his authentic children and, not least of them, the simple woman who unfolds her wonderful experiences in this small volume.

We know little of her life. Born within thirty years of St. Francis’ death, she entered on the life of penance after a youth passed in moral disorder. She lived in solitude with a religious companion in the neighbourhood of the Church of the <term>Friars Minor</term> <note>The "Lesser Brothers," the formal name for the Franciscan order.</note> at Foligno, until the year of her death in 1309. Her “Visions,” which are of the most touching and beautiful description, appear to have been all of the kind described by mystical theologians as “intellectual,” that is to say they were unaccompanied by any sensible manifestations <note>In the context of mysticism, "intellectual" visions are spiritual insights perceived directly by the mind, rather than "sensible" visions seen with the physical eyes or heard with the ears.</note>. Some of them, indeed, as she notes herself, occurred during sleep. They are an extraordinary blend of naïve candour and passion. They indicate, with an accuracy which I feel tempted to ascribe at times to the editing of the possibly more analytical <term>Fra Arnaldo</term> <note>Brother Arnold, the Franciscan friar who served as Angela's confessor and scribe, recording her spoken experiences in Latin.</note>, the moments of what may be called the dialectical process of <term>sanctification</term> <note>The spiritual journey of being made holy or purified from sin.</note>. The point of departure of her conversion was a purely self-regarding dread of the penalties of sin: the contemplative mood of the love of

->xxvi<-
->INTRODUCTION<-

God, to which she ultimately attained, absorbed all the intervening emotional categories. This little book may in fact be called, in the <term>Hegelian</term> <note>Refers to the philosophy of Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831), who viewed history and thought as a "dialectic" process—a progression where conflicting ideas eventually resolve into a higher truth. The author suggests Angela's spiritual stages follow a similar logical progression.</note> sense of the term, the **Logic of sanctity**. It is certainly one of the most important documents we have of medieval psychology, and illustrates in a very remarkable manner the completeness of that system of <term>Mysticism</term> <note>A spiritual tradition focused on achieving direct, personal union or communion with the Divine through contemplation and prayer.</note> which was at once the root and the blossom of the medieval intellect. Only in Angela the intellect is hidden under a succession of emotional moments, which develop by their own spontaneous dialectic out of the whole mood of which each of them is the passing but necessary expression. That Mysticism is the finest and, on the whole, perhaps the sanest there has ever been. It is far from being exclusively Christian. Deriving from the lecture halls of Alexandria <note>A major center of learning in the ancient world where Greek philosophy, especially Neoplatonism, blended with early Christian theology.</note>, as well as from the hills of Galilee, it has come down to us through a series of great **experimentalists**, who have furnished to those of like mind with themselves its justification in their own experience. It is the classic Catholic Mysticism, as the Roman Church has always understood and still understands the term.

Few systems of thought have swayed the imagination more profoundly. Remaining a true Mysticism by nature of its goal, the <term>Beatific Vision</term> <note>original: "Visio Beatifica." In Catholic theology, this is the ultimate, direct sight of God enjoyed by the saints in heaven, representing the highest possible human fulfillment.</note>, it has succeeded in incorporating in itself the ethical aspirations of Western energy. It thus mediates between the passive ecstasy of Hindu <term>Pantheism</term> <note>The belief that God and the universe are one and the same; the author contrasts a perceived Eastern "passivity" with Western "activity."</note> and the restless volitional activity of the <term>white race</term> <note>This term reflects the racialized historical scholarship of the late 19th and early 20th centuries, which often categorized spiritual or cultural traits by race, viewing Europeans as uniquely characterized by "will" and "action."</note>.

<summary>This page characterizes Angela of Foligno’s mysticism as a sophisticated "Logic of sanctity" that bridges intellectual philosophy and emotional experience. The author positions this medieval mysticism as a balance between Eastern contemplative traditions and Western active energy.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

<meta>Digitized by Google</meta>

The dogma of the <term>Incarnation</term> <note>The central Christian doctrine that God took on human form in the person of Jesus Christ. The author argues this provides a "human face" to an otherwise unreachable God.</note> has here been of the greatest service. The Unknown and Unknowable All is contemplated by the Catholic Saint in the person of Christ. The dogma gives him a lens with which he can focus the rays of Divinity and unite them in a shaft of light on which he can gaze without faltering. In this way he is provided with an inexhaustible object, adequate to his mind and will. On the one hand he can never know the tragedy of satiety <note>The state of being fed or gratified to excess; the author means the mystic never grows bored or tired of God because the subject is infinite.</note>, for, although he may faint with fatigue, his object is inexhaustible, and, on the other, his will is not ruled out as an illusion, but fortified by the prospect of an infinite perspective of effort and achievement. For Catholic Mysticism may perhaps be best summed up, in the phrase by which a great philosopher of our day has described life itself, as a **creative evolution** <note>A reference to Henri Bergson (1859–1941), a famous philosopher of the era who argued that evolution was driven by a "vital impetus" or creative force rather than just random mutation.</note>. The acts of Virtue, of Faith, Hope, and Charity of a Saint, are not waste of time or of merely negative use; they do not merely serve the purpose of withdrawing him from temptation, they actually constitute the spiritual life within him, like the words of consecration in the <term>Mass</term> <note>The central act of Catholic worship. The author compares the saint's virtuous acts to the priest's words which, in Catholic belief, transform bread and wine into the Divine presence.</note>, they create in his heart the Divinity which they assert. The mystic gradually passes beyond this mood into that profound and eternal rest of the soul, which theologians call the <term>Beatific Vision</term> <note>From the Latin *visio beatifica*; the ultimate, direct experience of seeing God face-to-face, which is the final goal of human existence in Catholic theology.</note>, and which is, according to the teaching of <term>Aquinas</term> <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the primary philosopher and theologian of the medieval Church.</note>, enjoyed by some still in life with an even greater intensity than by others who literally “sleep in the Lord” <note>A traditional scriptural and liturgical phrase for those who have died in the faith; original Latin: *obdormivit in Domino*.</note>.

The Roman Church has ever regarded Catholic Mysti-

It notes that according to Thomas Aquinas, the highest spiritual state—the Beatific Vision—can potentially be reached even before death.</summary>

->INTRODUCTION<-

...mysticism as the kernel of the <term>Deposit of Faith</term> <note>original Latin: *Depositum Fidei*; the body of revealed truth in the Scriptures and tradition which the Church is tasked with protecting.</note> of which she is the guardian. It was for it she fought through the long and wearisome controversies which brought to the birth at **Nicaea** <note>The Council of Nicaea in 325 AD, which established the foundational orthodox Christian belief regarding the nature of Christ.</note> the orthodox dogma of the **Incarnation** <note>The central Christian belief that God took on human flesh in the person of Jesus.</note>. For, as has been said, without that dogma, Catholic Mysticism would have missed its specific and characteristic note and would have become a mere variant of **Neo-platonist theurgy** <note>A system of late Greek philosophy that used rituals and magic to attempt to achieve union with the divine.</note>. The inherent tendency that all Mysticism has to **Pantheism** <note>The belief that God is everything and the universe is identical with God.</note>, indeed to **Nihilism** <note>The philosophical view that suggests the lack of belief in any meaning or intrinsic value in life.</note>, would have inevitably asserted itself. Nothing short of faith in the descent of the Infinite into the finite could have saved the wavering lines of human personality, turned inwards to gaze upon itself. All, including that personality, would have become an unstable illusion, the web of <term>Maya</term> <note>original: *Maia*; a term from Indian philosophy referring to the "illusion" or "pretense" of the physical world.</note> woven by the fallacious dreams of human desire. And European religion, at least at the point of its highest individual development, would have been indistinguishable from Buddhism. When **Schopenhauer** <note>Arthur Schopenhauer (1788–1860), a philosopher who often compared Western and Eastern religions, finding them similar in their asceticism.</note> drew his parallel between Catholic and Buddhist Mysticism, he failed to see the enormous difference made by belief in the dogma of the **God-man** <note>original: *Man-god*.</note>. It is perhaps not without significance that he dwells so much on the value in that connection of **Madame Guyon** <note>Jeanne-Marie Bouvier de la Motte-Guyon (1648–1717), a French mystic whose writings on "Quietism" became highly controversial.</note>, whose <term>Quietist</term> <note>A mystical movement emphasizing a state of perfect passivity and the suppression of the will to achieve union with God.</note> tendencies, resting ultimately on a <term>Docetic</term> <note>From the Greek *dokein*, "to seem." Docetism was the belief that Jesus' physical body and his suffering were only appearances rather than reality.</note> view of the Incarnation, were the cause of her difficulties with **Bossuet** <note>Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet (1627–1704), a prominent French bishop and theologian who vigorously opposed Guyon’s mysticism.</note>.

At first sight perhaps the visions of the **Blessed Angela** <note>Angela of Foligno (1248–1309), a medieval Italian mystic and Franciscan tertiary whose spiritual experiences are recorded in her "Book of Visions and Instructions."</note> may seem to have only an archaic value for us. The world in which she lived seems so remote from ours, the circum-

## INTRODUCTION

circumstances of her life so different from anything that could possibly occur to us, that her experience seems hopelessly irrelevant to our own needs. But this is a superficial view. The human heart is always the same: there exists a law of what may be called **sentimental constancy** <note>The idea that human emotions and the capacity for spiritual feeling remain consistent across different centuries and cultures.</note>. And nothing that has ever been believed by anybody is without value. It is enough if the faith and the experience were really genuine. **Angela** <note>Blessed Angela of Foligno (1248–1309), a major Christian mystic who recorded her visions and "steps" toward God.</note> is an authentic specimen of a clearly-defined class of human beings. **Mystics** <note>Individuals who seek a direct, personal experience or union with the Divine, often through prayer or contemplation.</note> are very rare, but they exist. Moreover they are only rare in the West. Oriental bazaars are crowded with them; they prophesy at the corners of streets surrounded by yellow dogs, picturesquely clad disciples, and occasional European tourists. Quite a considerable number of human beings pass their lives in a state of more or less constant inspiration. We are apt to forget this and limit the possibilities of all experience to our own. It is therefore enlarging to our minds to step sometimes into other people's worlds. In the world of Blessed Angela we shall find much to interest and astonish us, and perhaps to excite our admiration. We shall at all events enjoy the commerce of a pure and candid soul.

The translation here offered to the reader is that of the first **Italian version** which was made in 1510 from the Latin of **Fra Arnaldo** <note>Angela’s Franciscan confessor and scribe, who translated her spoken Italian/Umbrian accounts into the official Church language of Latin.</note>. This version is one of the rarest books in the world, and has a special value as being one of the earliest popular devotional works printed in the <term>vernacular</term> <note>The language spoken by ordinary people in a particular country or region; in this case, Italian.</note> in Italy. # PREFACE BY THE OLD ITALIAN TRANSLATOR

### *To all Readers Beloved in Jesus Christ*

**A**LTHOUGH in the Holy Gospel our most loving Lord has abundantly shown us the means and the way by which we may reach the perfection of a Christian life, his <term>Consolating Spirit</term> <note>The Holy Spirit, viewed here in its role as the Comforter or 'Paraclete.'</note> (the giver of all comforting and spiritual grace) has nevertheless not ceased, nor will ever cease, to reveal to us continually—by means of His most worthy instruments, who are saints and devout people—various ways and conditions for finding the most perfect and complete union possible for <term>wayfarers</term> <note>original: "viatores." In theological terms, this refers to human beings on their earthly journey toward God, as opposed to those who have already reached heaven.</note> in this life. 

And although through the ease of printing there has been produced a countless number of books—so many that they obscure the <term>sun of justice</term> <note>original: "sol iustitiae." A biblical reference to Christ, often used to contrast divine truth with the 'darkness' of worldly distractions.</note> upon earth (seeing that there are more evil books than good ones)—this has happened because, due to their warped judgment and worldly desires, men take more delight in imagining and listening to harmful things rather than wholesome ones, and because through the world's abuses...

<summary>This page introduces the preface by the Italian translator, who argues that while the Gospels provide the path to perfection, the Holy Spirit continues to inspire saints to write new guides for spiritual union. He also laments that the invention of printing has led to a surplus of harmful books that distract from divine truth.</summary>

<keywords>Christian perfection, Holy Spirit, printing press, spiritual union, wayfarers, sun of justice, religious literature</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface from page xli, moving from a lament about the surplus of harmful books to the specific reasons for publishing this account of Angela of Foligno’s life and teachings.</meta>

<page-num>xlii</page-num>

<header>PREFACE</header>

...evil men are more favored than good men, yet malice cannot overcome wisdom, nor can the many overcome the few. For this reason, God has chosen the weak to shame the strong, and in our own times He has inspired many women of noble spirit. They led holy and exemplary lives, walking the short and straight road. Among these is the **Blessed Angela of Foligno**, who, although a woman (and therefore of the <term>weaker sex</term> <note>A common historical reference to the perceived fragility of women; the author uses this trope to emphasize that Angela’s spiritual power was a miraculous gift from God rather than a result of her own physical or social status.</note>), nevertheless overcame all the strong and powerful of her time through her humble, patient, and steadfast rejection of worldly things and her chosen, beloved poverty.

To anyone who truly reads and wisely considers them, her conversion, her <term>penitence</term> <note>Acts of self-discipline or religious devotion performed to show sorrow for sins.</note>, her temptations, and her doctrine (as set forth in this book) will be of great benefit for walking in the way of God’s service until reaching the joy of heaven. 

This book has already been printed in Latin, divided into three <term>treatises</term> <note>Formal written works that systematically examine a subject; in this case, the stages of Angela's spiritual life.</note>: on penitence, on visions, and on doctrine. However, because the Latin was neither elegant nor scholarly, it was ignored by scholars and remained incomprehensible to <term>the simple</term> <note>A term used historically to refer to ordinary people or those without a formal education.</note>. For this reason, it was decided to translate it into the <term>vulgar tongue</term> <note>The "vernacular" or common spoken language (Italian) of the people, as opposed to the formal Latin used by the Church and academics.</note>, so that it might be understood by everyone and benefit a greater number of people. The writer has not tried to use fancy language, nor the <term>Tuscan</term> <note>The dialect of the Tuscany region, which was the prestigious literary standard of the time (the language of Dante and Petrarch).</note> or courtly style, but only to make the meaning clear. Therefore, everyone is encouraged to read it solely for their own spiritual profit and...

<summary>The translator explains that God chose the "weak" Angela of Foligno to shame the powerful through her devotion and poverty. He justifies translating her works from unpolished Latin into common Italian so that her teachings on penitence and visions can be understood by ordinary people, not just scholars.</summary>

# PREFACE

...for the good of his soul, which he will the more obtain the more he doth carefully read and digest, and put into practice that which he hath read and digested. For it is not the readers, but the doers, of good works who attain unto grace. And although it is to be believed that the writers of that time put down everything in the order in which it pleased God to recall it unto the memory of the **Blessed Angela**, yet hath it seemed more convenient in this translation into the <term>vulgar tongue</term> <note>The "vulgar tongue" refers to the common language spoken by ordinary people—in this context, likely Italian or English—rather than Latin, which was reserved for scholars and the clergy.</note> to put the treatise of doctrine into the second place, the which in Latin is given the third place, and to put here as third treatise that of the visions and consolations, which in Latin hath the second place. 

This hath been done because these consolations and visions are things most high, and it hath seemed right to leave them unto the last to be read by those who are more <term>perfected</term> <note>In mystical tradition, the "perfected" are those who have reached an advanced stage of spiritual maturity and union with God.</note> and instructed, and first to set down her teaching (which is likewise that of Jesus Christ), as being more universally profitable, and especially unto beginners. 

The treatise of doctrine is further divided into several chapters, which was not done previously, in order that it may be more easy and less wearisome unto the reader, because certain of the chapters were exceeding long. Each one is now prayed and exhorted that (for his own good) he weary not of reading this most excellent book, wherein he will find pointed out that <term>straight highroad</term> <note>A metaphor for the direct path to spiritual salvation, often contrasted with the "broad way" of worldly distractions.</note> (the road of **poverty**, of **pain**, and of **contempt**), whereby it is easy to find God, and from which none can excuse themselves as they might do...

<summary>The translator explains the reorganization of Angela of Foligno's writings, prioritizing her practical teachings (doctrine) over her mystical visions to make the work more accessible and useful for beginners. The text emphasizes that spiritual growth comes from acting on these teachings rather than just reading them.</summary>

# THE BLESSED ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

## TREATISE I

->OF THE CONVERSION AND PENITENCE OF THE<-
->BLESSED ANGELA OF FOLIGNO AND OF HER<-
->MANY AND DIVERS TEMPTATIONS<-

**A**S I walked (said the Blessed Angela <note>Angela of Foligno (1248–1309) was an Italian Christian mystic and tertiary of the Franciscan Order.</note>) by the way of penitence <term>penitence</term> <note>The state of being sorry for one’s sins and the act of performing spiritual exercises to make amends.</note>, I did take eighteen spiritual steps before I came to know the imperfection of my life.

The first step was that I did begin to reflect upon my sins, the knowledge of which did fill my soul with so great a dread that, fearing to be condemned unto hell, I wept bitterly.

The second was, that I did begin to be so exceeding ashamed of those my sins that for shame I could not fully confess them; wherefore many times did I communicate <note>To "communicate" means to receive the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> whilst yet unconfessed and with all my sins did I receive the Body of our Lord. Being day and night reproached by my conscience because of this thing, I did pray the Blessed Francis <note>St. Francis of Assisi, the founder of the Franciscan Order, to whom Angela was deeply devoted.</note> that he would grant me to discover a confessor <term>confessor</term> <note>A priest authorized to hear confessions and provide spiritual guidance in the Sacrament of Penance.</note> meet for my needs, who should be well acquainted <margin>with my soul</margin>

with my sins and unto whom I could fully confess myself. In that same night did the Blessed Francis <note>Saint Francis of Assisi (1181–1226), the founder of the Franciscan Order. Angela was a member of the Third Order of Saint Francis, intended for laypeople.</note> appear unto me and say: “Sister, if thou hadst prayed unto me sooner thy prayer would have been sooner granted; that which thou hath asked hath been done.” Upon the next morning, therefore, I went into the church of Saint Francis and found there a friar <term>friar</term> <note>A member of a mendicant (begging) religious order. Unlike monks, friars lived and worked among the people.</note> preaching in Saint Feliciano <note>The Cathedral of San Feliciano in Foligno.</note>, which friar was chaplain <term>chaplain</term> <note>A priest who has a specific role, in this case, serving the Bishop and acting with his delegated authority.</note> unto the bishop and did hold his authority; and to him I did determine to make my confession immediately that the sermon should be ended. Wherefore I did confess myself most fully and was absolved of all my sins. And in this confession I did feel no love, but only bitterness, shame, and pain.

The third step was that I did consequently persevere in the performance of the penance <term>penance</term> <note>Specific acts of prayer or self-denial assigned by a confessor to help a person make amends for their sins.</note> imposed upon me, and as yet I was filled with pain and misery without any other consolation.

Fourthly, I did begin to consider and to know the divine mercy which had granted me the aforesaid grace and saved me from hell. Here did I begin to be enlightened and I did now weep and lament more than heretofore, forcing myself to do more severe penance, of the which I will not speak here.

The fifth was that, being thus enlightened, and finding nothing save faults in me, I did condemn myself, as one who knew and was most assuredly convinced that she was worthy of hell; whereat I did again weep bitterly. It must be understood, however, that between the one step

and another there was a space of time, and I did ever weep and dolefully lament; and there was nothing else vouchsafed <note>To be "vouchsafed" means to be graciously granted or given something by a higher power.</note> unto me, only I did have some consolation in that I could weep, but truly it was a bitter consolation.

Sixthly, there was given unto me a constant and certain illumination <term>illumination</term> <note>A moment of divine spiritual insight or "awakening" where a truth becomes suddenly clear to the believer.</note> of grace, whereby I was made to know my sins so profoundly that, having offended the Creator, I saw that I had likewise offended the creatures <note>In medieval theology, "creatures" refers to all created things—not just animals, but other people, plants, and the physical world itself. Angela feels that by sinning, she has betrayed the whole of God's creation.</note> made for me. Therefore I did recall unto my memory all the sins which I had committed and in the confession (which I did make unto the Lord my God) I did most profoundly ponder over them. Wherefore I did beseech all the saints, with the Blessed Virgin <note>The Virgin Mary.</note>, that they would intercede <term>intercede</term> <note>To pray or plead to God on behalf of someone else.</note> for me, and I did pray the merciful Lord (who had vouchsafed unto me so many good things), that He would have pity upon me, and, seeing that I did know myself to be dead in sin <note>A reference to the state of being spiritually "dead" or separated from God due to serious sin, a common theme in the writings of Saint Paul.</note>, that He would raise me to life again through His grace. Moreover, I did pray all creatures (seeing how that I had offended them inasmuch as I had offended the Creator), that they would not accuse me before God. Thus did it appear unto me that all creatures and all the saints did have compassion upon me, wherefore with a greater fire of love did I apply myself to praying unto God more than was customary.

The eighth was, that as I did gaze upon the Cross I was granted a greater understanding how that Christ had died for our sins. Whereupon I did so clearly perceive all mine own sins, and with such exceeding great grief, that I did feel that I myself had crucified the Lord. Nevertheless I did not yet know how great a blessing was the **Passion of Christ** <term>Passion of Christ</term> <note>From the Latin word "passio" meaning suffering; this refers to the period of intense spiritual and physical suffering of Jesus Christ leading up to and during his death on the cross.</note>, nor did I understand as clearly then as I afterwards did, how that He had redeemed me from my sins and converted me unto repentance, and had died for me. In this beholding of the Cross I did so burn with the fire of love and remorse that, standing before the Cross, I did divest <note>To "divest" oneself usually means to strip away clothing or possessions; here, Angela uses it to describe a total surrender of her worldly identity and belongings to God.</note> myself of everything and did thus offer myself unto Him. And although I feared greatly, I did nevertheless promise to observe **perpetual chastity** <note>A lifelong vow to remain sexually continent and devoted only to God.</note> and not to offend with any of my members <note>An older term for body parts or limbs.</note>, accusing my members one by one of past sins. And I did pray Him that He would make me to keep this my promise, that is, to preserve chastity and to keep guard over my thoughts; for upon the one hand I did greatly fear to promise, and upon the other hand the aforesaid fire compelled me, and I had no power to resist.

Ninthly, there was given unto me the desire to seek out and know the **way of the Cross** <note>A traditional Catholic devotion that commemorates the path Jesus walked to his crucifixion; for Angela, it represents a spiritual journey of shared suffering.</note>, that I might stand at its foot and find refuge there where all sinners find refuge. Unto which end I was enlightened and instructed after this manner: that if I did desire to find the way and come

### OF FOLIGNO 5

to the Cross, I had to first forgive everyone who had wronged me. Then, I had to rid myself of all worldly things—not only from my emotions but also in my actual life. I had to leave behind all men and women, friends and family, and everything else; but most of all, I had to give up my possessions and even my own self. I had to give my heart to Christ (who has done such great things for me), choosing to walk the thorny path <note>The "thorny path" is a traditional metaphor for a life of penance and hardship, modeled after the suffering of Jesus.</note>, which is the path of struggle.

So then, I began to set aside my best clothes and garments and the most delicate food, as well as my expensive head coverings. But at that time, it was still a shameful and difficult thing for me to do, because I did not yet feel a great love for God and I was still living with my husband. Because of this, it was a bitter experience for me whenever I was insulted or wronged, though I bore it as patiently as I could. 

During that time, by God's will, my mother died—she had been a great obstacle <note>Angela’s mother is often depicted in her writings as someone who encouraged her earlier worldly and social interests, which Angela later viewed as sinful distractions.</note> to me in following God's way. My husband died as well, and in a short time, all my children also died. Because I had started to follow the path mentioned before and had prayed to God to free me from them <note>This is one of the most famous and difficult passages in Angela’s biography. To modern readers, her "consolation" at their deaths seems harsh, but in the context of medieval asceticism, she viewed the removal of family ties as a divine gift that allowed her to belong solely to God.</note>, I felt great comfort in their deaths, even though I also felt some grief. Therefore, because God had shown me this grace, I imagined that my heart was within the heart of God, and that His will and His heart were within mine.

The tenth step was seeking to know from God what I could do that would be most pleasing to Him. He did...

### THE BLESSED ANGELA

Out of His mercy, He appeared to me many times, both while I was sleeping and while I was awake. Appearing nailed to the Cross, He commanded me to look at His wounds, and in a marvelous way, He made me understand how He had endured all things for me; and this happened many times. When He had shown me, one by one, all the things He had suffered for me, He said: “What can you do for Me that will be enough?” 

Likewise, He appeared to me many times when I was awake, but in a way that was more beautiful than when I was asleep (even though I always felt His great grief and suffering). He showed me the pain in His head and the hair pulled from His eyebrows and His beard <note>The detail of the beard and eyebrows being plucked is a reference to the "Suffering Servant" in the biblical book of Isaiah (50:6), which medieval Christians traditionally identified as a prophecy of Christ's torture.</note>. He counted out all His scourgings to me, showing them to me in the exact places where they had been inflicted, saying to me: “All this was for you.” 

Then, I clearly remembered all my sins, and it seemed to me that I had wounded Him all over again. Because of this, I had cause for great mourning, and I undoubtedly felt much more sorrow than I had before. While showing His <term>Passion</term> <note>Passion: From the Latin 'passio' (suffering); it refers specifically to the physical and emotional suffering of Jesus between the Last Supper and His death on the Cross.</note> to me, He would say: “What can you do for Me that will be enough?” Then I wept and shed such burning tears that they actually burned my skin, so that I had to wash it with cold water to cool it down.

The eleventh step was that, because of my sins, I was moved to perform <term>penance</term> <note>Penance: In this context, it refers to voluntary acts of self-denial or physical hardship intended to show remorse for sins and to grow closer to God.</note> more severely, though it is not appropriate to speak of those specific acts here. As I reflected on this and forced myself to want to do it, it finally seemed to me that I could not do enough.

<summary>Angela experiences vivid, recurring visions of the crucified Christ, who questions if her devotion is enough compared to His suffering. These mystical encounters lead her toward more extreme acts of physical penance and deeper sorrow for her sins.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Christ's Passion, mystical visions, penance, sorrow for sin, medieval mysticism</keywords>

->OF FOLIGNO 7<-

<page-num>7</page-num>

...sufficiently do <term>penance</term> <note>Penance: Acts of self-denial or devotion performed to show sorrow for sins and to reorient one's life toward God.</note> as long as I possessed worldly things. Therefore, in order to do this more freely and to come to the Cross as I had been inspired to do, I determined most certainly to forsake everything. This determination was miraculously sent to me by God in this way: 

In my imagination, I cherished a great desire to become poor, and such was my zeal that I often feared I would die before I could attain this state of poverty. On the other hand, I was assailed by temptations, which whispered to my thoughts that I was still young, and that begging for alms might lead me into great danger and shame; and that if I did this, I would be forced to die of hunger, cold, and nakedness. Moreover, all my friends tried to dissuade me from this path. 

But at last, the Divine mercy sent a certain great illumination into my heart, from which I derived a deep assurance. This assurance, as I believed then and believe now, I shall not lose even in eternity. Therefore, I settled and determined that—even if I were forced to die of hunger, cold, and shame, because such a thing was pleasing or might be pleasing to God—I would by no means abandon my purpose. Even if I were certain that those evils would befall me, I chose to die willingly for the love of God rather than fall short of my intention. So then, I resolved in good earnest.

The twelfth step was that I afterwards prayed to the <term>Blessed Mother of Christ</term> <note>The Virgin Mary.</note> and the Blessed John the... <note>Likely referring to St. John the Evangelist, who in medieval tradition was often paired with Mary at the foot of the Cross.</note>

<summary>Angela describes her intense struggle to embrace voluntary poverty, overcoming fears of social shame and physical suffering through a divine "illumination" that confirmed her path. This marks her transition from mere regret for sin to a radical commitment to the Franciscan ideal of total renunciation.</summary>

[John the] Evangelist <note>Angela is continuing her prayer from the previous page, where she appealed to the Virgin Mary and St. John the Evangelist.</note> that (for the sake of the suffering which they had endured), they would obtain for me a certain sign whereby I might always keep in memory the **Passion of Christ** <note>The "Passion" refers to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion.</note>.

The thirteenth was, that, persevering in the aforesaid prayers and desires, a dream was granted unto me wherein was showed me the **Heart of Christ**, and it was told me that in that Heart there is no falsehood, because therein all things are true. And it seemed unto me that this sign was sent because I had mocked at a certain preacher.

The fourteenth was, that being once at prayer, Christ did show Himself unto me as I kept <term>vigil</term> <note>A period of purposeful sleeplessness, usually spent in prayer or devotion during the night.</note>, more clearly and giving me greater knowledge of Himself than heretofore. Then it was that He did set me free, and it was after this manner. Firstly, He did question me; then He did say unto me, “Put thy mouth into the wound in My side.” <note>This visceral image is a common theme in medieval mysticism, representing a deep, physical union with the divine through Christ’s suffering.</note> Then methought that I did put it there and did drink the blood which was running freshly from out of His side, and in the doing of this it was given me to know that I was cleansed. And here I did begin to receive great <term>consolation</term> <note>In a religious context, consolation refers to a sense of divine comfort, peace, or the felt presence of God’s grace.</note>, albeit I grieved when I did meditate upon the Passion. Then I did pray the Lord that He would cause my blood to be shed and poured out for His love’s sake, as His had been shed for me, and I did desire that for His love all my members should suffer affliction and death, more vile and more bitter than His Passion. Wherefore I <term>bethought me</term> <note>Considered or reflected.</note> and did seek to find one who should put me to death, in order that I might suffer for the sake of His faith.

[consolation] and love. <note>The text continues from the previous page where Angela began to receive divine comfort following her mystical experience of Christ's wound.</note> But I knew that I was not worthy to die as the holy martyrs had died. Nevertheless, I did desire that He would cause me to die, and by a death more **vile** and more slow, and more bitter. I could not bethink me of a death as vile as I did wish for, or one that would differ from the deaths of the saints—for I did most surely deem myself unworthy of dying their death. <note>This highlights a common theme in medieval mysticism: "holy self-contempt." Angela feels so sinful that she believes the "glory" of traditional martyrdom is too good for her.</note>

The **fifteenth step** was that I did fix my mind upon the **Virgin Mother of God** and upon **Saint John**, keeping them in my remembrance and praying them that (for the suffering which they had endured because of the **Passion of our Lord**) they would obtain for me the grace that I should always feel the pain of the Passion or at least of their sufferings. <note>The "Passion" refers to the physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion.</note> And they did obtain this grace for me in such a degree that one time Saint John did put upon me so great a pain that never did I feel a greater. Then did I perceive that the sufferings of Saint John and of the Mother of Christ (the which they did endure because of the Passion) were in very truth more than <term>martyrdom</term>. <note>Martyrdom typically refers to physical death for the faith; here, Angela argues that the emotional "compassion" (suffering-with) of Mary and John was even more agonizing than physical death.</note> 

Then was it that the desire was given unto me to revile myself with all my might. And although I was much assailed by the devil and ofttimes tempted that I should not do this thing, and was forbidden by the <term>Brothers Minor</term> <note>The "Brothers Minor" or Order of Friars Minor are the Franciscans. Angela was a member of their Third Order (lay people living by Franciscan values).</note> and by all from whom it was meet I should seek counsel, nevertheless by nothing either of good or of evil that could be done unto me was I prevented from giving all my possessions unto the poor. And if I had been hindered from doing this I would at least have forsaken

<summary>In her fifteenth step of conversion, Angela describes a mystical experience of the intense grief shared by Mary and St. John at the Cross, leading her to seek a life of self-reproach. She also recounts her determination to embrace radical poverty by giving away her possessions, despite resistance from her Franciscan advisors.</summary>

[To] me had been granted the hope that I might thereby be redeemed. <note>original: "vouchsafed." This refers to the hope of salvation granted through her previous mystical experiences.</note> I did now, moreover, begin to find consolation in dreams, for I did have fair and pleasant dreams, so that I was thereby consoled. I did likewise now begin to feel the sweetness and consolation of God within mine heart, and outwardly in my body continually, both sleeping and waking; but because I did not yet feel any assurance, my joy was mingled with bitterness, neither did my heart take any rest, but ever desired further mercies from God.

The **eighteenth step** was, that I did at last begin to have the understanding and the visions and the words of God, and I so greatly delighted in prayer that I did forget to eat. Wherefore did I wish that there were no need for eating, in order that I might be ever at prayer. This desire did occasion a certain temptation not to eat, or, if I did eat, that I should eat but a small quantity. <note>Angela describes a common phenomenon in medieval mysticism: "holy anorexia," where the spiritual "food" of prayer and the Eucharist replaces the desire for physical sustenance.</note> But I perceived this to be a snare; and there was such a great fire of love in mine heart that I did never weary of being upon my knees, or of doing other penance. After this I was filled with a yet greater fire and fervour of Divine love, in such a degree that if I did hear any man speaking of God I did cry aloud, and even had there been one with an axe ready to kill me I could not have refrained. <note>These "cries" are known in mystical tradition as "jubilus"—uncontrollable vocal expressions of overwhelming spiritual joy.</note> The first time that this happened unto me was when I did sell the little piece of land which was mine to give the money unto the poor (this was the best possession which I had), but in the beginning I did mock at myself for this crying, as one mocketh <note>The text cuts off as Angela begins to describe her initial self-consciousness regarding her ecstatic outbursts.</note>

...at Petruccio's house <note>Petruccio was likely a well-known figure in Foligno, though his specific identity is lost to history.</note>; then I realized that I could not possibly do otherwise. Because of this, it often happened that when I heard someone speaking about God, I would cry out loudly, even if I was in the company of other people, regardless of who they were. When people told me I was behaving in a disordered way for doing such a thing, I would answer that I was sick and overwhelmed, and that I simply could not help it. I was unable to convince those who spoke poorly of me because of this, and I felt a great deal of shame. Whenever I saw a painting of the Passion of Christ <note>The "Passion" refers to the final period of Jesus's life, including his suffering and crucifixion.</note>, I could barely contain myself; I would be seized by a fever and become physically ill. Because of this, my companion would hide such images of the Passion from me whenever possible so that I would not see them. During these episodes of crying, I received many spiritual insights <term>illuminations: moments of divine intellectual clarity</term>, understandings, visions, and comforts, several of which will be described in the following chapters.

## CONCERNING HER MANY AND VARIED TEMPTATIONS

**In** order, therefore, that I might not feel myself prideful because of the scale and number of these revelations, visions,

and conversations with God. To prevent me from becoming proud <note>original: "puffed up"</note> because of the joy I found in them, the great tempter <note>A reference to Satan or a demonic force permitted to test a saint's humility.</note> was sent to me. He tormented me with many different temptations, so that I was afflicted in both my soul and my body. 

The torments of my body were truly countless and were carried out by many demons in various ways; I hardly believe that all the sufferings and illnesses of my body could ever be written down. There was not one of my members <term>members: the individual parts or limbs of the body</term> that was not painfully tormented. I was never without pain, illness, or exhaustion. I was always weak and frail, and so full of pain that I was forced to stay in bed almost constantly. 

All my limbs felt as if they had been beaten, and the demons afflicted me with many troubles. I was constantly sick and swollen <note>This may refer to edema or inflammation, which Angela interprets here as a demonic affliction.</note>, and I felt pain in all my limbs, making it very difficult for me to move. Even so, I did not grow tired of lying still, though I was unable to eat enough. In short, while my physical sufferings were great, the sufferings of my soul were incomparably more bitter and numerous—and all were caused by the same demons. 

I can only compare myself to a person who is hanged by the neck, their hands tied behind their back and their eyes blindfolded, left hanging by a rope on the gallows. Even though such a person has no help, remedy, or support, they still continue to live in that agony and cannot die. I state firmly that I was tormented by demons even more desperately and with greater cruelty, for they "hanged" my soul...

When my soul saw itself cast down and all its virtue gone, it made a great lamentation, and then I cried out to my God.

After this, I endured another torment, for every vice <term>vice: a habitual sin or moral failing</term> was re-awakened within me. This does not mean—even though they were re-awakened—that they had the power to overcome my reason, but they did cause me much tribulation. Not only did I remember those vices that had troubled me in the past, but many others that I had never known before entered into my body; they inflamed me and caused me the utmost suffering. At times, the demons threw me into a most horrible darkness of spirit, in which it appeared that all hope of good was withdrawn from me. Then those vices which had been dead deep within my soul were revived on the surface—

->“Lord, if Thou wilt send me into hell, I pray Thee tarry not, but do it instantly, and since Thou hast abandoned me, make an end of it now and plunge me into the depths.”<-

Presently I perceived that this was the work of demons and that such vices <term>vices: in this context, sinful impulses or intrusive thoughts that feel external to the person's character</term> exist not in the soul, for never would I have consented thereto. Howsoever, the body doth suffer violence, and so great is the grief and pain that if it should endure the body would not be able to bear it. Moreover, the soul doth find that all its strength hath been taken from it, and albeit it doth in no wise consent unto vice, yet can it not resist. And seeing that it doth act contrary to the will of God, it loseth all hope of being able to resist and is tormented by those vices.

Among others, God did permit one vice to enter into me the which I had never known before, but I did clearly perceive that it entered into me by Divine permission, and it was so great that it did exceed all the others. Upon the other hand was there given unto me a certain virtue,

For virtue did increase and vice did diminish, and I was so upheld by that virtue that I could not consent unto wrong-doing, and likewise by means of that virtue was I so enlightened and strengthened that not all the men who were in the world, nor all the demons, could have persuaded me to commit the smallest sin. Hence proceedeth the aforesaid faith in God. 

The aforesaid vice was so great that I am ashamed to speak of it, and of such potency that if the virtue had tarried <note>original: "tardasset" — delayed or stayed behind.</note> in coming to succour me, neither shame nor suffering nor any other thing whatsoever would have sufficed to restrain me from instantly falling back into sin. ...that I was worthy of the nethermost depths of hell. It must be understood that this humility <note>In this context, Angela describes a "false" humility or "dejection" that brings despair rather than the peace usually associated with spiritual growth.</note> brought no contentment to my soul, nor any understanding of divine truth and goodness. Instead, it was a certain humble dejection which was the cause of countless evils. I perceived a backsliding of both soul and body, because of which I knew in my soul that I was surrounded by demons. During this time, God was hidden from me in all His power and grace; I could not by any means recall Him to my mind, because He would not permit it. 

Thus I perceived myself to be condemned. Yet, I could not believe that I had been the cause of my own damnation, because I grieved and lamented more for having offended my <term>Creator</term> <note>The Latin "Creator" emphasizes God as the source of life, making the offense feel like a betrayal of her very existence.</note> than for any other thing. For this reason, I struggled with all my strength against the demons to overcome the aforementioned vices, but I was not able. I could find no remedy or any way to escape or help myself, so deep did I feel myself to have fallen. 

Because of this, I was often plunged into the <term>abyss</term> <note>The "abyss" (Latin: abyssum) represents a state of total spiritual emptiness or a bottomless pit of despair.</note> of this humility, where I saw my sins and the overwhelming weight of my iniquities. However, I saw no way for these sins to be revealed and made known to everyone. > “This is that woman, full of evil and hypocrisy, a slave to all vices and iniquities, who performed good deeds only so that she might obtain...”

I did diligently make an outward show of being poor, but I caused many sheets and coverings to be put there where I lay down to sleep, causing them to be taken up in the morning in order that none might see them. <note>Angela is describing her struggle with "conspicuous poverty." ...I had fallen into a state of <term>despair</term> <note>In medieval theology, despair (*desperatio*) was considered a grave sin because it meant a total loss of hope in God’s mercy.</note> such as had never before been seen in this world. The cause of it was that I despaired of God and all His **divine gifts** <note>original: *beneficia*. For this reason, I was also certain that in the whole world there was no person more full of every kind of wickedness or more worthy of condemnation than I was. 

Furthermore, everything God had given me had been granted only to increase my despair and my condemnation. Therefore, pray to God that He waits no longer, for my head is already breaking, my body is failing, and my eyes are dimmed because of the flood of my tears. Finally, all my limbs are weakened and can no longer bear witness to the wickedness of my soul, but I rejoice that it has been partially revealed.” All this I perceived while in my state of humility. 

You must know, however, you who are writing down these matters, that what you have written is but a small thing in comparison to all my iniquities and my abuses, for I was still very young when I began to do wrong. I was compelled to say these and other similar things when I was submerged in the aforementioned state of humility.

After this came **pride**. Then I was filled with wrath and vanity, with melancholy and bitterness, and I was entirely puffed up with pride. ...dolorous grief <note>A deep, sorrowful pain often associated with intense spiritual suffering or mourning.</note>, marvelling that there had been any virtue whatsoever in me and doubting whether in truth there had ever been any. Neither did I perceive any reason wherefore God should have permitted this, and for this cause was all goodness shut away from me and hidden. The temptation of this thought did make me to be filled with pride and anger, most bitter sadness and affliction and a grief greater than I can declare, so that if all the wise men of the world and all the saints of paradise had given me every assurance to comfort me and had promised me every blessing which could be named, not even they could have done aught for me or rendered me any help, if God had not changed my soul and worked differently within it. 

Neither should I have believed in them, but all would have worked together to increase mine anger, affliction, sadness, and pain more than I could possibly declare. Wherefore, if God would but have liberated me from these torments and temptations, in lieu thereof would I willingly have suffered every ill and would have borne all the infirmities and suffering which have ever been known, and verily do I believe that they would have been less hard for me to bear than were the aforesaid torments. 

Wherefore have I ofttimes said that, if only I might be set free from them, I would gladly have endured every form of **martyrdom** <term>martyrdom</term> <note>In the medieval world, martyrdom was viewed as the ultimate physical sacrifice for faith.
