## OF FOLIGNO

I am not even yet entirely freed <note>Angela refers here to the lingering effects of the spiritual crises and temptations described on the previous page.</note>, although I do now feel it only seldom, and that only outwardly, not inwardly as I did before. But when I am in that state, I perceive that between that "evil humility" <note>A state of despair or self-destructive shame that mimics humility but actually leads to prideful anger or distance from God.</note> and that pride, there is a great purging and purifying of the soul. It is by and through this process that true humility—without which no one can be saved—is acquired; so that the greater the humility, the greater is likewise the purification. 

Thus I came to know that my soul must be burned and **martyred** <term>martyred</term> <note>In this context, Angela describes a "spiritual martyrdom," where the internal suffering of the soul is as intense as the physical torture of the early Christian martyrs.</note> between those two aforesaid extremes. Through the knowledge of my offenses and my sins (which knowledge the soul obtained through that same true humility), my soul became purged of both pride and demons. For this reason, it follows that the poorer the soul is made and the more profoundly humiliated, the more it lowers and purifies itself in order that it may be cleansed. In no other way can a soul be cleansed except by deep humiliation and by being most profoundly implanted and rooted in veritable and true humility.

->*Here ends the Conversion, Penitence, and Temptation*<-
->*of the Blessed Angela of Foligno.*<-

# TREATISE II
### THE GOSPEL DOCTRINE <note>original: "Doctrina Evangelica." In this medieval context, "Evangelical" or "Gospel" doctrine refers to teachings rooted in the life and words of Jesus as recorded in the Gospels, often interpreted through mystical experience.</note> SET FORTH BY THE BLESSED ANGELA
## CHAPTER I
#### HOW IT MAY BE KNOWN THAT GOD HAS ENTERED INTO THE SOUL

It must be known that God comes sometimes into the soul when it has neither called, nor prayed to, nor summoned Him. And He instills into the soul a fire, a love, and a sweetness that is not customary <note>This refers to a supernatural or extraordinary sensation that transcends ordinary human emotion.</note>, in which the soul greatly delights and rejoices; and it believes that this has been wrought by God Himself being present there, but this is not certain. 

Soon after, the soul perceives that God is inwardly within itself, because—although it cannot behold Him within—it nevertheless perceives that His **grace** <term>grace</term> <note>In the context of Angela's mysticism, "grace" is the divine influence that acts upon the soul to sanctify it and bring it into union with the Divine.</note> is present with it, in which it greatly delights. Yet even this is not certain. 

Presently it further perceives that God comes to it with most sweet words, in which it delights even more, and with much rejoicing it feels God within it; yet some doubts <note>The text concludes mid-sentence here, suggesting that the following page will address the persistent nature of spiritual doubt even during divine encounters.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Angela's teachings on how to recognize the divine presence, describing a progression of feelings from "customary" sweetness to the perception of indwelling grace and divine speech.</summary>
<keywords>divine presence, indwelling, grace, mystical experience, Evangelical doctrine, spiritual fire, Blessed Angela of Foligno</keywords>

<page-num>25</page-num>

...still remain, albeit but few. For the soul possesseth as yet no perfect certainty, neither is it assured that God is truly within it, because such converse and such feelings can be produced likewise by **other spirits** <note>In mystical theology, "discernment of spirits" is the practice of determining if an interior voice or feeling is from God, an angel, the human imagination, or a demonic source.</note>. Wherefore is it still in doubt. And it seemeth unto me that this cometh either of its own malice and sinfulness, or else truly by the will of God, who desireth not that the soul should feel certain and secure. 

But when the soul doth feel the presence of God more deeply than is customary, then doth it certify unto itself that He is within it. It doth feel it, I say, with an understanding so marvellous and so profound, and with such great love and **divine fire** <term>divine fire</term> <note>A frequent metaphor in mystical literature representing the intense, purifying warmth of God's love that consumes the soul's worldly desires.</note>, that it loseth all love for itself and for the body, and it speaketh and knoweth and understandeth those things of the which it hath never heard from any mortal whatsoever. And it understandeth with great illumination, and with much difficulty doth it hold its peace; and if it doth hold its peace, it holdeth it out of the abundance of its zeal, that it may not be displeasing unto God its **Lover** <note>Angela often uses "nuptial imagery," depicting the relationship between the soul and God as a marriage or a romance between lovers.</note> nor cause offence, and likewise by reason of its humility; for it desireth not to speak of things so exceeding high that it may not draw attention unto itself. 

Thus hath it happened divers times unto me, that, out of my burning desire to work the salvation of my neighbour, I did speak things for the which I was reproved, and it was said unto me, "Sister, turn thee again unto the **Holy Scriptures** <note>This passage reflects the historical tension between personal mystical experience and the official doctrine of the Church. Angela’s contemporaries—likely her Franciscan confessors—warned her when her revelations seemed to stray from or exceed the written Word.</note>, for they say not thus, and therefore do we not understand thee." But with that feeling whereby it is certified unto...

To the soul in which God dwells, there is given a **disposition** <note>In this context, "disposition" refers to an interior state or a holy temperament where the soul’s inclinations are perfectly aligned with God’s will.</note> so perfect that it most entirely and truly agrees with the soul in all things. In every way, all the members of the body agree with the soul and truly form **one cause** <note>This implies that the body and soul no longer struggle against one another (the "flesh vs. spirit" conflict), but instead act as a single unit dedicated to God.</note> together with it. They no longer rebel against the soul's will, but perfectly desire those things which are of God—things which, previously, they had not desired in any way. 

This disposition is granted to the soul through **grace** <term>grace</term> <note>The free and unmerited favor of God, which mystics believe transforms the human nature to make it capable of divine union.</note>—by which the soul perceives that the Divine Being has entered into it. This grace grants the soul the assurance and the desire for God and for all things belonging to Him, in the same manner of true love with which God has loved us. Thus, the soul feels that God is **mingled** <note>Angela uses the word "mingled" to describe a profound mystical union where the human and divine become intimately intertwined while remaining distinct essences.</note> with it and has entered into companionship with it.

Furthermore, when God comes into the soul, it is sometimes granted the ability to behold Him. The soul beholds Him **devoid of any bodily shape or form** <note>This refers to an "intellectual vision," a high state of prayer where the mystic "sees" God’s essence or presence without a visual image or hallucination.</note>, yet more clearly than one man might behold another. For the eyes of the soul behold a **spiritual presence** and not a bodily one; I am not able to speak of this clearly because words and imagination fail me. 

In very truth, the soul rejoices in that sight with an **ineffable joy** <note>The term "ineffable" means "too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words," a hallmark of mystical experiences.</note> and regards nothing else, because it is this vision which fills it with the most inestimable satisfaction. This searching and beholding—whereby God is seen in such a way that the soul can behold nothing else—is so profound that it grieves me deeply that I cannot manifest any part of it whatsoever.

Moreover, the soul knows in many other ways that God has undoubtedly entered into it, of which ways I will now speak of two. One is a holy <term>unction</term> <note>Literally an anointing with oil; in mystical tradition, this refers to a spiritual smoothing or healing that brings the body and soul into perfect harmony.</note> which so instantly revives the soul, makes meek all the members of the body, and causes them to agree together with the soul, so that they cannot be touched or offended by anything whatsoever—even things which could, in the smallest degree, agitate the soul. Therefore, the soul feels and hears that God speaks within it, and by means of this great and in all ways unspeakable <term>unction</term>, the soul understands with the utmost certainty that God is within it, because no saint nor any angel in <note>original: "Paradiso"</note> Paradise would have the power to grant this. Wherefore I pray God that He will pardon me, for this is not of my own will, and if I were able and if it were pleasing unto God, I would make manifest something of His goodness.

There is neither father, nor mother, nor son, nor any other person whatsoever who can embrace the object of their love with so great a love as that wherewith God embraces the soul. ...ness <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...which He gives unto the soul with such great sweetness."</note> that I think there is no one in the world who can describe it, express it, or even believe it, unless they have experienced it themselves. At that point, the soul has the assurance and certainty that Christ dwells within it; but everything we can say is as nothing compared to what it really is. 

Then the soul no longer sheds tears, whether of joy, sorrow, or any other kind, because when the soul is still weeping, it is in a lower state <note>In mystical theology, "tears" are often seen as a beginning stage of purgation or early devotion; the highest states of union are characterized by a quiet, overwhelming "excess" that transcends physical crying.</note>. Its joy is so great that it fills the entire body; and every injury the soul suffers, whether through actions or words, is seen as nothing and is turned into sweetness.

Because of this change in my body, I was not always able to hide my state from my companion or from the other people I lived with. At times my face was completely radiant and rosy, and my eyes shone like candles; at other times I was as pale as death, depending on how the visions varied. My joy...

They realize that everything they say, or could possibly say, is nothing compared to what He truly is. 

Therefore, if any preacher truly understood divine things (as I have sometimes heard them claim they do), they would be unable to speak of them. They would not dare to say anything at all about God, but would remain silent and speechless. Consider also how many and how great the benefits are that we have already lost—both you and I—because you did not obey me, but went against me. Therefore, in the future, you must be obedient in all ways to the things of God.” And immediately the body submits...

# CHAPTER II

## HOW THE SPIRITUAL MAN IS DECEIVED

**THERE** are various ways in which spiritual persons <note>In this context, "spiritual persons" refers to those dedicated to a life of prayer and asceticism, such as monks, nuns, or lay mystics like Angela.</note> may be deceived. One way, and the primary one, is when the love within the soul is not pure, but is mixed with a love that is personal and selfish—that is to say, with a person's own will. When this happens, they certainly possess some degree of worldly love, and the world flatters and praises them. 

But all worldly flattery and praise given on account of one's <term>devotion</term> <note>From the Latin *devotio*; here meaning a profound internal state of piety and religious practice.</note> is false. The person whom the world singles out and praises may seem to be more fervent in their religious life; their tears may flow more abundantly, and those feelings of sweetness, fear, and trembling—which do...

I have experienced all these things within myself, and I would not be able to distinguish these matters if my own soul had not reached a certain knowledge of the truth. For when love is pure, it considers itself dead to the physical senses in every way; it regards itself as nothing, and it "dies" in God and <term>decays</term> <note>original: *putrefieri*; in mystical theology, this refers to the total dissolution of the self-centered ego, often called "mystical death," so that the soul can be reborn in God.</note>, and it reveres God and humbles itself. It does not remember that it was praised, nor does it remember its own goodness. 

Indeed, the soul finds itself so low and so full of evil that it believes it can only be saved by God alone, and not by any saint. Yet, it often prays for help from the saints more readily than from God, not daring to pray to God Himself because of its unworthiness—even though it begs for help from the <term>Holy Virgin</term> <note>The Virgin Mary.</note> and all the other saints. And when such a person is praised by anyone, they consider that praise to be a mockery and a joke. This love is pure; it comes directly from God and is planted within the soul, revealing both the soul's own flaws and God's goodness. Therefore, the tears shed now and the sweetness enjoyed never lead to bitterness, but instead to assurance and sweetness. Through this love, Christ enters the soul, and the soul...

...knows through this that it can neither be deceived nor deceive itself. There is another way in which God permits spiritual people to be deceived. When a spiritual and devout person feels greatly loved by God, possesses spiritual gifts and the works resulting from them, and speaks openly about them—because they are too certain of themselves and have overstepped the proper bounds—God permits them to be deceived in some way. This is so they may learn to better know both God and themselves.

Another way is when the spiritual man feels God to be very near and is filled with a perfect, pure, and excellent love; when every day he performs good works with all his heart and has determined that he will in no way seek to please the world or gain a reputation for <term>sanctity</term> <note>The state of being holy or recognized as a saintly person.</note>, but in all things to please Christ and submit himself entirely to Him. Nevertheless, the soul must learn to keep what is its own and to give back to God what belongs to God; and if it fails to do this, then God permits deception in order to protect it. Because the soul is precious in His sight, He does not want it to overstep its proper limits. Furthermore, the soul is not satisfied with what has been described until God leads it to the knowledge of Himself and a full understanding of His goodness. In this state, there can be no deception, for the soul is so completely instructed in the truth that it considers itself to be as full as possible. ...it can neither see nor remember anything else, and thereafter it comes immediately to the understanding of His divine goodness. Then it sees both of these things together in a way that cannot be described; but since even this is not enough, God rouses the soul to even greater zeal and allows <term>tribulations</term> <note>Trials, suffering, or hardships intended to test and refine a person's faith.</note>.

But the state of being wherein He does not permit the soul to be deceived is <term>poverty of spirit</term> <note>A central theme in Angela's mysticism: the total internal emptying of oneself, recognizing that no talent, virtue, or possession belongs to the individual, but only to God.</note>. Verily, I have heard poverty of spirit so much commended in the divine words of God—and with such great and good proofs—that it surpasses our understanding in every way. Truly, God spoke to me thus: "If poverty had not been a most noble thing," said God, "I myself would not have assumed it." 

It is certain that pride can only exist in those who possess something, or who believe themselves to have something. For this reason—because they believed they possessed something of their own—there came the pride and the fall of the first man <note>Adam, who in biblical tradition lost paradise through the desire for his own knowledge and status.</note> and the angel <note>Lucifer, the fallen star, whose pride led him to believe he was equal to God.</note>. Neither the angel nor the man possessed anything in themselves or of themselves, for God alone has this power; and humility is found only in those who are poor and who are persuaded that they possess nothing. 

Wherefore is poverty a most excellent thing, and God Himself caused His dearly beloved Son to be more poor than any man ever was before or will be hereafter; and although He is greater than can be declared, yet did God nevertheless make Him as poor as though He were nothing. And to sinners and mortal men in whom was no true <term>light</term> <note>Spiritual clarity or divine wisdom that allows one to see the truth of things.</note> this thing did seem...

...nothing but <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...they possess nothing but..."</note> foolishness, but unto the wise and unto men of understanding it did seem just the contrary. For whosoever hath poverty can never be ruined, neither can he fall through deception, and if any person were truly to perceive the benefits of poverty he would love God, and if he were but to consider the immeasurable value of it he would no longer be able to keep any worldly possessions, and whosoever were to see how greatly God loveth true poverty, would retain nothing for himself. The proof of this is the proof of <term>divine wisdom</term> <note>A spiritual insight granted by God that allows a person to see the truth of their own soul and God’s nature.</note>, which first maketh us to see our faults and believe ourselves poor in all <term>merit</term> <note>The theological concept of "earning" spiritual standing through good deeds; Angela suggests that true wisdom shows us that even our virtues are gifts from God, not our own achievement.</note> and goodness, and thus honestly to esteem ourselves for what we really are. And in consequence we do make ourselves poor and do love that same poverty, and those who love it are enlightened by the <term>gift of grace</term> <note>An unearned favor or assistance from God.</note> (which is given unto those who do perceive the benefits of poverty). Then doth it make us to see the divine goodness in order that we may all love God, and whosoever loveth doth esteem himself as possessing nothing. And as a man loveth so doth he work, and thus doth he lose all confidence in himself and trusteth in God alone; wherefore, because he confideth himself entirely unto God, he is enlightened by God Himself and all doubts fall away from him. Whosoever knoweth this truth cannot be deceived either by demons or by any other thing whatsoever, because in this <term>poverty of spirit</term> <note>A central theme for Angela: the total internal emptying of oneself, recognizing that no talent or virtue belongs to the individual, but only to God.</note> the soul receiveth a clear and perfect comprehension and hath a most enlightened understanding of all the matters of this life, so that it can never be...

## CHAPTER III

->HOW GOD, DWELLING WITHIN THE SOUL, WORKS<-
->UPON THE UNDERSTANDING, THE AFFECTIONS,<-
->AND THE WILL<-

**The** soul, therefore, hears and understands only those matters whose inner meaning it can truly penetrate. When the soul is illuminated by the presence of God and rests in God’s bosom—and God in it—it is lifted above itself. It hears, rejoices, and rests in that divine goodness, which no one can fully describe because it is beyond all intelligence, all forms of speech, and all words. But in this state...

...the soul swims in joy and in knowledge. Being thus enlightened, it understands the meaning of all the difficult and mysterious sayings of Christ. It also understands how and why Christ’s soul experienced suffering without any relief at all. For this reason, my own soul (which was enlightened, as I have said, and completely absorbed in the **Passion of Christ** <term>Passion of Christ</term> <note>The physical, mental, and spiritual suffering of Jesus during the final hours of his life, including the crucifixion.</note>) also experienced such great suffering that there was no relief here, either. 

Therefore, when my soul remembered the suffering of Christ’s soul, it could find no more joy. This is different from when the soul remembers the physical suffering of his body, because in that case, grief is followed by joy. The soul understands these truths because of the spiritual **exaltation** <note>A mystical state where the soul feels lifted out of the body or ordinary reality into a direct experience of God.</note> mentioned before; it understands, furthermore, that the sufferings in Christ’s soul were as sharp while He was still in His mother’s womb as His sufferings later were upon the **Cross**, except that He had not yet physically experienced them <note>A common theme in medieval mysticism was the "pre-passion," the idea that Jesus, having divine knowledge, suffered the anticipation of the Cross throughout his entire life.</note>. Through the judgments and the unspeakable things of God, the soul further understands that it is lifted up to God by His very presence.

God often works within the soul in many marvelous ways that only He can perform. Sometimes the soul is suddenly lifted up to God with such joy that, if it were to last, I believe the body would not be able to endure it, but would lose the use of its limbs and all its senses. God often interacts with the soul in this way, and when the soul desires...

...to hold Him fast, He immediately departs. Nevertheless, a great joy and certainty remain in the soul—indeed, such a profound joy that it has no doubt that God is still present. However, there is nothing I can compare to that experience of seeing and hearing, and I am unable to describe it. 

These revelations, **exaltations** <note>A mystical state where the soul feels lifted out of ordinary reality into a direct experience of God.</note>, and delights do not always happen in the same way. They occur in various ways and are almost always accompanied by something new; yet none of them can be fully described. The revelations and visions differ from one time to another, as do the delight, the joy, and the happiness. I can say no more about this—in truth, if I were to speak of them, because of my own unworthiness, I would only speak poorly, spoiling and misrepresenting them. Indeed, I would be dishonoring them rather than revealing them as they deserve. 

My spirit is blinded, darkened, and lacks truth; therefore, my sons, listen to my words with suspicion, as if they were the words of an unworthy person. Pay close attention to everything, and do not believe anything I say unless it mirrors the teachings of Jesus Christ, works within your heart, and leads you to the **imitation of Him** <term>Imitation of Christ</term> <note>A central practice in medieval Christianity involving the attempt to live according to the virtues and sufferings of Jesus.</note>.

For now, my sons, I take no pleasure in writing. Instead, I continually mourn my sins and the redemption that was achieved through the **Passion of Christ** <term>Passion of Christ</term> <note>The physical and spiritual suffering of Jesus during the final hours of his life.</note> the Immaculate. Yet, I am compelled to write to you because of the letters you send me. Therefore, I now write to you about that which has recently been impressed upon my soul...

upon mine heart. Ye must know, therefore, oh my sons, that there is only **one thing necessary** <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke 10:42: "But one thing is necessary." In this context, it means total devotion to God above all earthly concerns.</note> unto us, which is God—to find God and wholly fix our minds upon Him. This is necessary unto us. 

But in order that our minds may be the better fixed upon God, it is needful that we should cast off all perverse and useless habits, all **superfluous familiarity** <note>Excessive or distracting social bonds that pull the mind away from spiritual reflection.</note> with men and women of whatsoever nature, all **superfluous knowledge** <term>Superfluous knowledge</term> <note>Original Latin: *curiositas*. Then must he instantly plunge into the **abyss of his wretchedness** <note>A common mystical theme involving a deep, humbling meditation on one's own sins and mortal limitations compared to the perfection of God.</note> and bethink him what things he hath done in times past, what he is doing in the present, and what he will do in the future, and how that his fate in the next world will be according unto his **deserts** <note>What one deserves based on their actions; justice.</note>. Then cometh death, which will be unto all eternity. And no day and no night must pass wherein he doth not think upon these things. 

Wherefore must he constantly think and meditate and use all his endeavor to comprehend the **mercy of God**, how that He did most mercifully ordain that Christ Jesus should suffer all this wretchedness with him, and he must take heed that he never forgetteth this **great benefit** <note>Specifically referring to the "Benefit of Christ," the belief that His suffering and death provided the means for human salvation.</note>.

## CHAPTER IV

### HOW OUR PERFECTION CONSISTS IN KNOWING OUR OWN WRETCHEDNESS AND GOD’S MERCY

**Our** perfection certainly consists in knowing God and ourselves; there is nothing in the whole world that I still delight to write or speak of except these two things—namely, the knowledge of God and of ourselves. For this, a man must lie forever within the prison of his own self; <note>The "prison of the self" refers to a state of deep internal reflection and withdrawal from worldly distractions to focus on one's own soul and conscience.</note> and if he obtains no profit from this, he must seek another prison.

Oh my beloved sons, every vision, every revelation, all sweetness and emotion, all knowledge, and all contemplation are worth nothing if a man does not know God and himself; for this reason, I tell you truly that without this knowledge all those other things will profit you nothing whatsoever. Therefore, I marvel that you desire to have letters from me; I do not see how my words can bring you comfort, seeing that I write of nothing else except this knowledge, for I take no more delight in speaking of other things—indeed, I have even imposed silence upon myself concerning any other matters. <note>Angela frequently expressed that after her mystical experiences, ordinary human speech felt inadequate or even burdensome when it did not concern the Divine.</note> I beseech you, therefore, that you pray to God that He will grant this light unto all generations and that it may remain with you forever.

Considering, moreover, how many things God hath done for us (and especially for our redemption), we are required, led, and instructed to reflect upon our condition; that is to say, to reflect that our condition is most noble, being so beloved of the most high God that He was willing to die for our sake, which He would not have done if man had not been a most noble creature and of great worth. <note>This argument—that the high price paid for humanity's soul (Christ’s death) proves the inherent value and "nobility" of the human being—is a hallmark of Franciscan optimism regarding the human person.</note> We are further required by

While He lived—and how in the end He, who was the Creator of heaven and earth, was willing to die a shameful death—when the <term>soul</term> <note>In Angela’s writings, the 'soul' refers to the internal spiritual faculty capable of direct communion with the Divine.</note> perceives and understands this, it is itself overwhelmed with grief. The soul is moved even more deeply when it perceives how, by reason of his sins, miserable man is deprived of all good things and benefits. It sees that man has earned eternal suffering and is despised and derided by that sublime <term>Divinity</term> <note>The essential nature of God; Angela often uses this term to denote the awe-inspiring, infinite power of the Creator.</note>, and by the angels, the demons, and all creatures. In order to raise man up again from this wretched poverty, the most high God, **Christ Jesus**, who is the most rich in all things, made Himself poor for our sake. He, the most blessed and most joyful, made Himself most wretched so that through His infinite suffering He might redeem man and save him from everlasting and unspeakable pain. 

The soul perceives, moreover, how the God of Glory, worthy of praise above all things, made Himself obedient and humble. He was willing to be despised, mocked, and reviled, appearing to be of no account and being treated as such; and He did this so that despised man might thereby be made glorious and honorable. When the soul well considers and more clearly perceives these things, the more it inclines itself toward Him and suffers with His suffering. ...then do not rest, my son, until you have truly filled your heart with one of these two things, which completely fill and satisfy both the heart and the mind. Therefore, my son, hold firmly to me and believe my words regarding what is necessary for someone who wishes to follow the way of God, to draw near to Him, and to enjoy His blessings in this world and the next.

Before all things, it is necessary that he should know God <term>in very truth</term> <note>Refers to experiential or "infused" knowledge of God’s essence, contrasting with mere intellectual study.</note>, and not only outwardly or superficially—as if it were merely through the color of writing, the sound of words, or the likeness of some created thing. This way of knowing Him, according to common speech, is certainly a <term>simple knowledge</term> <note>A superficial or historical understanding of God that does not transform the soul.</note> of God. 

But a person must know Him in truth; he must understand His supreme worthiness, His supreme beauty, sweetness, exaltedness, <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "virtus"; here meaning divine power or inherent strength.</note>, goodness, generosity, mercy, and compassion, and he must understand that God is the supreme good and the highest of all. It is true that a wise person understands these things differently than a simple person; for the wise person truly understands the matter as it is, whereas the simple person understands it only as it appears outwardly. 

It is like a precious stone that has been found, which the wise and the simple value in different ways. The simple man does not know its <term>virtue</term> <note>In medieval lapidaries (books about stones), "virtue" referred to the hidden medicinal or supernatural powers a gemstone was believed to possess.</note> and desires to possess it only for its beauty and its brightness, and for no other reason; but beyond the splendor and the brightness of the precious stone, the wise man knows its power and its worth, and when he

48

...once he has found it, he loves it with the utmost intelligence and fervor. In the same way, the wise soul seeks to know God—not only according to outward appearance or through careless reflection, but using all its effort to know Him <term>in very truth</term> <note>As established on the previous page, this refers to experiential knowledge rather than just intellectual study.</note>, to taste of His <term>supreme goodness</term> <note>original: "Summum Bonum," the theological concept of God as the highest possible good and the source of all other goods.</note>, and to know His worth. For not only is He good, but He is the Supreme Good; and knowing Him, man loves Him in every way for His goodness. In loving Him, the soul seeks to possess Him; and He, who is supremely good, gives Himself to the lover. Then the soul feels Him, tastes of His sweetness, and enjoys that greatest of all delights. 

At that point, the soul participates in that supreme good, which is supreme love; it enters into it with affection, and being enamored of the love of its Beloved, it desires to hold Him fast. Therefore, it embraces Him and presses Him to itself; it unites itself with God and draws Him into itself with the utmost sweetness of love. Then, by the <term>virtue</term> <note>In this context, "virtue" (Latin: virtus) means power, strength, or active efficacy.</note> of love, the lover is transformed into the beloved and the beloved is transformed into the lover. 

It is like <term>hard iron</term> <note>This is a famous metaphor in medieval mysticism (used by figures like Bernard of Clairvaux). It explains "deification"—how a human can become "god-like" through grace without actually becoming a god by nature.</note> which so assumes the color, heat, virtue, and form of the fire that it almost turns into fire itself; so does the soul, united with God through the perfect grace of divine love, itself almost become divine and transformed in God. Nevertheless, it does not change its own substance, but its whole life is transformed into the love of God, and thus it almost becomes divine in itself.

Behold, how greatly it profits us to possess a know—

### CHAPTER VII

#### HOW THE SOUL MAY QUICKLY AND EASILY FIND GOD THROUGH DEVOUT, PURE, CONSTANT, HUMBLE, AND FERVENT PRAYER

**YOU** must know, however, that the soul cannot gain this knowledge through its own power—neither through books, nor academic learning, nor any <term>created thing</term> <note>In medieval theology, "created things" (Latin: *creatura*) refers to anything that is not God, including human intellect, other people, or worldly wisdom.</note>—even though it may use and benefit from them. Instead, this knowledge comes solely through <term>divine grace</term> <note>The supernatural assistance given by God to the soul for its sanctification.</note> and the light it provides. 

For this reason, I believe that the soul can find no faster way to seek and obtain this from the most high God—who is perfect Good, perfect Light, and perfect Love—than through devout, pure, constant, humble, and fervent prayer. This prayer should be offered not with the mouth alone, but with the mind and heart and all the strength of the soul and the feelings of the body, asking and pleading with the most burning desire. # CHAPTER X

## HOW CHRIST REVEALED HIMSELF POOR IN POWER

THE third and supreme degree of poverty was that He did put away from Him His own nature. Firstly, because He made Himself poor and needy, laying aside His own power, He, the <term>Omnipotent</term> <note>All-powerful; having unlimited power.</note>, unto whom <term>naught</term> <note>Nothing.</note> was impossible, desired to appear and to live in the world as a man, weak, <term>infirm</term> <note>Weak or physically frail.</note>, and impotent, in order that beside the human miseries, the helpless childhood and other burdens which He did take upon Himself for our sake, He who was without blame or sin might appear as but a feeble man. Verily He endured much weariness in His journeyings, visitations, and disgrace.

## CHAPTER XI

### HOW CHRIST LAID ASIDE HIS WISDOM AND HIS OWN NATURE

**SECONDLY**, He did lay aside His own nature, making Himself poor in wisdom because He desired to appear as a simple man, one senseless and vain in the sight of men. Herein did He show unto us the way of truth, that is to say, that neither in learning nor in wisdom should we take glory unto ourselves, for being puffed up with this pride

Thirdly, He did lay aside His own nature in that He did make Himself poor of the <term>fame</term> <note>In this context, "fame" refers to one’s public reputation or how one is perceived by society.</note> of His holiness, goodness, and innocence. Which thing is exceeding marvellous, because He did so walk in the way of justice that not only did very few persons esteem Him holy, but He was held to be a sinner and the friend of sinners, a destroyer, seducer, and conspirator against His country, and albeit He was working out our <term>salvation</term> <note>The deliverance from sin and its consequences, believed by Christians to be brought about by Christ.</note> upon earth, He was reputed and condemned as a <term>blasphemer</term> <note>One who speaks sacrilegiously or irreverently about God or sacred things.</note> and numbered amongst thieves and evil-doers. He who was the chief of all saints, and king of all virtues, in whom was united all goodness, He did this thing, and did deign to put away from Him all His fame of holiness (saving only the truth of His teaching, life, and judgment), and to become poor in order that He might lay bare our <term>hypocrisy</term> <note>The practice of claiming to have moral standards or beliefs to which one's own behavior does not conform; pretense.</note>. For we do seek glory in the sight of men ; we do feign to possess those good things which we have not ; we do endeavour to beg for ourselves the fame of holiness and innocence, falsely denying our wickedness, and with lies attributing unto ourselves good works, lawfully and unlawfully, as often as we can.

Fourthly, He did lay aside His own nature in that He did renounce His kingdom, His princely estate and lordship, and all things which He did possess. He, the **King of kings** <note>A title used in the Bible (Revelation 19:16) to emphasize Christ's supreme authority over all earthly rulers.</note> and Lord of lords, whose kingdom is without end, deigned to live amongst men, and be as an humble servant, one dejected, cast out, and sold—and when they did desire to make Him king, He would not, but unto the kings of this world did He remain subject even unto death, obediently paying **tribute** <note>A tax or payment made by a subject to a ruler or state; a reference to Jesus' instruction to "render unto Caesar."</note> unto the courts, judges, and tax-gatherers who did ask it of Him; subjecting Himself not only unto kings, but likewise unto the lowest of their servants, officers, and ministers, ever obedient, even unto scourging and death upon the Cross.

And He, the King of kings, did say before **Pilate** <note>Pontius Pilate, the Roman governor of Judea who oversaw the trial and crucifixion of Jesus.</note> that His kingdom was not of this world. He sought not after the kingdom of this world or the temporal lordship over men, but was ever willing to be in subjection; not to be lord or king or prince, but the most humble servant, casting Himself entirely aside. Moreover, He was subject unto His most poor and humble Mother and His **putative father** <note>Referring to Saint Joseph, who was the legal and "reputed" father of Jesus, though not His biological father.</note>, obedient unto them and humbly serving them until His thirtieth year. He was obedient in the midst of His disciples, who were few in number, ignorant, and poor, and albeit He did choose to be as a king or ruler over them, He said that He was not come to be **ministered unto** <note>To be served or waited upon.</note>, but to minister unto them, even unto the rendering up of His spirit for them and for the other sinners who were to be **redeemed** <note>The act of being saved from sin or error; literally "bought back."</note>. Yea, He, the Head and Master of

Thus will our pride be confounded by the <term>Book of Life</term> <note>original Latin: "liber vitae." And for our safety will we not only endure the state of subjection, but, fleeing from all preferment <note>A position of higher rank, social advancement, or promotion.</note>, we will seek with whole hearts and fervent desire to be in subjection and an humble state.

Such, then, was the supreme, constant, and perfect poverty of Jesus Christ our Savior. Although He was the Lord of all riches, He nevertheless chose to be poor among us so that He might teach us the love of poverty. Truly, He was poor in possessions, in will, and in spirit, beyond human understanding, and all because of the deep love He had for us. He lacked riches and all worldly things; He was poor in friends and power, poor in worldly wisdom, in the reputation for holiness, and in all social honors. And finally, being poor in all things, He preached poverty and said that the poor were blessed and would judge the world <note>A reference to the biblical promise that the humble and the apostles would share in Christ's judgment of humanity.</note>. On the other hand, He condemned the wealthy along with their riches and abundance, saying that they deserved condemnation. He preached this through His deeds, by His own voice, and by His example, with all His might.

# CHAPTER XII
## HOW POVERTY OF SPIRIT IS REJECTED BY MANY

Oh, the shame and the pain of it! In these days, poverty of spirit is driven out and put to flight by almost everyone. And what is worse and even more hateful is that those very people who read about it in the <term>Book of Life</term> <note>original Latin: "liber vitae." Angela uses this term to describe the person of Jesus, whose life is a "text" that believers must study and imitate.</note>, and who understand, preach, and glorify it, still utterly reject it in their deeds, their will, and in all their acts and intentions. They will not have the power to escape, and all the wisdom of the world will be of no use to them. Instead, everything will be taken from them in actual fact, just as they truly desired to keep those things during their lives in defiance of the teaching of Christ. For this reason, they shall live in eternal torments. Amen.

# CHAPTER XIII

## ON THE HUMILITY AND CONTEMPT OF OUR SAVIOR JESUS CHRIST

**THE** second companion who constantly accompanied Jesus, the Son of God, while He lived on this earth was the voluntary and perfect contempt, rejection, disgrace, and shame which He chose to endure continually in this world. For He lived like a <term>base servant</term> <note>A person of the lowest social status, often treated as property and lacking legal rights or honor.</note> who had been sold and never ransomed; indeed, hardly even as a servant, but rather as one who was considered evil and wicked. He knew that He was to be mocked with insults, covered in scorn, ridiculed, bound, and whipped, and that finally—without justice or any defender—He was to be led out with thieves and sinners, condemned and killed alongside them, dying a shameful death. While He lived, He always used His words and actions to rebuke anyone who wanted to give Him worldly honor. He fled from the honors of this world and endured shame, which He accepted willingly, although He never gave any reason or cause for it Himself.

Almost everyone persecuted the Giver of the world; they ridiculed and mocked Him without reason or cause. Even from His cradle and swaddling clothes they began to persecute Him, driving Him away into a foreign country <note>A reference to the Flight into Egypt to escape King Herod.</note>. And when He reached adulthood, they called Him a Samaritan <note>In this historical context, "Samaritan" was used as a derogatory term for a religious outsider or heretic.</note> and an idolater; some even claimed He was possessed by a demon, a glutton, or a <term>seducer</term> <note>original: "seducer"; in this medieval context, the term refers to a deceiver or one who leads others away from the true faith.</note>, 

<summary>This page introduces the "second companion" of Jesus—voluntary humiliation and social rejection—detailing how He chose a life of disgrace and persecution from infancy to His execution.</summary>

This is a manifest sign of little faith, of little judgment, and of great <term>lukewarmness</term> <note>A spiritual state of being indifferent or lacking zeal; neither passionate nor completely cold, but spiritually stagnant.</note>. For either he hath truly committed that for the which he deserveth to be put to confusion, and to suffer pain and shame (from the which few can justly excuse themselves), or else he hath not committed it. If he hath committed any sin, manifest or hidden, and doth verily repent him...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "second companion" of Jesus Christ—His choice to live in shame and contempt. It specifically addresses how a follower should respond to being punished or disgraced, whether they are guilty or innocent of the specific charge.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>

<header>THE BLESSED ANGELA</header>

thereof, he must bear the punishment. And not only with patience shall he bear it, but with joy of soul and body, and this especially for two reasons. Firstly, because that shame, <term>confusion</term> <note>In this context, "confusion" refers to the experience of being publicly disgraced or shamed before others.</note>, and pain which he endureth is pleasing unto God, and unto his neighbour, according unto the ordination of <term>divine justice</term> <note>The idea that God’s law requires a balance or "payment" for wrongs committed; accepting punishment is seen as a way to restore that balance.</note>. And if he hath not committed that evil either in word or in deed, then must he bear and endure all confusion, shame, and pain, if God hath permitted it. And he must bear it with an hundred times greater patience and cheerfulness than he would have done in the first case, because that pain, confusion, and shame do but serve to increase the grace, and the greater the <term>merit</term> <note>A theological term for the "spiritual credit" or value of an action that makes one worthy of God's reward.</note> of the grace, the greater is likewise the gift and reward of glory.

Without any doubt it is by thus enduring shame and confusion (which do come without sin), that the holy souls and the friends of God do grow in grace and are made perfect, in the same manner as poverty and other afflictions sent of God do make perfect those who endure them. For Christ did love shame and refuse honour in order that He might show unto His friends how they could grow in virtue and in grace.

For this reason, therefore, doth this <term>second companion</term> <note>Angela describes the specific hardships of Christ’s life as his "companions." The first companion was poverty; this second companion is the voluntary acceptance of being despised and shamed by the world.</note> ever accompany us through life, and if we would clearly behold the beginning, middle, and ultimate end of the life of Jesus Christ the Son of God, we must be all humility and must live in the world without honour, despised and rejected of the world and the lovers thereof.

## CHAPTER XIV

### OF THE CONSTANT AND MANY SUFFERINGS WHICH
### CHRIST DID BEAR IN VARIOUS WAYS

His <term>third companion</term> <note>The author, Angela of Foligno, identifies three "companions" that accompanied Jesus throughout his life: Poverty, Contempt, and Suffering. This chapter focuses on the third.</note> was the one of which He had the most experience and which endured continually. This was supreme suffering, which immediately afflicted His soul when it was joined to His body. For in that moment when His soul was united with His human body, it was instantly filled with supreme knowledge. Because of this, Christ possessed full understanding even in the womb of His Virgin mother.

He immediately began to feel the greatest possible suffering; He possessed a universal and singular knowledge, foresight, consideration, and understanding of all the pains which His soul and body were eventually to undergo and bear for our sake. As though He were already close to death, He was filled with such agony and sadness that His body gave forth a **bloody sweat** <note>This usually refers to Christ's agony in the Garden of Gethsemane (Luke 22:44), but Angela suggests here that His foresight of the future caused this physical reaction throughout His life.</note> which dropped even upon the earth, foreshadowing the bitterness of death. 

His soul lamented in its great affliction, even though His body did not feel as much physical pain then as it did later when He was closer to His <term>Passion</term> <note>The "Passion" refers to the final period of Jesus's life, including his trial and crucifixion.</note>. That holy soul foresaw the "knives" of those most evil tongues, and the sharp, cutting words of every specific person. He knew and constantly considered by whom, when, how, and to what degree He was to be afflicted, mocked, and put to death.

death and being slain, and for this end He knew that He was born into the world, and therefore could not escape suffering. All this lay before Him and He did meditate thereon, which meditation could doubtless not be without exceeding great sadness and supreme suffering both of heart and mind. Wherefore was the whole life of Jesus Christ filled with the utmost suffering, sadness, and affliction.

A reference to the Epistle to the Philippians 2:8, a central text for medieval meditations on Christ's humility.</note>

Suffering like unto this cannot by any means be explained. Therefore I can only say that the unspeakable suffering which Christ did endure was conceded, permitted, and assigned unto Him by the **ineffable wisdom of the Divinity**, which <term>divine dispensation</term> <note>The "divine dispensation" (Latin: *dispensatio*) refers to the orderly plan or arrangement by which God governs the world and enacts the salvation of humanity.</note>, unspeakable and eternal, unspeakably and eternally one with Christ, did ordain that He should endure this supreme suffering. And the more admirable this divine dispensation, the sharper and more intense were Christ’s sufferings, where-fore hath there never been any mind so great that it was able to understand these sufferings.

This divine dispensation was the true cause and origin of all suffering, wherein it all beginneth and endeth. And just as it is impossible for any mind to comprehend the **infinite charity** <note>In medieval theology, "charity" (*caritas*) is not merely almsgiving, but the highest form of love—the selfless, sacrificial love of God for humanity.</note> of Christ (here made manifest), because, by His own death, He hath redeemed us, so is it impossible to comprehend the infinite suffering which He had to endure and for which He did lament. And this suffering did proceed from the **ineffable light** with which He was filled. It is certain that by this enlightening, Christ, by making Him one with this divine dispensation, and transforming Him with <term>divine illumination</term> <note>Illumination is a mystical state where the soul is granted a direct, supernatural perception of divine truths that surpass human reason.</note> in His sufferings, this divinity and ineffable light did inflict upon Him such pain that no words can suffice to describe it.

| **Ineffable Wisdom** | A wisdom so great it cannot be expressed in human language. |
| **Redemption** | The act of "buying back" humanity from the debt of sin through sacrifice. |

## CHAPTER XV

### OF THE MANY AND VARIOUS CRUELTIES USED TOWARDS CHRIST

**He** who considers the matter well will find that Christ Jesus, the Son of God, was pierced and wounded with four kinds of knives and spears. <note>Angela often uses vivid, violent metaphors—like knives and spears—to describe spiritual and emotional pains, a hallmark of 13th-century Franciscan mysticism.</note>

Firstly, there was the perverse and continuous cruelty inflicted willfully upon Him by obstinate hearts, for they did diligently seek, meditate, and conspire in what manner they could most dishonorably and with the greatest cruelty drive Him from the earth, He and His name and all those whom He was come to save, and all those who followed Him.

Secondly, there was the wickedness, anger, hatred, and malice borne Him by those who crucified Him. As many, therefore, as were the evil thoughts, perverse intentions, and wicked designs conceived against Him, so

Thirdly, there was the malice and falseness of the tongues which spoke against Him; for as many as were the accusations, evil counsels, deridings, mockings, blasphemies, curses <note>original: "maledictions." These are formal or ritualistic invocations of evil or misfortune upon a person.</note>, false witnesses, and false judgments, even so many pains did His soul endure.

Fourthly, there was the most cruel deed of the Passion <term>Passion: derived from the Latin "passio" (suffering), this refers to the short final period in the life of Jesus, including his trial and crucifixion.</term>, most cruelly inflicted upon Him, the which is manifest unto whosoever does meditate upon the whole matter thereof. And certain is it, that as often as He was dragged hither and thither, His hair and beard plucked, as often as He was bound, spit at and scourged, even so many were the different sufferings which He did endure. 

But especially did He suffer from the nails, for they did use nails of the largest kind, rough and square, and therewith did they most cruelly pierce through His hands and feet. <note>Medieval mystics like Angela often emphasized the physical "roughness" of the nails to help the reader visualize the gritty reality of the execution, a practice intended to stir deep emotional empathy.</note> And by thus piercing them quite through and utterly laming them, men did most cruelly fasten them upon the Cross. This kind of nail did cause pain worse than any other; and even had they been simply passed through His hands and feet and not driven into the wood, they would have occasioned the most acute pain. But not content even with spreading out His hands and feet, with stretching His bones and sinews and drawing all the joints of His body asunder, making Him fast unto the hard wood, they did also raise the Cross on high, exposing Him naked in the air and the wind, unto the gaze of all. <note>Public nakedness in the ancient and medieval world was considered a profound form of social shame and "spiritual" wounding, beyond just the physical exposure.</note>

In this way He died in the greatest suffering, and thus the full wickedness of mankind was carried out. In order that this same Jesus—who is both God and Man—might reveal something of this intense suffering (not for His own sake, but for us, just as He was bearing it for us so that we might fully enter into that suffering), He cried out in a loud voice, saying, "My God, my God, why have You forsaken me?" <note>original: "Eli, Eli, lama sabachthani." This "cry of dereliction" from the Cross is recorded in the Gospels of Matthew and Mark. Angela interprets it here as a proof of Christ's human nature.</note> It was not possible for Him to actually be forsaken by God, since He Himself was God; however, by crying out that He was forsaken, He bore witness to His <term>manhood: the human nature of Christ, which experienced physical pain and emotional distress, distinct from His eternal divine nature.</term>

Thus He demonstrated the unspeakable and overwhelming pain which He endured for our sake, and without doubt God shared that pain with the Christ who bore it. Therefore, it was for our sake alone that He cried out, so that He might make it clear to us that He was carrying that supreme suffering for us and not for Himself—a suffering that moved Him to frequently grieve and lament.

But no one should imagine that He only suffered while He was on the Cross. For in proportion as His soul was most holy, most tender and most noble, so were the sufferings wherewith He was tormented most keen and most intense.

The injuries, afflictions, and supreme suffering wherewith this most noble soul was tormented were all laid upon Him by the aforesaid supreme and unspeakable divine dispensation. And so deeply did they torment the soul of Christ, that each particular pain in itself did likewise afflict the body. Moreover, because this <term>virgin body: a reference to Christ being born of the Virgin Mary; such a body was believed to be perfectly formed and therefore more physically sensitive than bodies corrupted by original sin.</term> was more noble and more delicate than any other body born of woman, the pain was more intense, and it was more deeply afflicted by it.

Christ did feel this pain the more acutely because He Himself was the **true God**; <term>true God: a central Christian doctrine stating that Jesus was simultaneously fully divine and fully human. Angela argues that because He was God, His capacity to feel the weight of sin and the pain of injury was not limited like a human's, but was infinite.</term> wherefore every affliction and injury done unto Him was a boundless offense, inasmuch as it was not done unto man alone, but unto the true God. For this reason had He cause for infinite lamentation, inasmuch as He did endlessly grieve over every wickedness and affliction done unto Him.

While He was suffering all these things, the Saviour of the world, Christ Jesus, God and Man, did neither threaten nor curse, neither did He defend or vindicate Himself, nor excuse Himself when He was accused. He hid not His face when it was spat upon; He withdrew not His hands and arms when they were stretched upon the Cross, He sought not to escape death; but wholly and in all ways did He deliver Himself into the hands and unto the will of men, in order that, because of their iniquity, the work of **redemption** <term>redemption: the theological concept of "buying back" humanity from the debt of sin through Christ's sacrifice.</term> might be fulfilled, notwithstanding their opposition and ingratitude.

The most wondrous thought of all is, that in this dreadful deed of the **Passion** <term>Passion: from the Latin 'passio' (suffering); refers to the final period of Christ's life, including His trial and crucifixion.</term> committed against the Innocent One, He did furnish an example of patience; He taught the truth unto those who killed Him, and with cries and tears did pray unto the Father for them. In return for this greatest of sins (for which the whole world and human nature deserved to perish), He did bestow upon them the greatest benefits. This **reconciliation** <term>reconciliation: the restoration of a broken relationship; in this context, the restoration of the bond between humanity and God through Christ's sacrifice.</term> was effected through that self-same deed for the which the whole world and every creature was worthy of **damnation** <term>damnation: the state of being condemned to eternal punishment due to sin.</term> for His sake, because the creature had been found guilty of committing such a great sin against its creator.

Oh, pity! Oh, mercy immense! Oh, incredible benignity which did cause such infinite grace to abound there where only infinite iniquity had hitherto abounded! Verily, this mystery hath no end, but hath been ordained by that infinite Goodness and Mercy in order that we might have an example of endurance in all **tribulations** <term>tribulations: great distresses or sufferings, often viewed in a religious context as trials of faith.</term> and adversity, and to teach us not only that we should never render back evil unto our enemies, but that for love of Him we should do good unto them. ...speak and discourse of how the Son of God spent His whole life in suffering, and how He not only patiently bore the **tribulations** <term>tribulations: from the Latin 'tribulatio,' referring to great distress or emotional and physical suffering, often viewed as a trial of faith.</term> which happened to fall upon Him, but that—having never committed any sin—He actually chose this suffering and took it upon Himself of His own free will. Indeed, He sought out tribulation, and having found it, He loved it and submitted Himself to it, demonstrating His endurance in both words and deeds and preaching that all those who endured similar trials were blessed. Nor did He only praise and glorify these afflictions of body and soul with empty words; He actually bore in His own body and soul a weight of suffering that no one else has ever carried. And He says that through these tribulations He entered into His kingdom and His glory, affirming that it is impossible to attain **eternal grace** <term>eternal grace: the state of divine favor and salvation that grants the believer a share in God’s eternal life.</term> through any other way or means except through suffering, pain, and tribulation.

**VERILY**, this is the true and straight road pointed out by God. A person is exceedingly foolish who doubts this or who does not take it into account—seeing how the Son of God walked... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the thought that if God Himself walked this path, the believer must not hesitate to follow.</note>

upon that road), to follow the Lord, His **ineffable** <term>ineffable: from the Latin 'ineffabilis,' meaning something too great or sacred to be expressed in words.</term> **Guide and Redeemer**. Of a certainty, He knew how much good was to be found in pains and **tribulations** <note>original: "tribulatio." Referring to the distress or suffering that serves as a spiritual trial.</note>. For this reason, He chose to take these woes upon Himself, fleeing from joy, rejecting worldly comforts, and declaring them to be hateful. 

Therefore, it does not seem unreasonable to suggest that before the Son of God took these sufferings upon Himself in His own person, there might have been some excuse for those who followed their own desires and fled from hardship (even though He had already shown the opposite through His saints and prophets long before). But once the Son of God Himself chose to bear such great adversity, a person would be truly miserable and mad to doubt or ignore the truth so clearly established and so loudly preached—a truth of which the Lord Himself has given an example to the world. Certainly, no one would doubt this unless they were void of sense and worthy of all damnation.

Therefore, we wretches are worthy of this damnation. For, being lowly sinners, we not only refuse to take those sufferings upon ourselves as a **penance** <term>penance: an act of devotion or self-sacrifice performed to show sorrow for sins.</term>, but we even resist when God, in His supreme mercy and wisdom, sends them to us in order to save us and **purge** <term>purge: to cleanse or purify the soul, often through the "fire" of suffering or divine discipline.</term> us of evil. We flee from them and reject them with impatience, grumbling and complaining bitterly against them, while eagerly searching for comforts and remedies to be relieved of these trials. Oh, wretched...

...and truly miserable are we, who not only seek to escape those worldly pains and afflictions which are the remedy and cure for sin, but we do even refuse the assistance of our most wise physician **Jesus Christ**. Fleeing from harm and injury, we do refuse as far as we are able all suffering, affliction, or adversity which the Eternal God, our heavenly Physician, hath sent unto us, and nothing will we endure with **patience** or **resignation**.

These should be endured with great patience and willingness, even though their heavenly purpose remains hidden from the sufferer.

by the **supreme Physician** <term>supreme Physician: a traditional title for Jesus Christ, suggesting that He "prescribes" trials and hardships as medicine to heal the soul's pride and attachment to the world.</term>, our Saviour, we should not only bear them with patience, but for His love and our own great good we should accept them very willingly.    Oh, wretched that we are, what more can we say? ## CHAPTER XVII

### OF HOW PLEASING UNTO GOD IS THE SERVICE OF THE POOR, WHO SERVE FOR LOVE’S SAKE WITHOUT LOOKING FOR A REWARD

**IN** the service of God we should not always be thinking of the **spiritual consolations** <term>spiritual consolations: feelings of joy, peace, or "sweetness" during prayer or worship, which mystics warn can sometimes lead to seeking the feeling rather than God Himself.</term> therein to be found. Can it be supposed that when Mary, the mother of Jesus, beheld her beloved Son upon the Cross, crying aloud and dying, that she asked sweetness or consolation of Him in that hour? Assuredly not; she accepted the anguish, bitterness, and pain, and such anguish should be likewise in our souls. It is a sign of little love, yea, of great presumption, if the soul should desire to feel in this world aught of Christ save pain. 

Wherefore doth God doubtless take greater pleasure in the service of the poor <note>In the Franciscan tradition of Angela, "the poor" often refers to those who are "poor in spirit," meaning those who have surrendered their own will and even their desire for spiritual pleasure.</note>, who serve Him faithfully without reward or benefit whatsoever, than in that of the rich, who every day receive great rewards. And those who serve Him for the sake of reaping spiritual benefits, whose souls are **fat** <note>original: "pinguis." In medieval spirituality, a "fat" soul refers to one sated with easy spiritual comforts, often contrasted with the "lean" or "hungry" soul that must persevere through hardship.</note> and full of spiritual sweetness which they derive from the service of God, who run unto Him and serve Him for love’s sake, these have not as great **merit** <term>merit: the spiritual value of an action in the eyes of God; here, Angela argues that suffering makes service more valuable.</term> as have those who run unto God and serve Him likewise for love’s sake, but without any consolation whatsoever, indeed, rather with pain.

Thus, methinketh, doth the **divine light** <term>divine light: a mystical term for direct spiritual understanding or revelation granted by God to the soul.</term> which proceedeth from the life of Christ instruct me concerning the

<summary>Angela of Foligno explains that true devotion is found in serving God through pain and spiritual "dryness" rather than seeking emotional rewards. She uses the Virgin Mary’s suffering at the Crucifixion as the model for selfless love that asks for no consolation.</summary>

He loved us so much that for our sakes—oh, you children of God—He was willing to suffer a most bitter and **ignominious** <term>ignominious: shameful or disgraceful; here referring to the public humiliation of the crucifixion.</term> death. He did this only so that He might redeem us and provide us an example of how we should bear hard things for love of Him. 

Undoubtedly, the perfection and the true sign of **sonship** <term>sonship: the spiritual status of being a child of God, implying both a relationship of love and a duty to follow the Father's example.</term> is to love God and one’s neighbor. And because this holy **Man of Sorrows** <note>A traditional title for Jesus Christ, referencing a prophecy in the Book of Isaiah describing a savior who is "acquainted with grief."</note> loved us so faithfully and purely that He had no pity for Himself, but wholly gave Himself up to suffering for love of us, He desires that His lawful children should do the same, as much as they are able.

This moves the soul to discipline and reform itself, in both its physical senses and its inner spirit.

Life—that is to say, the life and death of Christ crucified, the <term>Man of Sorrows: a title for Jesus from the Book of Isaiah, emphasizing his suffering for humanity</term>. The soul also sees and recognizes the vast number of its sins, and how deeply it offends God in all its physical <note>original: "members," referring to the various parts of the human body</note>. It also sees above it the manifestation of divine mercy—meaning, it perceives how the crucified Christ endured cruel pain in every part of His body because of the sins committed by each of our own body parts. Thus, by means of the Cross, the soul may reflect on how greatly and in what way it has offended God. 

First, the head: a person washes, combs, and scents their head, doing various other things to make themselves look pleasing to others. Then the soul realizes that because of this sin <note>referring to the sin of vanity and pride in one's appearance</note>, Christ performed <term>penance: an act of devotion performed to show sorrow for sin</term> in His own head and suffered terrible pain. To make <term>reparation: the act of making amends for a wrong done</term> for how humans wash, comb, scent, and style their hair, Christ’s most holy head was stripped of its hair, pierced by the crown of thorns, beaten with rods, and covered in His own blood. 

In the same way, the soul looks at the other parts of the body. (In certain visions and sayings, Christ Himself—as if unintentionally—almost murmurs and laments against us, counting every part of His body). The soul sees not only the many sins committed by each body part, but also the extreme seriousness of those sins.

Truly, when the soul reflects upon the Cross, the infinite weight <note>original: "grievousness"</note> of sin is made clearly visible. Furthermore, the soul will read in this Book <note>Angela often refers to the Crucified Christ or the Passion as a "Book" that provides more knowledge than any written text</note>—more clearly than in any other book whatsoever—about divine justice and how it is impossible for sin ever to go unpunished.

## CHAPTER XIX

->WHERE THE SOUL MAY SEE THAT DIVINE WISDOM HAS USED INFINITE CARE AND DILIGENCE TO SAVE US THROUGH MERCY, WITHOUT DISREGARDING JUSTICE<-

The soul also understands how God the Father willed that His Son should suffer the agony of death and the torments of the Cross, rather than let the sins of the human race go unpunished. In this Book, the soul sees, moreover, how infinite was the goodness and pity of Christ. He had such great compassion for us that—perceiving that neither we ourselves nor any other created being could offer sufficient <term>satisfaction: the act of making amends or paying the debt owed for an offense to restore justice</term> for our sins—He Himself offered satisfaction for us. He did this so that we would not be left to eternal <term>damnation: the state of being condemned to everlasting punishment</term> and suffering.

<summary>Angela explains that reflecting on the Cross reveals the severity of sin and the necessity of Christ's suffering to satisfy divine justice. She portrays Christ’s sacrifice as an act of infinite mercy that pays the debt of human sin which humanity could not pay itself.</summary>
<keywords>divine justice, mercy, the Cross as a book, satisfaction for sin, penance, compassion of Christ, salvation</keywords>

...is to say, the life and death of Jesus Christ. And whereas this reading, or rather knowledge, cannot possibly be acquired without devout, pure, humble, fervent, attentive, and constant prayer (not with the lips alone, but with the heart and mind and all the strength), something must be said of prayer, as well as of the Book of Life. <note>Angela argues that the "Book of Life" (Christ's example) cannot be understood through intellect alone; it requires a total spiritual engagement of the person.</note>

It is through prayer and in prayer that we find God. There are divers kinds of prayer, but in these three kinds alone is God to be found. The first is corporal <note>From the Latin "corpus," meaning "body"; refers to physical or vocal prayer.</note>, the second mental, and the third supernatural.

Corporal prayer is that which is always accompanied by the sound of words and by bodily exercises, such as kneeling down, asking pardon, and bowing oneself. This kind do I continually perform; and the reason thereof is, that, desiring to exercise myself in mental prayer, I was sometimes deceived and hindered therefrom by idleness and sleep, and did thus lose time. For this reason do I exercise myself in corporal prayer, and this corporal prayer leadeth me unto the mental. But this must be done very attentively. Therefore when thou sayest the <term>Our Father<gloss>original: "Paternoster," the Latin name for the prayer taught by Jesus to his disciples.</gloss></term>, thou must consider well that which thou sayest, and not repeat it in haste in order to say it a certain number of times, as do those vain women who perform good deeds for a reward. <note>Angela warns against treating prayer as a mechanical chore or a transaction to earn "points" with God, a practice she associates with superficial piety.</note>

<margin>How rare!</margin> Mental prayer is when the meditation of God filleth the mind so entirely that it thinketh on naught else save on God. But when some other reflection entereth into the mind it asketh not that it should be mental prayer...

And understanding, it knows; but that which it knows it cannot explain, because all that it perceives and feels is above its own nature.

In these three degrees of prayer, therefore, man learns to know God and himself. And knowing Him, he loves Him, and loving Him he desires to possess Him; and this is the sign of love, for he who loves not only a part of himself, but the whole, transforms himself into the thing beloved. <note>Angela describes "mystical union," a state where the boundaries between the soul and God seem to disappear through the intensity of love.</note>

Therefore, it must be known that divine wisdom has ordered all things and given to each its appointed place. ...and manifesting both God and Himself. Shall we say that Christ prayed in vain? Then why are you neglectful, seeing that nothing is obtained without prayer? Since Christ—who was truly God and truly Man—did not pray for His own sake, but so that you might have an example of true prayer, it is necessary that you pray if you desire anything. For without prayer, you can obtain nothing. If He, who was actually God, would accept nothing without having prayed and asked for it, how do you—a miserable creature—dare to hope to receive anything without supplication and prayer?

Now you know well that without divine light <note>In mystical theology, "divine light" is the spiritual insight or "illumination" that allows a soul to perceive God's truth.</note> and grace, no one is saved; it is through this divine light that a person begins and moves forward on the right path. And even if you have attained the highest perfection <note>The state of "perfection" refers to a soul's complete union with God's will, the goal of the spiritual life.</note> and desire to be even more enlightened in order to remain in that state, you must pray. 

Pray if you desire faith; pray if you desire hope; pray if you desire **charity** <term>Caritas: the highest form of love, specifically the selfless love for God and neighbor</term>, or poverty, or obedience, or chastity; pray if you desire any virtue whatsoever.

The way in which you must pray is this: you must read the **Book of the Life of Christ Jesus**, which was a life of poverty, suffering, contempt, and true obedience. <note>Angela frequently uses the "Book of Life" as a metaphor for meditating on the physical and emotional experiences of Jesus's earthly existence.</note> When you have fully entered into this life and have profited by it, you will be afflicted by many tribulations—

...of himself. <note>This completes the thought from the previous page regarding the soul's realization of its own inherent emptiness.</note> Therefore, perceiving and knowing himself to be nothing and empty of all good, he will more earnestly offer praise and prayer to the majesty of the divine goodness which he sees and understands through this humility; and here virtue is born in him through the grace sent to him by God.

The greatest and chief of all virtues is <term>charity</term>, <note>In this context, charity (Latin: *caritas*) refers to the highest form of love—a selfless, spiritual love for God and others.</note> which is love toward God and one’s neighbor. And this love springs from that light; for when the soul perceives itself to be nothing, and sees God inclined toward such vile nothingness, abasing Himself and uniting Himself to it, the soul burns so violently with love for Him that through this burning love it is made one with God. And being thus transformed by love, what creature is there who would not love to the utmost of his power?

Truly, the soul thus transformed through love of its Creator loves all creatures created by Him in a proper and fitting way; for it perceives God in all His creatures, and beholds how greatly He loves them. The soul rejoices in this and in the good fortune of its neighbor, and grieves and mourns at its neighbor's evil fortune; and being kindly disposed, it does not presume to judge or despise the neighbor when misfortune falls upon them. For, enlightened by the light mentioned before, it looks at itself and knows itself to be in a plight as bad or even worse than that which has befallen its neighbor. And even if the soul has not fallen, it knows that it had no power of its own to resist, but that it had been helped by the grace...

What I have said of love, which grows from the root of humility, may likewise be said of faith, of hope, and of all the virtues; according to their different natures, they are all founded upon humility, and we will now speak of them one by one.

Faith is born of humility. Knowing itself to be nothing and lacking in all divine things, the soul believes what is told to it according to our faith. Furthermore, perceiving through its humility that it has no power of its own and is not able to achieve anything by its own strength, it places its hope in God. It is the same with the other virtues, which you can understand better for yourselves, taught by divine obedience, than by seeing them written down in any book.

## CHAPTER XXVI

### HOW HUMILITY CAUSES US TO SEE THE MULTITUDE OF OUR SINS AND HOW A HUMBLE LIFE WORKS CONTRARY TO THEM

**T**HIS silence proceeds from nothing else except from that twofold overwhelming: namely, by the divine immensity <term>divine immensity: the theological concept of God's infinite nature which exceeds all physical space and human understanding</term> and its own vileness; <note>In the medieval tradition, "vileness" (vilitas) refers to human lowliness and insignificance compared to the infinite nature of God, rather than moral "evil."</note> and the soul is thus overwhelmed in the aforementioned light of humility.

But, I pray you, where is this humility? Where is this vileness? Where is this light? Where is this silence?
