--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues Angela’s instructions on spiritual discipline, transitioning from the description of the effects of humility to the practical methods of achieving it through prayer and meditation on the Passion of Christ.</meta>

# THE BLESSED ANGELA

And by what means may we attain these things mentioned above? Certainly, we may attain them through fervent, pure, constant, and earnest prayer, and by reading the *Book of the Life of Jesus Christ*—that is, by reflecting upon His life and death. <note>In medieval mysticism, the "Book of the Life of Christ" refers to the practice of using Christ's life and physical sufferings as a spiritual "text" to be studied and imitated.</note> When the soul reflects upon His sufferings, it is granted the ability to recognize the multitude of its own sins and how it has offended God with every part of its being; consequently, the soul humbles itself, as has already been stated.

Therefore, I pray that you be always of one mind, and that there be no divisions or sects among you. Instead, let there be that within your souls which brings all discord into agreement under a single will; and this is lowliness. <note>The term "lowliness" is used here as a translation for humility (*humilitas*), emphasizing a state of being small or of little account in one's own eyes.</note> Lowliness does not concern itself with a person’s wealth of knowledge or common sense, but only inclines the soul to perceive its own faults and wretchedness. It leads the soul to accuse and judge itself, convincing itself of its own sins so that it may strive to remedy them. Lowliness does not consider itself superior to anyone, nor does it persuade others to think themselves superior. It does not make men puffed up or argumentative in speech, even though its way of life may offend those who are opposed to such simplicity.

Therefore, my children, I desire that your lives—your tongues kept silent in this humility of infants—and the zeal of your wise compassion should be like a clear mirror to those who oppose this way of life. Oh, my beloved, if I were to hear it said of you that humility had made you of one heart and one mind, my soul would be at peace.

<summary>Angela of Foligno explains that humility is achieved through prayer and meditation on Christ's life, urging her followers to maintain unity and avoid intellectual pride.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, prayer, Book of the Life of Christ, humility, lowliness, unity, compassion, spiritual discipline</keywords>

## CHAPTER XXVII

### ON CHARITY, AND HOW WE SHOULD FEAR THAT OUR LOVE IS NOT OF THE TRUE KIND AND DOES NOT FULFILL THE NECESSARY CONDITIONS

**CHARITY** and the love of God is the greatest of all virtues; without it, prayer is of no account and does not please God, and all other virtues are entirely without profit. So that you may believe this is true, listen to the *Book of the Life of Jesus Christ* <note>The "Book of the Life of Christ" refers to the Gospels or the meditative practice of using Christ’s earthly life as a template for spiritual conduct.</note>, which says: "If you bring your gift to the altar and there remember that your brother has something against you, leave your gift there before the altar; go your way and first be reconciled to your brother." <note>A translation of the Latin Vulgate, Matthew 5:23–24.</note> For the gift of prayer is worth nothing if it is not offered within the bonds of charity.

Moreover, when we are taught to ask for the forgiveness of our sins in the Lord’s Prayer—"Forgive us our debts <note>original: *debita*, often translated as 'trespasses' or 'sins'</note> as we forgive those who have a debt to us"—it means that our own debts will be forgiven during prayer only to the degree that we have forgiven our brothers for the injuries and offenses they commit...

Know, my children, that just as love contains all worth and goodness, so hate contains all unworthiness, sin, and evil. Therefore, my dear children, there is nothing in this world—neither man, nor devil, nor any other thing—which I fear as greatly as I fear love. For love penetrates the soul more than anything else, nor is there anything that so fills the mind and the whole heart as love does; so that if the soul does not have the armor <note>The "arms" or "armor" refers to the spiritual defenses provided by the virtues of wisdom and discretion.</note> with which to protect itself, it may easily fall into evil and suffer great ruin. I am not speaking here of wrongful love <note>In medieval theology, "wrongful love" refers to lust or carnal desire.</note>, which must be avoided by everyone and shunned as something perilous and diabolical; rather, I am speaking of good and spiritual love, such as exists between God and the soul, or between one neighbor and another.

The truth of this is plainly proven. For if the love which the soul bears toward God is not armed with great wisdom and discretion, but instead moves with excessive fervor, it will then either quickly cease, or the soul will be deceived, or it will lead toward some unseemly end. For things in which there is no order are neither good nor healthy; <note>Angela refers to the concept of *ordinata caritas* or "ordered love," a traditional teaching that love must be prioritized and balanced by reason to remain holy.</note> and for this reason, there are many people who believe they are filled with the love of God, when in reality they are filled with hatred of Him and instead love the world, the flesh, and the devil.

When, therefore, any man loves God because He can...

At first, reason <note>In medieval theology, 'reason' is the faculty of the soul that allows a person to distinguish between good and evil.</note> does dissuade them from this, for as yet it has not been entirely overcome by love; but love grows and reason begins to be obscured and the spirit is weakened and commences to believe that it is no sin and in no way hurtful to the soul to touch the thing beloved. Therefore does the spirit permit this, and so does it begin to do wrong and to fall from the state of perfection little by little, as reason is obscured by love. 

And according as it sinks it begins to esteem as nothing those things which are dangerous, and to say, "This we may do, for we mean no evil and it is not a great sin." <note>This illustrates the "subtle traps" Angela warns of, where the soul justifies small compromises that gradually erode moral discipline.</note> So gradually these things come to be considered lawful, and, the love increasing more and more, one submits entirely to the will of the other, each doing what the other desires and not opposing any reason to the contrary. Hence it follows that the lover obtains all his desire, and because of the aforesaid disorder he cannot refuse if he be invited to evil-doing; and if he be not invited, then does he invite the other, knowing that it is pleasing to the beloved.

Then do they refrain from prayer, from abstinence, from solitude, and from all the other virtues which they did formerly practice, imitating divine love <note>original: *amor divinus*</note> in this most miserable worldly love <note>original: *amor mundanus*</note>. And sometimes this love increases to such a degree that neither the words nor the presence of the beloved give the satisfaction they did at first. But loving more ardently, each desires to know if the beloved be wounded by the **sword of love** <note>A common medieval mystical metaphor for the overwhelming and sometimes painful piercing of the heart by intense affection; here, Angela applies it to the destructive nature of carnal obsession.</note> as deeply...

<summary>This page describes the process by which reason is blinded by misplaced affection, leading individuals to abandon spiritual disciplines and justify sinful behavior. Angela warns that once reason is obscured, even "small" sins are eventually viewed as lawful, leading to a complete spiritual collapse.</summary>

# OF FOLIGNO

as is the lover, and then it is that they fall into peril. Then are they confident and sure of one another, and because they are satisfied neither with words nor with presence, both lover and beloved are inclined toward all manner of evil.

For this reason do I say that I fear love more than all things, for herein are all evils committed. Therefore take heed of it, as you would of a serpent. <note>Angela often uses the "serpent" as a metaphor for the subtle, creeping way that temptation enters a life that otherwise seems holy.</note>

Because of evil love, therefore, I fear even good love between one person and another, for good love often turns into evil in the manner described above.

### CHAPTER XXVIII

->THE SOUL IS UNITED WITH GOD IN THREE DIFFERENT<-
->WAYS, BY WHICH IT IS EQUIPPED WITH A WEAPON<-
->TO GOVERN THE LOVE OF GOD AND OF ONE'S<-
->NEIGHBOR<-

IF the love of God is not directed with discretion <term>discretion: the spiritual gift of discernment (Latin: *discretio*), allowing one to distinguish between divine inspiration and human impulse</term> and protected by its weapons, it turns into evil. The weapons <note>In the tradition of spiritual warfare, "weapons" refer to specific virtues and disciplines used to defend the soul against sin.</note> by which the good love of God and one’s neighbor may be governed are given to a person through the transformation of the soul.

Now this transformation is threefold: sometimes the soul is transformed in the will of God, sometimes with God, and sometimes within God and God within the soul. <note>These three stages represent a progression of mystical union, moving from aligning one's choices with God's will to a total indwelling where the distinction between the soul and the Divine becomes blurred.</note>

<summary>Angela concludes her warning on the dangers of disordered human affection and introduces the three modes of mystical transformation that allow a soul to love God and neighbor correctly.</summary>
<keywords>discretion, spiritual weapons, transformation of the soul, divine union, neighborly love, mystical theology, Angela of Foligno</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XXVIII (commenced on page 123) regarding how the soul’s transformation serves as a "weapon" or tool to regulate spiritual love and prevent it from becoming disordered or purely emotional.</meta>

-># THE BLESSED ANGELA<-

The first transformation is when the soul uses all its effort to imitate the life of Christ crucified, for in this, the will of God Himself is made manifest.

The second is when the soul is united with God and loves God; not only because it chooses to do so, but because it has a great knowledge and joy of God. This experience, however, is one that the soul is still able to explain and describe in words.

The third is when the soul is so entirely made one with God, and God with it, that it knows and enjoys the highest mysteries with God. These things cannot possibly be expressed in words, nor even imagined, except by the person who experiences them. <note>This refers to the "ineffable" nature of mystical union—a common theme in medieval theology where the highest experiences of God transcend human language.</note>

Therefore, the first transformation does not govern the love of lovers as perfectly as it should (even though it has much control), because it can still be deceived. <note>Simple imitation of Christ's outward life, while good, can sometimes be mimicked by the ego or misunderstood without deeper spiritual insight.</note> The second, if the soul is very alert, is sufficient to govern love. But the third is supreme in the ruling of love, because this third transformation, together with the second, is perfectly instilled into the soul and is given to it by grace. And this third and second transformation (even if not yet perfect) nevertheless produce a certain wisdom. By means of this wisdom, the soul is enabled to govern both the love of God and the love of the soul <note>In this context, "love of the soul" refers to spiritual friendships or the love one has for their neighbor.</note>. For the knowledge, sweetness, and fervor which come from the grace of God so rule the soul and fill it with this wisdom that love endures and continues as it began. It does not show itself outwardly through laughter, dancing, or other similar gestures. <note>Angela often cautioned against "spiritual gluttony" or excessive outward displays of emotion, which she felt could be a sign of a superficial or uncontrolled spiritual life.</note>

In the same way, the soul behaves wisely and soberly in the—

Whoever does not feel themselves to be filled with this wisdom should never unite themselves in a close and exclusive love <note>original: "singular love"; an intense personal attachment that risks becoming more about the person than about God.</note> with any person whatsoever, no matter how much they might do it for God's sake, even with the good intentions of avoiding those perils which proceed from such love. Neither should any person ally themselves with anyone for the sake of love, unless they have first learned those things which enable them to separate themselves easily from any person whenever they choose.

## CHAPTER XXIX

### OF THE VARIOUS PROPERTIES OF LOVE

**I**n order to understand how the aforementioned wisdom rules the love of God, you must know that love has various properties. Firstly, it makes one tender; secondly, it makes one sick; and thirdly, it makes one strong.

when the <term>Man of Sorrows</term><note>A title for Jesus Christ, originating from the Book of Isaiah (53:3), emphasizing the suffering He endured for humanity.</note> lived this mortal life, He was ever upon a bitter cross of <term>penitence</term><note>In Angela's spirituality, "penitence" is not just feeling sorry for sin, but an active, lifelong participation in the sufferings and poverty of Christ.</note>. The length of time, therefore, ordained for penance, is as long as a person shall live. The amount and severity of it must be according to what a person can perform with <term>discretion</term><note>A crucial virtue in medieval mysticism, "discretion" means the wisdom to balance intense religious practice with one's physical and mental health.</note>. And this is unity with the will of God, a unity which He has ordained should not be in words only, but in doing the works of the Cross and of penitence, such as Christ Himself performed.

When the soul is transformed in God and is in God, and has that perfect union and fullness of vision, it is quiet and performs no work whatsoever <note>This refers to the state of "mystical rest," where the soul is so united with God that active thought or effort ceases, and it simply receives the Divine presence.</note>. But when it comes again to itself, it strives to transform itself into the will of God in order that it may behold again that vision. With this vision, it directs its love of God and of its neighbor as though with arms <note>A common medieval metaphor where the two "arms" of the soul represent the dual commandment of love for God and love for one's neighbor.</note>. For in this state the soul beholds the Being of God, and how all creatures have their being from that <term>Supreme Being</term><note>God viewed as the source and sustainer of all existence.</note>, and it perceives that there exists nothing which does not derive its being from that same Supreme Being.

From this vision the soul, thus instructed, draws marvelous learning, inexpressible wisdom, and a mature gravity. From this vision it draws likewise the true knowledge of the perfection of all things belonging to that Supreme Being; and it cannot doubt it, for it perceives how all things made by Him are well made. Things are bad only when we ourselves destroy what the Supreme Being has made well.

The aforesaid vision of the Being uncreate doth not only bestow <term>love uncreate</term><note>original: *amor increatus*. In medieval mysticism, this refers to the Holy Spirit or God’s own love dwelling within the person. It should be supposed that when this vision is given unto the soul, the soul worketh and desireth to be united as closely as possible with the Supreme Being; but then the love uncreate worketh within the soul and persuadeth it to forsake all creatures that it may be more closely united with itself. Wherefore doth this love uncreate do the works of the other love. The principle of such a love is to illuminate, and to awaken fresh desire. And, verily, the new love is the stronger, and for this the soul worketh nothing. But the love uncreate is the one which worketh.

It is true that we are all sons of God by creation, but His elect <term>spiritual children</term><note>Angela distinguishes between those who are simply created by God and those who actively choose to follow Him through "spiritual" devotion.</note> are they in whom the God of love has planted His love and in whom He delights, because He finds His own likeness in them. This likeness in the soul of each one of the sons of God is formed there solely through the grace of God and the perfect divine love. And perfect is he who has already transformed his behavior and life into the likeness of the life of Christ, who lived in this world poor and despised and full of suffering.

# CHAPTER XXXIV

### OF THE PROPERTIES OF LOVERS

There are three properties peculiar to lovers that are necessary to know. Likewise, there are certain signs of love by which everyone may know whether they are a true lover or not.

The first property is to be truly transformed into the will of the Beloved. To me, it seems that His will is His life, which He displays within Himself. In this, He shows us <term>poverty</term><note>In the Franciscan tradition of Angela, "poverty" is not just a lack of money, but a total stripping away of the "self" and worldly attachments to make room for God.</note>, <term>suffering</term><note>original: *dolor*. This refers to the soul's participation in the Passion of Christ, sharing in His physical and spiritual pain.</note>, and <term>contempt</term><note>original: *contemptus mundi*. This refers to being despised by the world or choosing to be lowly and rejected, just as Christ was.</note>. We must all truly experience these things; when the soul is strengthened and practiced in them, neither vice nor temptation can enter it.

The second property is to be transformed into the characteristics of the Beloved, of which I will mention only three for now. The first is <term>love</term><note>original: *caritas*. This is the "ordered love" or charity that loves God above all else and loves others for His sake.</note>; that is, to love all creatures in a fitting way. The second is to be humble and gentle. The third (which God grants to His rightful children) is <term>steadfastness</term><note>original: *stabilitas*. A spiritual state of being "unmoveable" or constant, where the soul is no longer tossed about by shifting emotions or worldly changes.</note>; for the closer the soul is to God, the less it changes in...

<summary>Angela explains that love for God is proportional to our knowledge of Him and outlines the first two "properties" of a true lover: transformation into God's will and adopting His characteristics like humility and steadfastness.</summary>

### *Of the Signs of Love*

These are the signs of love:

The first sign of true love is that the lover submits his own will to the will of the beloved.

The second is that he forsakes all other friendship which might be contrary to his love. He likewise leaves behind father and mother, brother and sister, and every other affection which is contrary to the will of the beloved.

The third is that there is nothing hidden in one which is not revealed to the other; and this (according to my thinking) is the sum and completion of all the other signs and workings of love.

<summary>Angela describes the final stage of spiritual transformation where the soul lives in God and provides three diagnostic signs of true divine love: submission of will, the abandonment of conflicting worldly ties, and total transparency with the Beloved.</summary>

->THE BLESSED ANGELA<-

The fourth and last is that the lover strives to make himself like the beloved. If the beloved is poor, he strives to be poor; if the beloved is considered <term>vile</term><note>In this context, "vile" (from the Latin *vilitas*) refers to being of low social status, humble, or disregarded by the world, rather than being morally wicked.</note>, then he seeks to be vile also; if the beloved is in grief, he seeks to be a sharer of that woe, so that the condition of one may be like that of the other.

Truly, I hold that true and perfect love cannot exist between the rich and the poor, the honorable and the lowly, the sorrowful and the joyous, because these conditions are vastly different from one another. There can be no perfect love between them because one does not share the condition of the other. Love is a true virtue which not only makes things like one another but also unites them; it always leads the soul toward its like and not toward its opposite.

Certainly, Jesus Christ, the Eternal Love, possessed all these signs. He submitted His will to man’s will, and even unto death was He obedient to those who slew Him, although He could have overthrown them simply by raising His hand. Moreover, He forsook all friendship, His kindred, His mother, and His own flesh and blood for man’s sake, leaving them and going to His death upon the Cross. He also revealed His secrets to us, saying, <note>original: "Iam non dicam vos servos..." from John 15:15.</note> “I have not called you servants,” and so forth. Moreover, He desired to make Himself like man, taking upon Himself true humanity and mortality, becoming like man in all things, saving only sin.

Therefore ought we also to do all these things for His

And though we may find it difficult to perform <term>penance</term><note>Voluntary acts of self-discipline or suffering intended to show repentance for sins and spiritual devotion.</note> (which, nonetheless, seems necessary to me), let us at least turn this necessity into a virtue. Let us bear the <term>tribulations</term><note>Great distress or trials, often seen in this context as a test of faith or a means of spiritual growth.</note> sent to us by God with patience and a willing spirit.

There is no doubt that when people face troubles both externally and internally, it is a sign that they are loved by the <term>Beloved</term><note>A mystical title for Jesus Christ, emphasizing the deep, personal bond of love between the soul and God.</note>. The things that God the Father loved, chose, and gave to His only dearly beloved Son, are the same things that this only Son chooses and gives to His own dear children. God the Father chose for His Son poverty, contempt, and suffering, as well as persecutions and afflictions. The Son of God endured these things both outwardly and inwardly—experiencing weariness, fear, anxiety, and agony, along with countless other trials that no tongue can describe nor heart imagine.

Let us learn, then, how to endure worldly troubles with patience—and even with cheerfulness—because this is the sign that the Beloved takes delight in us, has chosen us, and will grant us the pledge of His <term>inheritance</term><note>The promise of eternal life and the "Kingdom of Heaven" granted to the faithful.</note>.

When you suffer, think only of the sufferings of the <term>Man of Sorrows</term><note>A title for Jesus Christ originating from the biblical Book of Isaiah (53:3), portraying Him as a figure who took on the grief and suffering of humanity.</note>, and all your own sorrows will be healed.

# CHAPTER XXXVI

## OF THREE BENEFITS DERIVED FROM THE MOST HOLY TRIBULATION

**FROM** this most holy <term>tribulation</term><note>Great distress or trials. In this mystical tradition, suffering is not seen as a punishment, but as a "holy" tool used by God to refine the soul.</note> proceed three things unknown unto us.

   Firstly, it <term>converteth</term><note>From the Latin 'conversio,' meaning a turning around. It refers to the soul turning its focus away from the world and toward God.</note> us unto God ; and, being converted, it draweth us more nigh unto Him.

   Secondly, it maketh us to grow ; just as good earth, well prepared, putteth forth shoots and beareth fruit when the rain falleth upon it, so doth the soul grow in <term>virtue</term><note>Spiritual and moral excellence, such as patience, humility, and charity.</note> when tribulation cometh upon it.

   Thirdly, it giveth peace, comfort, rest, and calm.

   This most holy tribulation is exceeding profitable for us ; wherefore let us not avoid it nor hold it in horror. For of a certainty and with all mine heart say I unto you, that our noblest <term>advocates</term><note>Literally those who plead a case in court. Angela suggests that our earthly sufferings act as legal representatives that justify us before God's judgment.</note> and truest witnesses, those who will be most readily believed in the presence of God, are precisely these holy and precious tribulations, whose worth we know not ; for with them do we purchase the kingdom of heaven, and the joys of eternity are obtained through poverty, tears, suffering, and persecution.

   And I do firmly believe that nothing doth so help towards the leading of a good life as do these tribulations ; wherefore do I feel an holy envy of all who are troubled. It is certain, oh my sons, that if the worth and profit of

The better he knoweth the more doth he love, the more he loveth the more powerfully doth he work, and this work is the sign and measure of love. # CHAPTER XXXVIII

# CHAPTER XXXIX

## OF THE MANY SIGNS AND EFFECTS OF LOVE WHICH ARE CAUSED BY THE SACRAMENT OF THE EUCHARIST

**I COME** now to the Sacrament <term>Sacrament: A religious ceremony or ritual regarded as an outward and visible sign of inward and spiritual divine grace.</term> of love and grace which is called the Eucharist <term>Eucharist: The Christian ceremony commemorating the Last Supper, in which bread and wine are consecrated and consumed.</term>, and I will say something more about this than what has already been said.

This Sacrament has the power to inspire devout prayer within us and ensures that its power works for our good. It likewise grants us deep humility of heart and moves us with a desire for true love and charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to the Latin "caritas," meaning the highest form of love—a selfless, spiritual love for God and others.</note>. I have no doubt, but am quite certain, that no soul who gazes upon and reflects on this holy Sacrament could be so indifferent to love that they would not be instantly filled with it, considering how greatly they have been loved in this Sacrament.

Truly, it seems to me that this Sacrament—this holy Mystery—must be considered with great care by those who wish to celebrate and receive this sacrifice. The soul must not pass hastily over this meditation, but must dwell upon it carefully and earnestly. And although the things that can be said of this Sacrament cannot be fully explained or described, I believe they can be organized into seven points, or meditations, which should be considered one by one.

Firstly, this holy Mystery is new and above all things <note>The text ends here, beginning a numbered list of seven points regarding the spiritual significance of the Eucharist.</note>

marvellous, and far beyond our understanding. Although this Mystery was revealed long ago, as recorded in the Holy Scriptures, and is ancient in its tradition, it is still "new" in the way the Sacrament is presented, allowing us—His creatures—to receive grace and new strength. For we know well, and hold as an absolute certainty of faith, that through the holy words first spoken by Christ Himself (which He commanded His ministers, the priests, to speak as well), the bread and wine are substantially transformed into Christ by infinite divine power. 

Therefore, the substance of the bread and wine becomes the body of Christ through <term>transubstantiation: the theological concept that the "substance" or inner reality of the bread and wine changes into the body and blood of Christ, even though the outward appearance remains the same.</term>. Christ, as both God and Man, enters into that Mystery through the act of consecration. The color, taste, essence <note>original: "virtue," meaning the inherent properties or power of the substance.</note>, shape, and all the physical qualities of that same bread and wine still remain; however, these qualities do not exist *in* Christ, but are maintained by divine power over their own natures. For the color exists by itself, and the taste, shape, and other qualities all exist by themselves <note>This refers to the Scholastic idea of "accidents"—physical properties like color and taste that exist without their usual underlying substance (bread/wine) after the consecration.</note>.

Because of this, this "new thing" is truly great. It is a work of divine wisdom performing out of supreme love, infinite power, and great goodness—a work the Creator performs within His own creations. Moreover, there are many other special and new things which the body and blood of Christ accomplish within His friends and His chosen people. But let no one be shocked by this, nor by the other wonders performed in this Sacrament, for one must always remember the power of Him who performs them. Neither should anyone wonder how the

<summary>This page explains the doctrine of transubstantiation, describing how the substance of the bread and wine changes into Christ while the physical "accidents" (taste, color, and shape) remain through divine power.</summary>

The Sacrament can be upon many altars at the same time—on this side of the sea and on the other, here and in other places as well. For He says: “You do not understand Me; I have done this without your help and worked without you, and nothing is impossible for Me. I am the One who made you what you are, but for your own benefit, you cannot understand this now. If I had wanted to, I could have made you strong enough to understand, but I did this so that through your faith you might be deserving <note>In this context, "deserving" refers to the theological concept of "merit." In medieval thought, believing in something that cannot be seen or proven by logic was considered more spiritually valuable than believing in something obvious.</note>; for there would be no merit in it if you had seen clearly why it was done. Believe, therefore, and do not doubt.”

Secondly, this Sacrament is gracious above all things and sets love ablaze. For the motive of Him who established this most holy Sacrament was the greatest of all things, and the benefit that would come from it was no less great. I do not know what name I should give it, except that of immeasurable love, because it was out of His boundless love that He instituted this Sacrament.

<margin>|</margin>Because of His great love for us, He entered into the Sacrament and will remain there until the end of the world. He did this not only in memory of His death, which is our salvation, but so that He might always and forever remain near us. And whoever wishes to enter into this deep Mystery <term>Mystery: A religious truth that can only be known through divine revelation and cannot be fully understood by human reason alone.</term> must have "good eyes" <note>This refers to "spiritual sight" or the eyes of faith, which allow a believer to see the divine reality behind the physical appearance of the bread and wine.</note>. Christ knew at His Last Supper that He must soon depart in the body; therefore, overcome by the love that always unites the lover and the beloved, He established this Sacra-

Oh, burning and unquenchable love! Such was and is the love which He held for us! For although He had death before His eyes and foresaw the most horrible suffering, He nevertheless gave Himself for those very same sinners who persecuted Him, so that He might be with us at least in this <term>Sacrament: The Eucharist or Holy Communion, which Christians believe is the real presence of Christ</term>. The joy of this Sacrament lies in being united with the <term>Son of Man: A title Jesus used for himself to emphasize his shared humanity with all people</term>.

What soul is so cruel that, reflecting deeply and earnestly on that love, would not feel moved to return the love of such a Lover? He never forgot us in life or in death, but gave His whole self so that He might be forever united with us in love. Truly, there is no soul who, thinking well upon this, would not be completely transformed by love.

Thirdly, this Sacrament is compassionate above all things and leads us toward compassion and suffering, because He endured mortal and unspeakable pain.

When Christ ordained this most holy Sacrament, He was at the point of being separated from His beloved disciples and from the Virgin Mary, His most dear mother. ...[ody to] treatment <note>The text continues from the previous page's "He gave His b...", likely referring to Christ giving His body to the cruel treatment of His captors.</note>, the Cross, the hard nails, and the other things then being prepared for Him. This Sacrament is, therefore, most proper as a memorial of His grievous and bitter <term>Passion: the period of intense spiritual and physical suffering undergone by Jesus leading up to his death</term> and the shedding of His blood for us miserable sinners. This is why Christ said, “As often as you shall do this, you do it in remembrance of Me.” <note>Refers to 1 Corinthians 11:24-25.</note> And what soul is there who could behold these sufferings and not itself be transformed by suffering? Certainly not one, except for someone who does not remember the cruel sufferings of Christ’s heart.

Fourthly, this Sacrament is above all things worthy and venerable, to be regarded with the utmost reverence and humility, because He who ordained this sacrifice was Jesus Christ, God supreme and <term>uncreate: existing eternally without having been created; a term used to distinguish God from all other beings</term>.

The soul who considers this sacrifice must not only consider Him who ordained it, but likewise that which is contained in that Sacrament. For herein is contained God uncreate, invisible, and <term>omnipotent: all-powerful</term>, He who does all things, who is most merciful and just, Creator of the heavens and the earth, of all things visible and invisible; and this is the chief thing upon which we need to reflect here.

Consequently, in this present life, the soul sometimes receives greater delight from this "lesser" thing <note>the human nature of Christ</note> than from the "greater" <note>the divine nature</note>, because it is better able to receive and understand the lesser thing which it beholds in **God incarnate** <term>God incarnate: the core Christian belief that the divine Son of God took on a human body and nature as Jesus Christ</term>. The soul certainly beholds here the union of many things contained within this mystery. From the "lesser" part—that is, the humanity—it is led toward the divinity; and in the same way, from the divinity it is led toward the humanity. For this reason, the soul that considers this deeply beholds the ineffable divinity, in which are all the treasures of wisdom, knowledge, and incorruptible riches. In that divinity, it also beholds the only joys that can truly satisfy our minds, along with many other unspeakable things.

Furthermore, the soul beholds here the most **Precious Soul** and the holy, **immaculate oblation** <term>oblation: a formal religious offering or sacrifice made to God</term>, together with all the virtues and gifts of the Holy Spirit. It beholds here the most precious body of our Redeemer, the blood by which we were saved and given new life, along with other unspeakable things, all of which should stir within us...

Many other gifts and benefits beyond counting are conferred upon us if we receive this Sacrament worthily; it cannot be expressed how much grace the soul obtains in one single worthy reception, provided only that its own misdeeds cause no hindrance.

# TREATISE III

## OF THE MANY VISIONS AND CONSOLATIONS RECEIVED BY THE BLESSED ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

### FIRST VISION AND CONSOLATION, WHEREIN SHE BEHELD GOD INASMUCH AS HE IS ALL GOODNESS; WHENCE COMETH HATRED OF THIS LIFE AND THE DESIRE TO ENJOY GOD

**BLESSED** God and Father of my Lord Jesus Christ, who comforts us in all our tribulations, You have truly deigned to console me, a sinner, in every tribulation. For during the time of my conversion, already related in the <term>eighteenth step</term> <note>Angela’s spiritual autobiography is structured around thirty "steps" or stages of her conversion from a worldly life to one of mystical union with God.</note>, and after the enlightenment miraculously granted to me as I was repeating the <term>Paternoster</term> <note>original: "Paternoster." This is the Latin name for the "Our Father" or the Lord's Prayer.</note>, I felt great consolation and sweetness in this manner.

I was inspired and drawn to the contemplation of the blessed union of the divinity and humanity of Christ, and in this contemplation I felt an exceedingly great delight, which was greater than any I had ever felt before. For this reason I remained for a great part of that day standing in the cell <note>A small, simple room used by religious recluses or nuns for prayer and sleep.</note> where I was praying,

<meta>This page continues the account of Angela’s first "consolation" or mystical vision. She describes the physical and spiritual effects of her divine encounter, specifically her temporary loss of speech and her commitment to the Franciscan ideal of poverty.</meta>

astonished, locked in and alone. My heart was entirely wrapped up in that joy, and I became like one who is <term>dumb</term> <note>In historical English, "dumb" refers to being struck speechless or unable to talk, often as a result of an overwhelming physical or spiritual shock.</note> and I did lose my speech. For this reason, it happened that when my companion <note>This likely refers to Masazuola, Angela's long-time friend and spiritual companion who lived with her after Angela’s family passed away.</note> arrived, she believed that I was about to die; but she only caused me weariness and was a hindrance to me.

Once, before I had finished giving all I possessed to the poor (although only a little then remained for me to give), while I was persevering in these matters, it happened that one evening when I was at prayer, it seemed to me that I felt nothing whatsoever of God. Because of this, I lamented and prayed to God, saying:
"Lord, that which I do, I do only so that I may find You; therefore, having done it, grant me the grace that I may find You."

And I said many other similar things in my prayer, and this answer was granted to me: "What do you desire?"

Then I said: "I desire neither gold nor silver; indeed, if You were to give me the whole world I would not accept it, because I desire You only."

Then He said to me: "Strive diligently and make yourself ready, for when you have accomplished what you are now doing, the whole <term>Trinity</term> <note>The central Christian belief that God is one essence but exists in three persons: the Father, the Son (Jesus), and the Holy Spirit.</note> will descend into you."

Many other things were also promised to me, which eased me of my <term>tribulation</term> <note>A term for deep distress, suffering, or a period of great trial.</note> and filled me with divine sweetness. And from that hour, I waited for the thing which had been told me to be immediately fulfilled.

And I related this to my companion <note>This companion is Masazuola, a close friend and spiritual sister who accompanied Angela throughout her later life.</note> with some doubtfulness, seeing that great things had been told and promised to me; nevertheless they had left me with that divine sweetness and gentleness.

After this I went to the church of <term>Saint Francis</term> <note>The Basilica of Saint Francis in Assisi, the burial place of the saint and a major pilgrimage site.</note>, near to <term>Assisi</term> <note>A town in the Umbria region of Italy, famous as the birthplace of St. Francis and the center of the Franciscan movement.</note>, and the promise was fulfilled by the way as I went there. Nevertheless, I had not yet finished giving all things to the poor, but there was little yet remaining. This had happened because of the death of a noble and holy man, to whom this matter had been entrusted and who had not been able to accomplish it. He had been converted by God's <term>grace</term> <note>In this context, grace refers to the divine influence that changes a person’s heart and leads them toward a holy life.</note> through our admonitions, and was about to deprive himself of all he had and give his possessions to the poor. But he died upon the way. Nevertheless, God did perform many miracles by means of him, and his tomb is held in reverence.

As I went to <term>Saint Francis</term>, therefore, I prayed by the way. And amongst other prayers, I did ask the **Blessed Francis** that he would implore God for me, that I might serve well his <term>Order</term> <note>The Franciscan Order, or Order of Friars Minor, which emphasizes the imitation of Christ through poverty and humility.</note>, to which I had but lately renewed my promises, and that he would obtain for me the grace that I might feel somewhat of Christ, but above all, that He would make me become poor and end my days in poverty. For this cause (namely, to have the liberty of poverty <note>Angela describes poverty as "liberty" because she believed that being free from material ownership allowed for total spiritual freedom.</note>) had I journeyed to Rome, to pray the **Blessed Peter** <note>Saint Peter the Apostle. Angela's trip to Rome to visit the tomb of St. Peter was a significant step in her quest for "the privilege of poverty."</note> that he would obtain for me the <term>grace</term> of true poverty. Through the merits of the **Blessed Peter** and the **Blessed Francis**, the gift of **true poverty** <note>The Franciscan ideal of "Altissima Paupertas," involving the total renunciation of material goods and self-will to follow Christ.</note> was granted to me by divine mercy, just as I had been asking for in prayer as I walked along the way.

Now when I had come to that place which lies between **Spello** <note>A town in the Umbria region of Italy, located on the slope of Mount Subasio near Assisi.</note> and the narrow road which leads upward toward **Assisi**, and is beyond Spello, it was said to me:

> “You have prayed to My servant Francis, and I have not willed to send you another messenger. I am the **Holy Spirit**, who has come to you to bring you such **consolation** <note>In mystical theology, "consolation" refers to an interior sense of God’s presence that brings peace and joy.</note> as you have never before tasted. And I will go with you even to the church of Saint Francis; I shall be within you, and but few of those who are with you will perceive it. I will bear you company and will speak with you all the way; I will make no end to my speaking and you will not be able to attend to anyone except Me, for I have bound you and will not depart from you until you come for the second time to Saint Francis. Then will I depart from you in so far as this present consolation is concerned, but in no other manner will I ever leave you, and you shall love Me.”

None can excuse themselves for not having this love, because it is possible for all persons to love God, and He asketh nothing save that the soul shall love and seek Him. He is the love of the soul. I felt a sweetness so peaceful, quiet, and great that I do not know how to describe it. Because of this, I longed for death; the peace and sweet joy I felt made life a greater grief to me than I can say. I longed for death so that I might reach the delight I was now beginning to feel, and for this reason, I wished to leave this world. Life was a greater sorrow to me than the deaths of my mother and my children—heavier than any other grief I can remember. <note>Angela’s conversion followed the tragic loss of her mother, husband, and children in a short span of time. Here, her spiritual ecstasy is so intense that it outweighs even the most profound human bereavement.</note>

I remained inside my house for eight days, completely weakened. I cried out, "Lord, have mercy on me and do not let me stay in this world any longer." From that time on, I often noticed indescribable scents; but I cannot explain these or the other things I experienced, so great was the sweetness and joy I felt in them. <note>This refers to the "odor of sanctity," a recurring theme in hagiography where divine presence is manifested through pleasant, supernatural fragrances.</note> The voice spoke to me many other times, but never for very long, nor with such sweetness or deep meaning.

After I had returned from Assisi, as I mentioned, and was lying in my house, my companion—who was a person of marvelous simplicity, purity, and virginity—heard a voice saying, "The Holy Spirit is in this room." <note>The "cell" refers to Angela’s private living space. Angela lived as a "tertiary" (a lay member) of the Franciscan order, often in a small, simple domestic setting.</note> Then she came to me and began to look around, asking, "Tell me what is wrong with you, for I have been told three times to come to you." I answered, "I am happy with what you were told." From that time on, I began to share many of these secret things with my companion. <note>This companion is usually identified as Masazuola. She played a crucial role in Angela's life, acting as a witness to her ecstasies and helping to bridge the gap between Angela's private visions and her public life.</note>

### THE SECOND VISION: SHE BEHOLDS GOD AS BEAUTY, MAKING ALL CREATED THINGS SEEM DEFORMED AND HIDEOUS BY COMPARISON

At a certain time when I was at prayer and my spirit was lifted up, God spoke many gracious words full of love to me.

And when I looked, I beheld God who spoke with me. If you seek to know what it was I beheld, I can tell you nothing, except that I beheld a fullness and a radiance. <note>The original terms suggest "plenitudo" (fullness) and "claritas" (brightness/clarity), standard terms in medieval mysticism to describe the overwhelming presence of the divine.</note> I felt these within me so abundantly that I cannot describe it in any way, nor provide any likeness of it. For what I saw was not physical, but as though it were in heaven. Thus, I beheld a beauty so great that I can say nothing about it, except that I saw the **Supreme Beauty** which contains within itself all goodness. All the saints were standing before this beautiful Majesty, praising it.

It seemed to me, however, that I remained in this trance for only a very short time. Then God said to me, "My beloved daughter, dear to Me, all the saints of Paradise bear a special love toward you, as does My mother, and they will bring you to Me." <note>In the tradition of the "Great Visions," the intercession of the Virgin Mary and the Saints is a common theme, representing the "Communion of Saints" supporting the visionary.</note> And although these words were spoken to me, everything concerning His mother and the saints seemed like a small thing to me. For my joy in Him was so great that I took no notice of the angels or the saints; because all <note>Angela describes a state of "perfect union" where the soul is so focused on the Creator that even the highest created beings (the Saints and Mary) do not distract her from the Divine Essence.</note>

<summary>Angela describes a mystical vision of God as the "Supreme Beauty," an experience so intense that it transcends physical description and renders even the saints and the Virgin Mary secondary to the direct presence of God.</summary>
<keywords>Blessed Angela, Foligno, Supreme Beauty, mystical vision, Paradise, Saints, Divine Majesty, trance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between God and the Blessed Angela of Foligno during her mystical vision of Divine Beauty. ->THE BLESSED ANGELA<-

their goodness and all their beauty was from Him and in Him; He was the whole and **Supreme Good** <note>original: "Summum Bonum," a theological term for God as the ultimate source of all that is good.</note>, with all beauty, and so great a joy had I in His words that I paid no heed to any creature.

Again He said unto me, “Infinite is the love which I bear thee, but I do not reveal it unto thee—yea, I do even conceal it.”

Then answered my soul, “Wherefore hast Thou such love and joy in me, who am hateful, inasmuch as I have offended Thee all the days of my life?” <note>Angela often reflects on her life before her conversion in 1285, viewing her former worldly life and attachments as a deep offense against God.</note>

To this did He make answer, “So great is the love I bear thee that I no more remember thy sins, albeit Mine eyes do see them; for in thee have I much treasure.”

Then did my soul feel an assurance so true that it doubted no more. It felt and saw that the eyes of God were searching within it, and it had such joy in those eyes that neither man nor saint come down from heaven could declare it. When He told me that He concealed much love, because I was not able to bear it, my soul answered: “If Thou art **God omnipotent** <term>Omnipotent: From the Latin 'omni' (all) and 'potens' (powerful); the theological concept that God possesses infinite power.</term>, make Thou me able to bear it.”

Then He made answer finally and said: “If I were to do as thou askest, thou wouldst have here all that thou desirest, and wouldst no longer hunger after Me. For this reason will I not grant thy request, for I desire that in this world thou shouldst hunger and long after Me and shouldst ever be eager to find Me.” <note>This concept of "holy hunger" is central to Angela's spirituality; the idea that spiritual satisfaction on earth is withheld so that the soul's desire for the divine continues to grow.</note>

<summary>In this passage, Angela of Foligno describes a dialogue with God where He reveals His immense love for her despite her past sins. God explains that He deliberately limits her experience of His love to maintain her spiritual hunger and longing for Him during her earthly life.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Divine Love, Supreme Good, spiritual hunger, omnipotence, repentance, mysticism, visions</keywords>

### THIRD VISION, WHEREIN SHE BEHELD GOD INASMUCH AS HE IS INVINCIBLE OMNIPOTENCE IN ALL THINGS, WHICH GAVE UNTO HER THE GRACE TO BE OF BENEFIT BOTH TO THE PRESENT AND TO THE FUTURE GENERATIONS. MOREOVER, SHE BEHELD LIKEWISE THE DEEP HUMILITY OF GOD

UPON a certain occasion it was told me, saying, “I who speak with thee am the **divine Power** <note>original: "Potentia Divina." In medieval theology, "Power" was an attribute specifically associated with God the Father.</note>, who bring unto thee divine grace. The grace is this: I desire that thou shouldst be of benefit unto all men who shall behold thee; and not only unto these, but likewise unto all who shall think of thee and remember thee and hear thee spoken of. Nevertheless, thou wilt be of the greatest benefit unto those into whom I have most fully entered.” <note>This suggests that the spiritual impact of Angela's life would be most effective for those who are already open to or filled with God’s presence.</note>

Then, albeit the soul rejoiced greatly, it said, “I desire not this grace, for I fear that it would be hurtful unto me and make me to be **vainglorious**.” <note>Angela expresses a common medieval fear: that receiving public spiritual gifts might lead to the sin of "inanis gloria" (empty pride or vanity).</note>

But the Lord answered, “With this hast thou naught to do, for it concerneth thee not and thou hast only the **care** <term>Care: From the Latin 'cura,' meaning stewardship or the responsibility of looking after something that belongs to another.</term> thereof. See that thou servest it well and renderest it again unto Him to whom it belongeth.”

Then my soul saw and understood that in this way it could be neither perturbed nor hurt. And the Lord said also unto me, “Much am I pleased by thy great fear.”

After this I went into the church, and there did the Lord speak most sweetly and graciously unto me, whereat all my mind did greatly rejoice and take comfort. He—

<summary>In this vision, God reveals Himself as Divine Power and informs Angela that her spiritual life is intended to serve as a grace for all people. Despite Angela's fear that such a public role might lead to pride, God reassures her that she is merely a steward of this gift.</summary>
<keywords>Third Vision, Divine Power, Divine Grace, humility, vainglory, stewardship, spiritual benefit, Foligno</keywords>

Having been told that her experiences are intended for the benefit of others, Angela now receives a direct revelation of God's absolute power and surprising humility, which leads to a profound sense of her own spiritual insignificance.</meta>

said, “My beloved daughter,” and many other things better still, and added, “No creature can console thee, only I alone, who desire to reveal My power unto thee.”

And immediately the eyes of my soul were opened and I beheld the **plenitude** <term>Plenitude: From the Latin 'plenitudo,' meaning the absolute fullness or completeness of God’s presence which fills all of existence.</term> of God, whereby I did comprehend the whole world, both here and beyond the sea, and the **abyss** <note>In mystical literature, the 'abyss' refers to the unfathomable depths of both the created universe and the divine essence.</note> and all things else; and therein did I behold naught save the **divine power** in a manner assuredly indescribable, so that through excess of marvelling the soul cried with a loud voice, saying, “This whole world is full of God!” Wherefore did I now comprehend that the world is but a small thing; I saw, moreover, that the power of God was above all things, and that the whole world was filled with it.

Then said He unto me, “I have shown thee something of My power,” the which I did so well understand that it enabled me better to understand all other things. He said also, “I have made thee to see something of My power; behold now and see Mine **humility**.” <note>For Angela and other Franciscan mystics, the 'humility of God' was a profound concept, focusing on God's willingness to lower Himself to the level of humanity, most notably through the Incarnation.</note> Then was I given so deep an insight into the humility of God towards man and all other things that when my soul remembered His unspeakable power and comprehended His deep humility, it marvelled greatly and did esteem itself to be nothing at all, for in itself it beheld nothing save pride.

Thus did I begin to think within myself and to esteem myself in every way unworthy of the **Holy Communion** <note>To 'communicate' means to receive the Eucharist. Angela’s reaction is a common theme in hagiography, where a vision of God’s holiness causes the saint to feel too sinful to participate in the sacrament.</note>, wherefore did I refuse to communicate.

Then was I filled with unspeakable sweetness and great joy, which I do think I shall never lose all the days of my life.

### FOURTH VISION AND CONSOLATION, WHEREIN SHE BEHELD GOD INASMUCH AS HE IS SUPREME WISDOM, WHEREBY SHE DID LEARN TO JUDGE OF ALL THINGS WITHOUT ERROR

**A MAN** did once ask of me that I would pray God for certain things which he was desirous of knowing. But I doubted whether I should do this, for I held it to be an act of pride and great folly to pray unto God for such things. In mystical theology, it refers to the infinite nature of God that fills all things.</term> of divine wisdom, in which is all goodness.

From the time when I beheld this vision, it has been given to me to understand and judge all spiritual persons and things when I hear them spoken of. > “Why, O Lord, did You create man, and why, after having created him, did You permit so much suffering to be laid upon You <note>The 'suffering laid upon' God refers to the Passion of Christ, which Angela views as God taking on the weight of human sin.</note>—as was borne by Your Son because of our sins? Following this, I was given to understand that the reason why God had done and permitted this was so that His goodness could be more clearly revealed to us, and also because this path was more suited to our human needs. Yet this explanation did not fully satisfy my understanding, because I knew with certainty that God could have acted in another way if He had wished to do so.

At another time, when I was exalted in spirit <term>Exalted in spirit: A state of mystical ecstasy or 'elevation' where the soul is lifted beyond its normal sensory perceptions to communicate with the divine.</term>, I perceived that this inquiry had neither beginning nor end. Being in this state of uncertainty and anxiety, I was lifted even higher and enlightened so that I might behold the ineffable <note>original: "ineffabilis," meaning that which is beyond the power of words to describe.</note> power of God.

In this state, I saw the will, the justice, and the goodness of God. Within these virtues, I clearly beheld the answer to what I had sought to know. Thus was my soul brought out of the darkness in which it lay while upon the earth; for in

By this illumination, my soul was raised up straight and I stood upon my feet, even upon the tips of my toes, in such bodily agility and renewed life as I had never hitherto experienced. <note>Mystics often described physical sensations accompanying their visions; here, the "light" or "illumination" gives Angela a feeling of weightlessness or supernatural energy.</note> Moreover, there came upon me such a fullness of charity <term>Charity: From the Latin 'caritas,' meaning the highest form of Christian love—a selfless, divine love for God and others.</term>, and with so great a joy did I understand that power, will, and justice of God, that not only was I satisfied concerning the questions I had asked, but likewise concerning all creatures, even the demons and the damned, for I felt I was called to save them one and all. <note>This is a profound moment of "universalism," where Angela feels a divine impulse for the salvation of all beings, reflecting her total immersion in God's love.</note>

From that time onwards, I did feel myself so contented and safe that if I had known of a certainty that I was to be damned, I should on no account have bewailed myself, nor should I for this reason have striven and studied less to worship and honor God than I had done before, so clearly had I understood His justice and the righteousness of His judgments. <note>Angela expresses a state of "holy indifference," where her love for God's justice is so great that she would accept even her own damnation if it were God's righteous will.</note> Wherefore was my soul filled with so great a peace, quietness, and firmness that never do I remember any other so complete; and herein have I continued.

I saw something as fixed and stable as it was indescribable; and I can say no more than what I have already said many times: that it was entirely good.

And although my soul did not see "love" itself, nevertheless when it saw that indescribable thing, it was filled with an equally indescribable joy. It was taken out of its previous state and placed into this most great and unutterable condition. I do not know if I was in my body or outside of it at that time, but it is enough to say that all my other visions seemed lesser than this one. In this vision, I was granted the "mortification" of sins <term>Mortification: From the Latin 'mortificatio,' meaning the 'putting to death' of sinful desires or the ego to live more fully in God.</term> and the assurance of virtue. Because of this, I now love all things—both the good and the bad, things well-made and those made poorly—for I no longer look down on these things.

I was, therefore, left in a state of great peace and reverence for the heavenly judgments. Now, when I cry out, "By Your judgments, or by Your judgment deliver me, O Lord," I say it with as much joy and trust as if I were saying, "By Your Passion <term>Passion: The period of suffering and the crucifixion of Jesus Christ.</term> deliver me, O Lord." The reason for this is that I cannot see the goodness of God any more clearly in a saint or a holy person (or even many of them) than I can in a single condemned person or a multitude of the damned. <note>This is one of Angela's most radical mystical insights: she perceives God's perfection and "goodness" even in the act of divine justice and the existence of hell, seeing the entire cosmic order as a manifestation of His nature.</note> Although this profound truth was shown to me only once, it has never left my memory, nor have I lost the joy it brought me. Even if it were possible for everything belonging to my faith to fail me, there would still remain within me the true certainty of the supreme God, of His perfect justice, and of His judgments.

### SIXTH VISION AND CONSOLATION, IN WHICH SHE BEHELD GOD AS LOVE, THROUGH WHICH SHE WAS TRANSFORMED IN DIVINE LOVE

I thought <note>original: "Methought"</note> one time during Lent that I was extremely "dry" and lacking in devotion; for this reason, I prayed to God that He would give me something of Himself, because all goodness seemed to have dried up within me. To me there seemed only a continuation and eternity of it, such that I can describe neither its likeness nor its color; but the moment this love reached me, I beheld all these things more clearly with the eyes of the soul than I could ever do with the eyes of the body.

This love came toward me in the shape <note>original: "after the manner of"</note> of a sickle. <term>Sickle: A curved hand tool used for harvesting grain. ### SEVENTH VISION, WHEREIN SHE BEHELD GOD IN THREE PERSONS, BUT SHE BEHELD HIM DARKLY; AND THIS VISION DID INSPIRE HER WITH PERFECT AND HOLY HOPE AND FULL ASSURANCE

THERE was a time when my soul was exalted to behold God with so much clearness that never before had I <note>The "darkly" (original: *in caligine*) mentioned in the title refers to a famous concept in mystical theology called the "Divine Darkness." It suggests that God is so far beyond human understanding that He appears like a dark cloud or a blinding light that the mind cannot process.</note>

<meta>This page continues Angela's account of her transition from a focus on the physical suffering of Christ (the Passion) to a higher, more abstract mystical state. On the previous page, she described a state of "quiet" where her personal will was so aligned with God's that she felt love even for "toads and serpents" and could no longer feel the pain of Christ’s suffering as she once did.</meta>

-> THE BLESSED ANGELA <-

-> 182 <-

...beheld Him so distinctly. But I did not see love here so fully; rather, I lost what I had before and was left without love. Afterward, I saw Him darkly, and this <term>darkness</term> <note>In mystical theology, "Divine Darkness" (or *caligo*) refers to a state where God is experienced as being so far beyond human language and thought that He appears as a "darkness" or "nothingness" to the mind. This is not a lack of light, but an excess of it that blinds the intellect.</note> was the greatest blessing that could be imagined, and no thought could conceive of anything that would equal this.

Then, the soul was given an assured faith and a firm, certain hope, in which I felt so sure of God that all fear left me. For by that blessing which came with the darkness, I gathered my thoughts and was made so certain of God that I can never again doubt that I truly possess Him. Thus, my hope is now made certain, for now I see so clearly that what I see can neither be spoken with the mouth nor imagined in the heart. And by that blessing (which is most certain and includes that darkness), I have attained all my hope; and because I now see clearly, I have all that I desired to have or to know.

In this state, likewise, I see <term>all Good</term> <note>The "Good" (or *Summum Bonum* in Latin) is a philosophical and theological term for God as the ultimate source of all perfection and morality.</note>; and seeing it, the soul cannot think that it will depart from it, or it from the Good, or that in the future it must ever leave the Good. The soul delights unspeakably in this, yet it sees nothing that can be described by the tongue or imagined in the heart. It sees nothing, yet sees all things, because it beholds this Good darkly—and the more darkly and secretly the Good is seen, the more certain it is, and more excellent than all things. For this reason, every other good which can be seen or imagined is undoubtedly less than...

<summary>Angela describes her experience of the "Divine Darkness," a mystical state beyond emotion or intellect where she finds absolute certainty of God’s presence and the possession of the "Ultimate Good." This state provides a level of faith and hope that surpasses anything that can be expressed in words or visualized in the mind.</summary>

But seen thus darkly, the Good brings no smile to the lips, no fervor or devotion or love into the heart, for the body does not tremble or become moved or distressed as it does at other times. And the cause of this is that the soul sees, and not the body, which rests and sleeps, and the tongue is made dumb and cannot speak. All the many and unspeakable kindnesses which God has shown to me, all the sweet words and all other divine sayings and doings are so much less than this which I have seen clearly through the darkness, that I put no hope in them. Yes, even if it were possible that all these were not true, it would in no way lessen the hope which I have in this and all other good.

### EIGHTH VISION AND CONSOLATION, WHEREIN SHE BEHELD GOD AS CLEARLY AS IS POSSIBLE IN THIS LIFE, IN THE WHICH VISION SHE ACQUIRED STRENGTH IN GOOD INTENTIONS AND IN THE PERFECT DELIGHT IN GOD

Being thus exalted in spirit during the time of **Lent**, I was joined with God in a manner different from what I had usually experienced. It seemed to me that I was in the midst of the **<term>Trinity</term>** <note>The Christian concept of God as three persons in one essence: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Angela describes this not as an idea, but as a physical and spiritual location where she "stood."</note> in a way that was higher and greater than usual. The blessings I received were greater than any before, and I was continually given gifts full of delight and a joy so great it cannot be described. All of this was so far beyond anything that had happened to me previously that truly a **divine change** took place in my soul—a transformation that neither saint nor angel could find the words to describe.

Through these divine blessings and actions performed in my soul, God first presented Himself inexpressibly through His works. Then, He manifested and revealed Himself fully to my soul, giving it great gifts with a clarity and certainty that cannot be described.

And when I am uplifted in this way, I understand the whole world and believe myself to be in heaven with God instead of on the earth. This state is far more excellent than any other I have experienced; it is so full of satisfaction, so clear, ennobling, and expansive that I never felt any other state that could compare to it.

***

### NINTH VISION, WHEREIN IT WAS CERTIFIED UNTO HER THAT IN HER VISIONS AND HEAVENLY CONVERSATIONS SHE HAD NOT BEEN DECEIVED

U**PON** a certain occasion, at the <term>Feast of the Blessed Virgin Mary</term> <note>Likely one of the major liturgical feasts dedicated to Mary, such as the Assumption or the Nativity of the Virgin.</note>, some little while after my <term>conversion</term> <note>Angela's "conversion" refers to her transformative shift from a worldly life to one of extreme penance and mystical pursuit, which began around 1285.</note>, I did pray the Blessed Virgin that she would implore her Son to grant me grace whereby I might know that I had not been deceived.

<summary>Angela describes the soul’s inseparable union with God and explains that even the greatest earthly mystical experience is but a shadow of the joy held by the saints in heaven. She begins a new vision centered on seeking divine assurance that her revelations are authentic.</summary>

<keywords>divine union, grace, eternal life, saints, Ninth Vision, Blessed Virgin Mary, spiritual certainty, conversion</keywords>

grace that I might be certain that I had not been deceived in the conversations which had been held with me. Then came unto me a heavenly voice promising me that my request should be granted, and saying further : 

> “ God hath shown Himself unto thee, hath spoken with thee, and hath endowed thee with understanding of Himself ; take heed, therefore, that thou neither speakest, beholdest, nor hearkenest unto anything whatsoever save according unto His will.” 

I perceived that these things were said unto me with much discretion and ripe wisdom. The aforesaid conversations had left me with great joy and the hope of obtaining that for which I had asked, and the foregoing words did, moreover, tell me that God would grant the grace that everything I did should be done with His permission.

I began, therefore, to do the three things which had been told unto me. Accordingly my heart was uplifted from all things earthly and fixed on God. And nothing that I did, or ate, or spake prevented my heart from being always fixed on God ; neither could I see, or think, or feel, save according unto God’s will. When I had been engaged in prayer and desired to go and eat and drink, I did humbly ask permission, and He answered, 

> “ Go and eat with the blessing of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.” 

And thus did He give me permission, sometimes immediately and sometimes later.

This lasted three days and three nights. Finally I beheld God in spirit during Mass, at about the time of the <term>elevation of the Body of Christ</term> <note>The "elevation" occurs during the Catholic Mass when the priest raises the consecrated Host (the bread) for the congregation to see and adore, marking the moment the bread is believed to become the actual Body of Christ.</note>. ### TENTH VISION AND CONSOLATION, WHEREIN IT WAS FURTHER CERTIFIED UNTO HER THAT SHE HAD NOT BEEN DECEIVED IN HER CONVERSATIONS

UPON another occasion whilst I was at prayer, exceeding pleasant words were spoken unto me after this manner :
> “ Oh my daughter, who art far sweeter unto Me than I am unto thee ; thou art the temple of My delight, and the heart of the <term>Omnipotent God</term> <note>Latin: *Omnipotens Deus*; the All-Powerful God.</note> resteth upon thy heart.”

Together with these words there came upon me a feeling of the utmost joy, such as I had never before experienced, inasmuch as all the members of my body felt it. <note>This highlights the "somatic" or physical nature of Angela’s mysticism, where spiritual ecstasy manifests as tangible physical sensations.</note> And as I did prostrate myself at these words, it was further told me :
> “ The Omnipotent God loveth thee more dearly than any other woman of this city <note>Referring to Foligno, Angela's home in Umbria, Italy.</note>. He rejoiceth in thee and in thy companion <note>Likely referring to Brother A., her confessor and scribe, or perhaps a fellow member of the Third Order of St. Francis who assisted her.</note>. Do ye both strive, therefore, that your lives be as a light unto all who desire to follow your

My soul did here understand that this cruel judgment was pronounced against the learned <note>The "learned" refers to scholars and theologians who studied the Bible intellectually but often lacked the personal, mystical experience of God that Angela describes.</note> rather than against the laity, because they do despise these heavenly things by reason of knowing them in the Scriptures. And I was told that His eyes were now upon us; whereupon methought that I beheld His divine eyes with the eyes of my mind, and I rejoiced more than I can say. Nevertheless, I do grieve because the words we are now saying are so unworthy. <note>Angela frequently expresses "ineffability"—the belief that human language is too poor and limited to describe the reality of divine encounters.</note>

Albeit I had great joy of this matter, yet did I remember my sins and I did esteem that neither now or at any time had there been in me any good which might be pleasing unto God. Wherefore began I to doubt, seeing that great things had been spoken unto me; and I said:

> “If Thou who speakest unto me wert truly the Son of Almighty God, my soul would feel a joy higher and greater than this, and I should not be able to bear it, feeling that Thou wert in me, who am so unworthy.”

> Unto this He made answer, “I desire not that thou shouldst have a greater or more perfect joy than this at the present time, but I have prepared a greater one for thee. Thou must know that the whole world is full of Me.”

<summary>Angela reflects on a divine warning that her life will serve as a judgment against intellectual scholars who lack true spiritual feeling. And truly, I perceived then that every created thing was full of Him. <note>This refers to a common mystical experience where the visionary sees the presence of God permeating all of creation, often called "panentheism."</note> Again He spoke to me, saying, “I can do all things; I can make you see Me as when I talked with My disciples and yet not feel Me.”

This was not said to me in actual words, but my soul understood what He said, and many things greater still, and so it felt them to be true. Yet in order to be certain whether what was said was truly true, my soul cried out:

> “Since You are <term>Almighty God</term> <note>original: *Omnipotens Deus*</note> and the things You tell me are true, give me a sign so that I may be sure of them, and release me from this great doubt.”

Then I begged Him that He would give me some tangible sign, something which I could see; such as putting a candle into my hand, or a precious stone, or some other thing, or that He would give me any sign He pleased, promising Him that I would show it to no person except those whom He should desire. <note>In the Middle Ages, mystics often sought "discretio spirituum" or the discernment of spirits. Angela is asking for a physical miracle to prove her visions are from God and not a delusion or a trick of the devil.</note> Then He replied:

> “This sign that you seek is one that would only give you great joy when you looked at or touched it, but it would not free you from doubt, and you might even be deceived by that sign. Therefore will I give you another sign, better than the one you seek, and which will be forever with you, and in your soul you shall always feel it. The sign shall be this: you shall be ever fervent in love, and the love and the enlightened knowledge—" <note>The text breaks off here as the "enlightened knowledge" likely refers to the "donum intellectus" or the gift of understanding often described by Angela as a burning internal heat or light.</note>

...knowledge of God shall be ever with you and in you. This shall be a certain sign to you that I am He, because none except me can do this. And this is a sign which I will leave in your soul, which is better for you than that which you asked of me. My love I leave in you, so that for love of me you will endure <term>tribulations</term> <note>In this context, "tribulations" refers to trials, suffering, or persecutions that test a person's faith.</note>, and if any person speaks or does evil to you, you will be grateful, declaring yourself unworthy of so much mercy. Such is the love which I bore for you all, for whose sake I patiently and humbly endured all things. Thus you shall know whether or not I am in you if, when any person shall speak or do evil to you, you are not only patient, but even desirous that they should hurt you and be grateful to them. And this is a certain sign of the grace of God. And behold, I do now anoint you with an <term>ointment</term> <note>original: *unguentum*. In medieval mysticism, an "ointment" or "unction" is a spiritual sensation of divine grace that often feels like a physical soothing or a sweet fragrance to the visionary.</note> wherewith a saint called Siricus <note>Likely Saint Cyriacus (also known as Siricus), a 4th-century Roman martyr who, according to legend, was tortured and killed for his faith. His mention serves to link Angela's spiritual experience to the endurance of the early Christian martyrs.</note> and many other saints were anointed.”

Then I immediately felt that ointment, and it was so sweet that I longed for death, and that I might die with all manner of bodily torments. The torments suffered by the martyrs who had died for Christ I esteemed as nothing, and I desired that for love of Him my torments should be more terrible than theirs, and that the world should cry out against me with insults and revilings.

Moreover, I rejoiced greatly in praying for those who might work me these evils, and I did not marvel at the saints who prayed for their murderers and persecutors;

<summary>God grants Angela an internal sign of His presence—the gift of divine love—and anoints her with a spiritual sweetness that inspires in her a profound desire to suffer and even die for Christ.
