Because of this, I was very ready to pray for those who did me evil, to love them with a great love, and to have compassion for them. In that <term>anointing</term> <note>original: *unguentum*. A spiritual sensation of divine grace, often described by mystics as a soothing or fragrant "oil" poured into the soul to heal and strengthen it.</note> I felt such sweetness both within and without that I had never felt anything like it before, and I have no words to express even the smallest part of it.

This <term>consolation</term> <note>In the context of Christian mysticism, a "consolation" is a gift of divine joy, peace, or emotional relief that God grants to a believer during prayer or contemplation.</note> was different, and of a nature unlike the others. In other experiences, I had desired to leave this world immediately, but in this one, my desire was that my death should be painful and prolonged, with every kind of torment, and that my <term>members</term> <note>A traditional term for the limbs or parts of the physical body.</note> should suffer all the tortures of the world. Yet all this seemed like a small thing to me, for my soul knew well that every torment was but a small thing in comparison with the blessings promised in the eternal life. My soul was certain of this truth; even if all the wise men in the world had told me the opposite, I would not have believed them. And if I were to swear that everyone who walked upon this path would be saved, I would believe that I was speaking the truth.

This sign did God leave so firmly implanted in my soul, with such a bright and clear light, that it seems to me I could endure any <term>martyrdom</term> <note>The suffering of death on account of adherence to a cause and especially to one's religious faith.</note>. This sign, moreover, leads one continually upon the straight path of salvation—that is to say, it leads to love and the desire to suffer for the love of God.

# HERE BEGIN THE CONSOLATIONS WHICH SHE HAD WHEN THINKING UPON THE PASSION OF CHRIST

## FIRST CONSOLATION OF THE PASSION OF CHRIST

As I was once meditating upon the **Passion** <note>The "Passion" refers to the period of intense spiritual and physical suffering of Jesus Christ leading up to and including his death on the cross.</note> and the poverty of the **incarnate** <term>incarnate</term> <note>From the Latin *incarnatus*, meaning "made flesh"; the theological belief that the divine Son of God took on a human body and nature.</note> Son of God, Christ appeared to me and showed me His great **poverty**. He intended to place this poverty into my heart so that I might clearly see it and reflect on it diligently. I likewise beheld those for whose sake He had become poor, and I felt such great grief and aversion for them <note>Angela likely refers here to humanity in its sinful state, whose failings necessitated Christ's sacrifice.</note> that my heart almost failed me.

<summary>Angela receives a divine command to conclude her writings with a spirit of gratitude and a focus on the Cross, before beginning a new series of reflections on the spiritual "consolations" found in Christ's poverty and suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Passion of Christ, Consolation, Poverty of Christ, the Cross, spiritual vision, mystical experience, Deo gratias</keywords>

After this, He did show His **Passion** <note>The "Passion" refers to the period of intense spiritual and physical suffering of Jesus Christ leading up to and including his death on the cross.</note> unto me more fully. Then I beheld Him poor of kindred and of friends, and moreover so poor of Himself that, having regard unto His **humanity** <term>humanity</term> <note>In Christian theology, this refers to Christ's human nature, emphasizing that He felt physical pain and emotional abandonment just as any person would.</note>, He was not able to help Himself.

Again after this it pleased Him to make me understand more things concerning His Passion than I had ever hitherto heard related. Then did I understand how in His Passion Christ saw that the hearts of those wicked and obstinate men were all turned against Him; He saw how all the members <note>The individuals involved in the conspiracy to execute Him.</note> carefully destroyed His name, and how they used great attention and diligence to destroy it speedily; He saw the subtlety, the cunning **machinations** <note>Cunning or devious plots and schemes.</note> with which they conspired against Him, and He saw their evil designs, their slanders, rage, and fury, and the plans and preparations which they had made in order that they might most cruelly afflict Him. Of a certainty, the cruelty of the Passion was very great, inasmuch as He foresaw all the pain, the injury and shame.

My soul beheld far more of the Passion than I have will to say, yea, rather will I keep silence. Therefore did my soul then cry aloud, saying:

> “**Holy Mary**, mother of Him who is thus afflicted, give me something of this Passion of the Son of God, for thou hast seen more of it than hath any other saint. Thou hast seen Him with the eyes both of thy body and thy mind, and most intently hast thou observed Him, because thou hast loved Him more than all.” And my soul cried again, saying, “Is there here any saint who can tell

reasons for that bitter suffering. Firstly, because that soul was most holy and entirely without sin, for which reason it deserved no punishment whatsoever. Then, because He endured it for love of us, and we, ungrateful, hostile, and unworthy, did despise and mock at Him even whilst He was redeeming us through His **Passion**. <note>The Passion refers to the final period of Jesus’s life, encompassing his trial, suffering, and death on the cross.</note> Then, because the sin of those who crucified Him was exceeding great, and that Soul which did hate and utterly abhor all sin grieved more over this than over any other sin.

And finally, He suffered above all things because that most noble soul was dolefully assailed upon every side ; every person was unto Him a cause of grief ; His Soul felt pain and anguish from all the pain and anguish inflicted upon that most holy, virginal, and delicate Body. And all these things together were laid upon that one most holy Soul.

### FOURTH CONSOLATION OF THE PASSION OF CHRIST

**U**PON the fourth day of the <term>great week</term> <note>The "great week" is a traditional term for Holy Week, the final week of Lent leading up to Easter Sunday.</note> I was meditating with grief upon the death of the Son of God, striving to empty my mind of all other things in order that my soul might be the more absorbed in this Passion and Death.

Being, therefore, wholly occupied with the endeavour and desire to cast out every other matter from my mind in order that I might the more speedily and completely think only upon this, I heard the divine voice saying within my soul, “My love for thee was no deceit.” <note>original: "Non cum ioco te amavi." This is one of the most famous lines in Angela's writings; it conveys that God's love is not a game or a trivial thing, but a commitment proven through the reality of the Crucifixion.</note> This word was as a shock of mortal pain unto my soul, for the eyes of my mind were instantly opened, and I saw that what He said was very true. effect of that delight; I saw all that the Son of God had done for the sake of this love, and I saw what <term>Christ Crucified</term> <note>This term refers to the representation of Jesus during his execution on the cross, a central image in medieval devotion used to focus the mind on his physical and emotional suffering.</note> had borne in life and in death for the sake of this deep and unspeakable love. ### FIFTH CONSOLATION OF THE PASSION OF OUR LORD

Upon a certain occasion when I was in the church of the Blessed Francis <note>This likely refers to the Basilica of Saint Francis in Assisi or a local church in Foligno dedicated to the saint, where Angela frequently experienced her visions.</note>, and it was near the time of the <term>elevation of the Body of our Lord</term> <note>The most solemn moment of the Catholic Mass when the priest raises the consecrated bread, which is believed to have become the actual body of Christ, for the congregation to adore.</note>, while the organ was playing the angelic hymn, “Holy, holy, holy,” <note>The *Sanctus*, a prayer from the liturgy based on the vision of the prophet Isaiah.</note> my soul was caught up in that <term>Light uncreate</term> <note>A theological term for the divine, eternal light of God that is not part of the physical, "created" world.</note>, and there it was so rapt and absorbed in a way that is completely impossible to describe.

After this (while I was still in that first state of ecstasy and attraction), there appeared to me the figure of the Blessed Christ Crucified, as though He had only just then been taken down from the Cross; His blood was as fresh and red and flowed from His wounds as easily as though it had only that instant been shed. Moreover, there appeared such a <term>dissolution</term> <note>A literal coming apart or unravelling of the physical form.</note> of all the members of that blessed body that the sinews and joints of the bones seemed to me as though they were loosened from their proper harmony—that is to say, from their rightful position in the body. This was caused by the hard and cruel stretching and the horrible ill-treatment of those <term>virgin limbs</term> <note>In medieval devotion, "virgin limbs" refers to the purity and sinlessness of Christ's body, emphasizing the injustice of His torture.</note> at the hands of wicked men upon the torturing Cross; nevertheless, the skin was nowhere broken.

### SIXTH CONSOLATION OF THE PASSION OF OUR LORD 
### JESUS CHRIST

ANOTHER time when I was praying and meditating with great grief and compassion in mine heart upon the Passion of our Lord Jesus Christ, I did bethink me over and over again of how great was the iniquity of all my sins, seeing that before God could be appeased or I could be reconciled unto Him, or obtain remission <term>remission</term> <note>The formal forgiveness or "sending away" of the guilt and punishment due to sin.</note> of those sins, it was needful that the Son of God should not only pray with tears and supplication, but that He should die for this upon the Cross.

<summary>Angela concludes her vision of Christ's speech regarding His faithful companions and begins a new meditation on the gravity of sin, realizing that only the death of the Son of God could achieve reconciliation with the Father.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, Passion of Christ, Consolation, Mercy, Last Judgment, Remission of Sins, Crucifixion, Mysticism</keywords>

I reflected, moreover, upon how complete must be the damnation, how great the infinite miseries and countless torments which awaited me for each **mortal sin** <term>mortal sin</term> <note>In Catholic theology, a "mortal" or deadly sin is a grave action committed with full knowledge and intent that separates the person from God's grace.</note> which I had committed. In this context, it refers to the theological "debt" created by sin that Angela believes only Christ could pay.</note> for them, it was necessary that no mere angel or archangel, but God Himself—Jesus Christ, the Son of God—should suffer for me and endure the misery of death and the tortures of the Cross.

I also reflected upon my own ingratitude. I realized that not only do I do nothing in return for such a great benefit, but I even offend Him every day, refusing to remember the gift of His redemption or to work together with Him for my salvation through **penance** <term>penance</term> <note>Acts of repentance or self-discipline performed to show sorrow for sin and to resolve to do better.</note>. I was amazed by these things—by the infinite goodness and mercy of God and my own great wickedness and foolishness. As I thought upon this, it was shown to me how we are liberated by the **Passion** of Christ from all the sins, torments, and punishments we have deserved; this was shown to me so clearly that I could hardly restrain myself from crying out loud in front of everyone.

Thus was it told to me by the Lord Jesus Christ when He appeared to me: because He had been hanged for us upon the Cross, no man or person whatsoever could have an excuse if they were not saved. I was hung naked upon the Cross, and vile men stripped off My tunic and My vest and cast dice for them before Mine eyes. And, naked as when I was born of the Virgin, in the cold, the wind, and the air, I was exposed and stretched out on high in the sight of all men and women, in order that I might be the more easily seen and mocked at and might suffer the greater shame.

<summary>Christ describes how his physical suffering on the Cross atones for specific human sins of the feet and body, such as dancing, vanity in clothing, and seeking physical comfort. ### OF FOLIGNO

> "Because of the sin of your wealth—through which you have done evil by acquiring it, spending it wrongly, and hoarding it—I chose to be poor. I possessed no palace, no house, nor even a hut where I might be born or live during My lifetime. Even in death, I would have had no **sepulchre** <note>A stone tomb. Because Jesus owned no property, he was buried in a tomb provided by a follower, Joseph of Arimathea; Angela emphasizes that without this charity, his body would have been abandoned.</note> to rest in—but would have been left as prey for dogs and birds—if someone had not been moved by compassion for My misery to receive Me into his own tomb. I have given My blood and My life for perverse and sinful people; I have kept nothing for Myself. In both life and death, I desired to be and remain poor forever.

> "But why should I say more? No matter how you describe it, you cannot find any sin or any disease of the soul for which I have not provided the true **medicine** <note>In medieval theology, Christ was often called the "Divine Physician" (*Christus Medicus*), whose suffering acts as the medicinal cure for the "sickness" of human sin.</note> and offered enough satisfaction for all sinners. I have paid the price for all the infinite torments and painful suffering that a wretched soul would otherwise deserve to endure in hell. If you stop being neglectful, you no longer need to grieve—provided that you suffer here with Me, always have compassion for Me, and remain My companion in poverty, **ignominy** <term>ignominy: public shame, disgrace, or loss of reputation</term>, and contempt for as long as you live. Mary Magdalene <note>For Angela and her contemporaries, Mary Magdalene was the ultimate model of the "penitent" sinner. Her "sickness" was her past life of sin, and her "healing" came through her intense, emotional devotion to Christ.</note>, who was sick, experienced all these things and desired to be completely freed from them. This is why she was healed of all her infirmities—and in the same way, anyone who experiences these things can find spiritual health just as she did."

The Crucified One spoke further to me and said:

> "When these My sons, who through sin have departed...

And this He does because of the boundless love which He bears them, and because of the pity which He has for their wretchedness; also because they do grieve that they have offended so great a Majesty and do know themselves to be worthy of hell.

After this the Lord spoke to me again and said:
> “Whosoever desires to find grace must not lift his eyes from the Cross, whether I do grant and permit him to live in joy, or whether he live in sorrow.”

### SEVENTH CONSOLATION OF THE PASSION OF OUR LORD
### JESUS CHRIST

On another occasion, as I was gazing at the Cross with the **Crucified** <note>The "Crucified" refers specifically to the physical figure of Jesus on the cross.</note>, and was looking at the Crucified with my bodily eyes, such a fervent love was suddenly kindled in my soul that even the members of my body felt it with great joy and delight. I saw and felt that Christ embraced my soul with the arm wherewith He was crucified,

# OF FOLIGNO

wherefore I rejoiced with a joy greater than I had ever had before.

From this time forth there hath remained unto me a certain joy and clear enlightenment, whereby the soul knoweth and understandeth how it is that we see our flesh made one company <note>The word "company" here refers to a deep, mystical communion or fellowship between human nature and the Divine.</note> with God. And this true delight of the soul was more unspeakable, this joy was more constant and this enlightenment was clearer than I had ever had before. There remained unto me, therefore, so complete an assurance and certainty of my state that I had no more doubt whatsoever but that this state is most certainly from God, and likewise that the admonitions or words <note>These "words" refer to internal locutions—a mystical phenomenon where a person perceives a voice speaking to their soul.</note> which I heard within me are also from God. And I marvelled how hitherto I could possibly have doubted this. And so entirely certain was I of this state that I could not have believed otherwise even if all the men in the world had told me the contrary.

Even now, when I am rapt in this vision and embrace, my soul is so filled with joy that I can feel no sadness whatsoever of the Passion, albeit I see that that Hand is wounded. All my joy lieth now in Christ Crucified <note>original: "Christus Crucifixus." This focus on the physical suffering of Jesus was a hallmark of Franciscan spirituality in the 13th century.</note>, and sometimes it seemeth unto my soul that in the aforesaid close embrace it entereth into the side of Christ <note>A common medieval mystical image representing the entry into the Sacred Heart or the very essence of God's love through the spear-wound in Jesus' side.</note>—and the joy which my soul hath of this illumination cannot be described, for it is so great that sometimes I cannot stand upon my feet, but do fall down and lose my speech. <note>This describes a state of "ecstatic aphasia," where the intensity of a spiritual experience causes a temporary loss of physical strength and the ability to speak.</note>

# THE BLESSED ANGELA

### HERE BEGIN THE CONSOLATIONS AND VISIONS WHICH SHE EXPERIENCED CONCERNING THE SACRAMENT OF THE ALTAR <note>original: "Sacramentum Altaris." This is a traditional term for the Eucharist or Holy Communion, referring to the bread and wine consecrated during the Mass.</note>

**The** first vision occurred when, during the celebration of the Mass, I was striving to think devoutly about the humility of God and His supreme goodness—specifically how it pleased Him to come to us in the Sacrament of the Altar. In that moment, I was exalted in spirit <note>This "exaltation" refers to a state of religious ecstasy or a mystical trance where the soul feels lifted above the physical world to perceive spiritual truths.</note> and received a new and clear understanding of how God is present in this Sacrament.

First, I was told that, through His divine power, the body of Christ could be present upon every altar simultaneously. This is a concept that cannot be fully grasped in this earthly life. Although the Scriptures speak much of this power, those who merely read the words understand very little of it. "But those who feel something of Me," said God, "understand more of it; however, neither those who read nor those who feel understand it fully. But the time is coming when you shall understand."

After this, I was enlightened and understood more clearly than ever before—or since—how God comes to be in this Sacrament. I saw Christ approaching with a most beautiful company of beings, and I was struck with wonder at their beauty. Desiring to know who they were, I was told they were the "Thrones" <note>In traditional medieval theology, the "Thrones" (Latin: *throni*) are the third-highest rank of angels. They are considered the carriers of God's throne and symbols of His divine justice.</note>. That company was filled with brilliant light, and

### SECOND VISION OF THE SACRAMENT OF THE ALTAR

The Sacrament appeared in the way I had prayed for—that is to say, poor, suffering, bleeding, crucified, and dead upon the Cross. If I had not seen them myself, I would never have believed that angels were so pleasing, or that they could have filled the soul with so much joy.

Now, while the Mass was being said, there was a priest celebrating who was nearing the time of <term>Communion</term> <note>The part of the Mass where the bread and wine are consumed.</note>. I believed then that the priest was not in a proper spiritual state to receive the Body of Christ, and I prayed, saying, "Grant that he may not be such a person." Instantly, it was answered: "He shall not be this way eternally."

This would I not have related or caused to be written down like the other things had I not been advised there-unto.

### FIFTH VISION OF THE SACRAMENT OF THE ALTAR

**D**URING a certain time of sickness I greatly desired to <term>communicate</term> <note>To receive the Sacrament of the Eucharist (Holy Communion).</note> at the <term>Feast of the Angels</term> <note>Most likely the Feast of St. Michael the Archangel, celebrated on September 29th, a significant day in the liturgical calendar.</note>, and forasmuch as there was no person to bring me the most holy <term>Body of Christ</term> <note>The consecrated bread, which Angela, as a Catholic mystic, believed to be the literal presence of Jesus.</note>, I began to grieve exceedingly.

And behold, I was then suddenly exalted, and I saw before me a great multitude of angels; and they led me unto an altar and said unto me, "This is the altar of the blessed angels." And upon the altar did they show unto my soul Him whom the angels praise, that is to say, they showed Him who is all praise; and they said—

### SIXTH VISION OF THE SACRAMENT OF THE ALTAR

Another time I beheld Christ in the <term>consecrated Host</term> <note>The bread used in the Mass which, through the prayer of the priest, is believed to become the actual Body of Christ.</note> as a Child. He appeared certainly to be a child of twelve years of age, very lordly, as though He held the sceptre and the dominion. <note>The age of twelve is significant in the Gospels as the time Jesus was found teaching in the Temple; in medieval mysticism, it represents the dawning of divine authority.</note> He appeared to hold something in His hand as an emblem of lordship, but I know not what, and although I saw it with my bodily eyes I cannot say what it was that He held in His hand. When I beheld Him I did not kneel down like the others, so great was my delight at beholding Him, but I was much displeased and grieved because the priest replaced the <term>Host</term> <note>From the Latin word 'hostia,' meaning a sacrificial victim.</note> too soon upon the altar.

Verily, His beauty and adornment cannot be described, and so great was my joy at the sight of Him that I do think it will never fade, and there was such certainty with it that I do in no way doubt of the truth thereof. And finally, my delight in that vision was so great that I asked no help of Him and spake neither good nor evil, for I was so absorbed in the delight of that beauty that I knew not what to say.

### SEVENTH VISION OF THE SACRAMENT OF THE ALTAR

Another time whilst Mass was being celebrated I was uplifted in spirit, and I did question the Lord, saying—

> “Behold, Lord, You are in this <term>Sacrament of the Altar</term> <note>Also known as the Eucharist or Holy Communion; the ritual where bread and wine are consecrated.</note>, but tell me where are Your faithful ones?”

Then He opened my understanding and made answer,

> “Where I am, there are My faithful ones with Me.”

Then I perceived that it was so, and I comprehended clearly that in every place where He was, there should I be also. But this "being" is not the being within God, but the being which is outside of Him. <note>Angela is making a theological distinction: the faithful do not become part of God’s own essence (being "within" Him), but they are perfectly united to Him as His creations (being "outside" of Him) wherever He is present.</note> And it is He alone who is in every place and encompasses all things.

I have many times seen the Body of Christ in various forms in this Blessed Sacrament. For sometimes I have seen the throat of Christ more splendid and beautiful than the sun, and by that beauty was it confirmed for me that God Himself was here, seeing that it was incomparably greater than the sun both in beauty and size, which is why it greatly grieves me that I cannot make it known. <note>Visual revelations of specific body parts of Christ, such as the throat or the heart, were common in medieval female mysticism and were used to emphasize the "Real Presence"—the belief that the bread actually becomes the physical body of Jesus.</note> Sometimes I have seen two eyes of great splendor, and so large that I beheld nothing of the <term>Host</term> <note>The thin wafer of unleavened bread used in the Mass.</note> except for the very edge of it.

At the sight of both these things I was refreshed with so much joy that I cannot compare the one with the...

# HERE BEGIN THE VISIONS WHEREIN SHE WAS COMFORTED BY THE BLESSED VIRGIN

## FIRST VISION OF THE BLESSED VIRGIN

On one occasion, my spirit was lifted up <note>This refers to an ecstatic state or mystical "rapture" where the soul feels detached from the physical body to experience divine truths.</note>, even though I was not praying at the time. It was after dinner and I had sat down to meditate, so I was not thinking about such things in any way. Suddenly, my soul was raised up to see and contemplate the Blessed Virgin Mary in glory. Seeing a woman placed in such nobility, glory, and dignity as she was, I was filled with a marvelous delight; the sight produced an immense joy within me. The glorious Virgin was praying for the human race, and her excellence, humanity, and virtue were so great that she was entirely <term>ineffable</term> <note>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words.</note>, which is why my joy was unspeakable.

As I gazed at these things, Jesus Christ suddenly appeared in that place, seated beside her in His <term>glorified humanity</term> <note>The Christian belief that Christ's physical body was perfected and made radiant after his Resurrection.</note>. And as I looked at Him—even though I knew how His flesh had been tormented, torn, insulted, and shamefully crucified, and though I miraculously understood all the heavy suffering, injuries, mockery, and degradation He endured for our sake—I did not feel any grief. Indeed, it is impossible to describe the delight I felt because of it, and my...

<summary>Angela describes a spontaneous mystical vision of the Virgin Mary in heaven followed by the appearance of the glorified Jesus Christ. She emphasizes that in this state, her knowledge of Christ's suffering caused her joy rather than sorrow.</summary>

<margin>OF FOLIGNO</margin>

speech failed me, so that methought I was about to die. I say, moreover, that it was unto me an exceeding great grief that I did not die, so that I might speedily attain unto that unspeakable thing which I beheld.

This vision endured for three days in succession, and I was not hindered from eating, albeit I ate but little. My body did lie down in constant weakness and I spake not at all. Nothing else did hinder me, but when I heard God spoken of I could not contain myself for my immense joy.

### SECOND VISION OF THE BLESSED VIRGIN

WHEN I was in the church of the <term>Brothers Minor</term> <note>The "Little Brothers," better known as the Franciscans, the religious order founded by St. Francis of Assisi.</note> at Foligno <note>A city in the Umbria region of Italy where Angela lived and experienced her visions.</note> upon the morning of the Feast of the Purification of the Blessed Virgin <note>Also known as Candlemas (February 2nd), this feast commemorates the ritual purification of Mary and the presentation of Jesus in the Temple.</note>, a voice spake unto me, saying, "This is the hour wherein the Virgin Mary came into the Temple with her Son." This thing did my soul hear with exceeding great love; thereupon it was exalted and I beheld our Lady entering that place. My soul advanced to meet her with great reverence and love; but because it feared somewhat to approach near unto her, our Lady did greatly reassure it, and holding out her Son towards me, she said, "Oh lover of my Son, take Him!" And with these words she placed her beloved Son in mine arms. And as I gazed upon Him, His eyes appeared to be fast closed as though He were asleep, and He was wrapped in clothes, or rather <term>swaddled</term> <note>The ancient practice of wrapping infants tightly in long strips of cloth to restrict movement and provide warmth.</note>.

Our Lady, who was wearied by walking, then sat her down and made such beautiful, fair, and pleasing gesture (showing how honest and gracious <note>In the medieval sense, "honest" refers to being virtuous or honorable, while "gracious" implies being filled with divine grace.</note> were her manners), that for the sweetness and delight which it had of beholding her my soul was not only unable to look at the Lord (whom I held clasped in mine arms), but it was constrained only to contemplate our Lady herself.

And as I stood thus, the Child remained all naked in mine arms; and opening His eyes He lifted them and looked to me. And at the look of those eyes I did suddenly feel myself filled with so much love that I was altogether overwhelmed. Because from those eyes there issued forth such splendour and fire of love and joy that it is a thing indescribable. After that there appeared suddenly a great and ineffable <term>Majesty</term> <note>Angela uses the word "Majesty" to describe a direct perception of the power and glory of God that transcends human images.</note>, saying—

> “ He who beholdeth Me not small <note>A reference to Christ's humility and vulnerability as an infant.</note> will not behold Me great <note>A reference to Christ's divine power and eternal glory.</note>.” After this He added, “ I am come unto thee and have offered Myself unto thee in order that thou mayest offer thyself unto Me.”

Then did my soul in a manner most marvellous and indescribable offer itself unto the Lord; finally I offered myself wholly, and likewise my children <note>While Angela's biological children had died years earlier, this likely refers to her "spiritual children"—the followers and disciples who looked to her for guidance in the Franciscan tradition.</note> who followed after me perfectly. I offered myself in all things, keeping back nothing whatsoever either of mine own possessions or of theirs, and my soul understood that God had very willingly accepted this offering, receiving it with great readiness. Wherefore I cannot tell how great was the joy, jubilation, and delight which my soul felt when

### HERE BEGIN THE VISIONS WITH WHICH SHE WAS COMFORTED CONCERNING HER CHILDREN WHO SHOULD FOLLOW AFTER CHRIST

#### FIRST VISION CONCERNING HER CHILDREN <note>While Angela’s biological children died shortly after her conversion, she uses the term "children" here to refer to her "spiritual family"—the group of friars and laypeople who followed her teachings.</note>

**BEING** exalted upon one occasion, and drawn and absorbed into the <term>Light uncreate</term> <note>The "Light uncreate" (Latin: *lux increata*) refers to the eternal, divine light of God’s own essence, which is not a created thing like the light of the sun.</note>, I beheld that which cannot be related. And as I remained in that state, there appeared unto me the image of the blessed God made Man, Christ Crucified, as though He had been but then lifted down from the Cross, and His blood issued forth from His wounds as fresh and red as though it were at that very moment first bursting from the new-made wounds. In all His joints, moreover, there appeared such a dissolution of the sinews (caused by the cruel stretching upon the Cross) that the bones seemed all to be loosened from their proper places.

<summary>Angela concludes her eucharistic visions with a blessing from the Virgin Mary and begins a new series of revelations concerning her spiritual disciples. She describes a powerful vision of the "Light uncreate" and a visceral, graphic encounter with the physical suffering of Christ on the Cross.</summary>

At this sight my bowels <note>In medieval language, the "bowels" or "entrails" were considered the seat of deep compassion and intense emotion, rather than just a physical organ.</note> were pierced with anguish and I grieved more than I had ever grieved before. And as I stood thus plunged in grief, there suddenly appeared around the Crucified a multitude of my children <note>Referring again to Angela’s "spiritual family" of followers rather than biological offspring.</note> who were devout and given unto preaching and to following the example of poverty, contempt, and suffering of Christ Crucified. The Blessed Jesus called them unto Himself, and drawing each one unto Him, He embraced him and made him to kiss the wound in His side <note>The Wound in the Side was a central focus of medieval devotion, seen as the entrance to the Heart of Christ and a refuge for the soul.</note>, and clasping him close with His own hands, He laid his head there in that place. And because of the joy born in my soul at this sight, I did forget the aforesaid sorrow.

The degree of my children, however, was different one from another, for some did He press less and some more into His side; some did He place there often and some were wholly absorbed into His side, whence there appeared on their lips the redness of red blood, and the mouths of some were adorned with this blood and their faces all coloured with it, according unto their aforesaid degrees. And bestowing upon each one His liberal blessing, He said—

> “Oh ye blessed sons, discover ye the way of the Cross—that is to say, the way of My poverty, contempt and suffering. For in these times especially do fellow-workers abound, and ye have I most especially elected in order that through you and your preaching and example may be discovered and made manifest My truth which hath hitherto been trodden upon and hidden by the world.”

Then my soul understood that just as my children had been pressed against His side according to their different degrees <note>Angela often uses "degrees" to describe the varying levels of spiritual maturity and devotion among her followers.</note>, these words were also spoken to each person in a different way. It is not possible in any way to describe the immense love that shone from that blessed Face—and especially from the Eyes of God-made-Man, the Lord Jesus Christ—upon these children as He pressed them against His sacred wounds. This love was further revealed in all the signs, words, and blessings <note>original: "benedictions"</note> made and spoken over them.

### SECOND VISION CONCERNING HER CHILDREN

On another occasion, while I was walking in a certain procession, I felt that same unfathomable attraction toward the <term>Word Uncreate</term> <note>The "Word Uncreate" refers to the eternal, divine nature of Christ (the Logos) as the second person of the Trinity, existing before the creation of the world.</note> in the same unspeakable manner mentioned before. I beheld the blessed God, Three in One, dwelling within the minds of my sons and transforming them into Himself in different ways according to their degrees. To see this was truly like being in Paradise! He descended upon them so tenderly and lovingly that I never grew tired of watching them. The blessing that the <term>God Uncreate</term> <note>This term emphasizes God as the eternal source of all being, who was not Himself created by any other power.</note> poured out upon these children was so full, sweet, and heartfelt that it is altogether indescribable. Then I called out to my children, saying:
“Oh my sons, be like a <term>burnt sacrifice</term> <note>Also known as a "holocaust," this refers to an offering completely consumed by fire; in mystical terms, it represents a soul entirely consumed by the fire of God's love.</note>, wholly consumed in both mind and body.”

In the same way, the supreme God-made-man, the <term>Christ Crucified</term> <note>This refers to the image of Jesus on the cross, which served as the central focus of Angela’s intense mystical meditations on divine suffering and love.</note>, was shown to me. > “I am the one who takes away the sins of the world. I have taken your sins away, and they shall never again be <term>imputed</term> <note>In this context, to be "imputed" means to have a fault officially recorded or held against one's spiritual account.</note> to you. For this is the fountain of true and perfect cleansing; this is the price of true redemption; and this is the house of the true dwelling place. Therefore, my children, do not fear to reveal and defend—in both word and deed—this most excellent truth of life and of my way, which is attacked by the perverse and the wicked. For I am always with you, your Helper and Defender.”

Furthermore, both now and at other times, I was shown the cleansing of all my sons <note>Angela refers to her spiritual followers or disciples as her "sons."</note> in three degrees. The first was a special gift of great grace given to some of them so they could joyfully perfect themselves through works of virtue. The second degree was a special gift of great grace and strength that allowed them to easily resist sin. The third and final degree was the perfection of the soul and its transformation into the Crucified Christ. And although great beauty is granted to the soul through all these changes and graces, the first degree is nonetheless a great, pleasing, and most excellent beauty. Likewise, the final degree—

### THIRD CONSOLATION CONCERNING HER CHILDREN

During that same procession, as we were approaching a church of the Blessed Virgin, behold, the Queen of Mercy and Mother of all Grace herself arose. Bowing toward these children in a new and gracious way, she bestowed on them blessings sweeter than all other blessings, kissing them all upon the breast—some more and some less—and some she embraced with her arms as she kissed them. Her charity was so great that—appearing entirely luminous—she seemed to absorb those children into that infinite light within her own breast. Yet it seemed to me that I saw no arms of flesh, but rather a certain miraculous light into which, as has been said, she absorbed them with a most great and tender love.

With so much affection did he then praise these children for their good intentions, comforting them with the promise that they should assuredly make good progress and that he would help them in his own intention, and blessing them so lovingly that he appeared as though he would rend himself <note>To "rend" oneself suggests an intensity of love so powerful it seems to tear at the very soul or being.</note> for love of them.

And as a dead man is not moved either by honor or beauty, so must ye be in all ways unchangeable and outside of all worldly fear; and ye must preach unto others rather for the <term>mortification</term> <note>The spiritual practice of self-denial and "killing off" worldly impulses to strengthen the soul's connection to God.</note> of your own lives than for the sake of making contentious discourse. And I pray that He who hath deigned to ask this thing of you will likewise deign to fulfill His desire in you, through the merits of His most holy Mother, the Virgin Mary.

His best beloved sons. Wherefore, amongst other things, was this example immediately told unto me:

“Let us suppose that there was a man who had many dear friends whom he had bidden unto a feast <note>The "Great Banquet" is a recurring biblical motif (see Matthew 22:1–14), used here to represent the invitation to salvation and spiritual intimacy.</note> prepared with great care and diligence. If it should happen, now, that many of those who were bidden refused to come, dost thou not think that he would grieve for those who came not, seeing that he had abundantly and with great cost prepared the feast, and that he would receive those who did come with great honour? And is there any doubt but that, amongst those who were all joyfully received, there were not some more familiarly loved, whom he placed near unto himself at table, eating with them out of the same dish and drinking with them from the same cup?”

With great joy in my soul I then asked: “Oh Lord, when Thou biddest all, do Thou tell me more fully of the difference.”

And He answered: “I have bidden all unto the life eternal and preparations have been made for all, and none can excuse himself for that he hath not been asked. But of all who were bidden only few come and are given places at the table.” And He gave me to understand that He Himself was the table, and not only the table, but the food likewise <note>In mystical theology, Christ is often described as both the Host who provides the meal, the Table that supports the soul, and the Food (the Eucharist) that nourishes it.</note>.

I did also ask by what way were those come who were come, and it was immediately told me by the way of <term>tribulation</term> <note>From the Latin "tribulatio," referring to the distress, suffering, or trials that a person undergoes. Understanding thus the reasoning and explanation, every word did greatly delight me. Yet all had He bidden and all did He equally call His children. In these words was it notified unto me that virginity, poverty, sickness, loss of children and possessions and other temporal goods, and finally that all tribulations were sent by God unto His children for their good, albeit at that time they did not know this and could not understand it. For this reason there were many greatly troubled in the beginning, but afterwards, like true sons, they endured everything with patience and thanksgiving.

Such are those who most commonly attain unto eternal life, nor is there any other way. But those who are bidden unto the special table, whom God maketh to eat out of the same dish and drink out of the same cup with Himself <note>In the Middle Ages, sharing a dish or cup was a sign of the highest intimacy and friendship, reserved for those of the same rank or special favorites.</note>, are those who desire and strive to know who is this good Father and Lord who hath bidden them to the feast, and who seek to please Him by the imitation of His most holy Cross and by voluntarily taking upon themselves poverty, contempt, <term>ignominy<gloss>Public shame, disgrace, or loss of reputation</gloss></term> and affliction. For unto such sons as these doth God send many tribulations, the which are sent unto them as an especial grace in order that they may eat out of one dish with Him. “For at that table,” said Christ, “I was called to drink the cup of the shameful <term>Passion<gloss>The period of intense spiritual and physical suffering undergone by Jesus in the garden of Gethsemane and on the Cross</gloss></term>, which was made sweet unto Me through your love, although it was of itself exceeding hard and bitter.”

->OF FOLIGNO<-

Recognizing this grace and mercy, therefore, even though they find these trials bitter, these children nevertheless consider them supremely sweet because of the love, grace, honor, and worthiness <gloss>a</gloss> contained within them. Therefore, they are no longer distressed by their troubles; rather, the greater their <term>tribulations<gloss>From the Latin 'tribulatio,' meaning distress or deep suffering; in a spiritual sense, it refers to trials that test and refine one's faith</gloss></term>, the more delighted they are and the closer they feel themselves to God. For this reason, I say and affirm that the children of God feel a divine sweetness mingled with persecutions, tribulations, and <term>penances<gloss>Acts of self-mortification or devotion performed to show sorrow for sins</gloss></term>, all of which was revealed to me in the preaching mentioned previously. I have experienced this countless times, and I could not reveal or declare the sweetness I felt or the tears of exceeding great joy that I shed when I was troubled or reviled by my brothers, my friends, or my relatives.

During a time of great sickness, as I was lying very weak, there appeared to me again God-made-Man, Jesus Christ the Consoler, and I felt that He had great compassion on me and said to me: “I have come to serve you.” The service which He rendered me was to stand before my bed, showing Himself to be so pleasing and so gracious that it is a thing wholly unspeakable. And I beheld Him more clearly with the eyes of my mind than it is possible to behold any person with the eyes of the body, and so great was my joyfulness and supreme delight that it can in no way be expressed or declared, because it is entirely <term>ineffable<gloss>Too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words</gloss></term>.

...I said to my companion: “Let us seek Jesus Christ the Crucified, and let us go to the hospital <note>In the 13th century, a 'hospital' was a place of hospitality for the poor and pilgrims as much as a place for the sick.</note>, for perhaps we shall find Him among those poor, suffering, and afflicted ones.” So we took the cloths we wore upon our heads—for we had nothing else—and gave them to the servant of the hospital. We told her to sell them and use the money to buy something for the poor of the hospital to eat. Even though she was afraid to do it, telling us that we would be mocked and reviled, we were so persistent that she eventually went and sold those poor little cloths and bought fish with the money. To this, we added the bread that had been given to us for the love of God for our own food. 

After this, we washed the feet of the poor women and the hands of the men, especially those of a <term>leper<gloss>A person suffering from leprosy (Hansen’s disease). In the Middle Ages, lepers were severely stigmatized and lived in isolation; touching them was a radical act of Christian charity.</gloss></term>, whose hands were rotting, mangled, and full of <term>corruption<gloss>In this medical context, it refers to pus, infection, or decaying flesh.</gloss></term>. Afterward, we drank the water we had used to wash him. That drink was so sweet to us that we felt its sweetness all the way back until we reached this place. And because a <note>original: "squama"</note> scale <note>A scab or piece of dried skin from the leper's sores.</note> from those sores had caught in my throat, I tried to swallow it as though I were receiving <term>communion<gloss>The sacrament of the Eucharist, where bread and wine are consumed as the body and blood of Christ.</gloss></term>. At last, I did swallow it, and I found it to be so sweet that I cannot describe it in any way.

I have told this story because although a person is troubled at the beginning by the <term>penances<gloss>Acts of self-punishment or devotion performed to show sorrow for sin.</gloss></term>, afflictions, and trials they have accepted from God, they eventually find exceedingly great consolation in them.

<summary>Angela of Foligno recounts a radical act of charity at a hospital where she and a companion sold their veils to feed the poor and washed the sores of a leper. She describes a mystical experience where drinking the wash-water and swallowing a scab from the leper provided her with divine sweetness, illustrating how spiritual trials lead to consolation.</summary>

### A FURTHER CONSOLATION GIVEN TO HER BY GOD

Another time, when I was stirred by spiritual tribulation <note>This refers to a period of "spiritual dryness" or "the dark night of the soul," where a person feels a painful distance from God.</note>, it seemed to me that for the space of a month I felt nothing whatsoever of God. Indeed, it seemed as though He had completely forsaken me, and I was not even able to confess my sins.

Although, on one hand, I thought this had happened to me because of my pride, on the other hand, I perceived my sins so plainly and with such deep conviction that I doubted if I would ever be able to confess them with proper <term>contrition<gloss>Sincere and complete remorse for one's sins, arising from a love for God.</gloss></term>, or even speak them aloud. For this reason, I could neither praise God nor pray. Yet I perceived that this alone remained to me of God: that I was not as greatly troubled as I deserved to be for having desired to turn away from God through sin. Despite this, I could not have brought myself to offend God for all the good and evil in the world, nor could I consent to wickedness. Thus, I was deeply and most terribly troubled and afflicted during the whole of that time.

Then, through the mercy and pity of God, a voice spoke to me and said: “Daughter beloved of Almighty God and of all the blessed saints in Paradise, God has placed His love in you and bears more love toward you than toward any other woman in the Valley of Spoleto <note>original: "valle Spoletana." This is a region in Umbria, central Italy, where Foligno is located; it was a heartland of the early Franciscan movement.</note>.”

Then did my soul cry aloud and answer: “How can this be?”

<summary>Angela describes a month-long period of spiritual desertion and the inability to pray, which is ended when she hears a divine voice affirming God's unique love for her.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual dryness, confession, contrition, Valley of Spoleto, divine voice, pride, mercy</keywords>

“How can I believe this, seeing that I am full of <term>tribulation<gloss>A state of great suffering or a trial of faith.</gloss></term> and seem to be forsaken by God?”

The voice made answer: 
> “When you think yourself to be most forsaken, then are you most dearly beloved and nearest to the eternal God.” 

And again he added: 
> “A father who has but one dearly beloved son administers to him delicate meats in moderation, and does not permit him to drink pure wine; instead, he mingles the wine with water and permits him to eat nothing harmful, so that his food may do him no hurt but always good. And God does likewise, mingling temptations and grievous tribulations with His consolations, and keeping the soul in these tribulations and temptations; for if He did not do this, it would plunge into intemperate enjoyments as into a sea and would be drowned. Therefore, as I have said, when the soul seems to be most forsaken, then is it most loved.”

Then were my tribulations somewhat tempered, but not wholly taken away, for I did not yet feel disposed to <term>confess<gloss>The sacrament of Penance, where one's sins are spoken to a priest.</gloss></term> and <term>communicate<gloss>The act of receiving the Eucharist (Holy Communion).</gloss></term>. But in a short time they were all taken away and thus was it told me—

> “It pleases Me that you should <term>communicate</term>—because if you receive Me, you have already received Me, and if you do not receive Me, you have received Me nonetheless. <note>This refers to the concept of "spiritual communion," where the soul's intent and love for God allow it to receive grace even if the physical sacrament is not currently possible.</note> Receive communion, therefore, with the blessing of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. And do it out of reverence for Almighty God, for Saint Mary the Virgin, and for Saint Anthony whose Feast it is today;” <note>Likely referring to Saint Anthony of Padua, a major figure in the Franciscan tradition to which Angela belonged.</note>

<summary>Angela receives a divine explanation for her spiritual suffering, hearing that God "waters down" joys with trials to protect the soul from pride. She is ultimately commanded to receive the Eucharist on the Feast of St. Anthony, despite her lingering doubts.</summary>

Wherefore I communicated in His faith, and there was granted unto me a marvellous feeling which left in me a peace whereby I was given to understand and to feel that all <term>tribulation<gloss>A state of great suffering or a trial of faith.</gloss></term> which had happened unto me was for my good. And this Communion which I received did bring my soul in faith unto this perception and desire—that is to say, I desired to give myself wholly unto Christ because He hath given Himself unto us. Wherefore do I take new delight in the longing for <term>martyrdom<gloss>The act of suffering or dying for one's religious beliefs; in mystic tradition, this often refers to a "living martyrdom" of intense spiritual and physical suffering.</gloss></term>, and I desire so much that I rejoice when grief and tribulation more than usual do fall upon me. Hence do I conclude that in the end God consoleth every troubled soul.

Thus I do remember me that, being one time afflicted—

Moreover He said unto me,

Thus in many ways did He deign to console me, He who consoleth all who are in tribulation, unto Whom be honour and glory for ever and ever.

### AN ENLIGHTENMENT OF THE UNDERSTANDING GRANTED TO HER BY GOD CONCERNING THE PATH AND THE STATE OF SALVATION

AT a certain time as I was praying in my private room <note>In this context, a "closet" refers to a small, private chamber or oratory dedicated to prayer and contemplation.</note> these words were spoken to me: “All who are instructed and enlightened by God to the end that they may understand the way of God, and who, in spite of this light and revelation especially given them by God, do shut their ears that they may not hear and their eyes that they may not see, who refuse to understand or listen to that which God says to the soul, but do more readily debase themselves and follow after other <term>doctrine<gloss>Religious teachings or instructions; here, it refers to worldly or superficial beliefs that conflict with direct divine guidance.</gloss></term> than that given them by God (desiring against their conscience to keep upon the broad highway <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 7:13, where Jesus speaks of the "broad way" that leads to destruction, contrasted with the "narrow way" to life.</note>), all these I declare to have earned the curse of Almighty God.”

Not once only, but many times, was this judgment told to me, and I was filled with horror when I heard it; it seemed to me a matter most grave, for I feared there was no deception in it—that is to say, that God did indeed curse those to whom He had first given His light and grace. Then an example was shown to me, and it was commanded of me that I should cause it to be written down; and the example was this:

“Suppose there was a father who sent his son to school, spending large sums upon him, clothing him

To illustrate the gravity of this spiritual neglect, she begins to record an allegorical example involving a father and son.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, enlightenment, salvation, divine judgment, broad highway, spiritual responsibility, grace</keywords>

<page-num>250</page-num>

->THE BLESSED ANGELA<-

“...dressing him honorably in fine clothing <term>raiment<gloss>An archaic word for garments or clothing.</gloss></term> and providing him with books. After the son has been taught by an ordinary teacher, the father sees that he is moved to a better teacher to advance higher. If this student then acts lazily and ignores the knowledge he has gained, returning instead to a common <term>vulgar<gloss>In this historical context, "vulgar" refers to a common, worldly, or unrefined state of life, rather than something offensive.</gloss></term> lifestyle and lowly jobs involving great exhaustion and toil—so that nothing remains of what he learned—the father would certainly feel great distress and anger toward his son.”

Such a son represents the person who is first taught through preaching and the **Scriptures**, and is afterwards especially enlightened by God. To this person, it is given through a special light and divine inspiration to understand how to follow the path of Christ. God first allowed others to show him this learning, and then God Himself instructed him through His own special light and **doctrine**, which can be taught by God alone. God does this so the person may know how to guide himself and serve as a light to others. 

But if such a man falls into negligence, degrading himself and becoming spiritually lazy <note>The original text uses the phrase "growing fat," a common biblical metaphor (as in Deuteronomy 32:15) for becoming complacent or indifferent to God after receiving His blessings.</note> and despising the light and divine inspiration he has received, then God will take that light and grace away and curse him instead. I was commanded to have this written down so that I might tell it to the brother <note>This refers to Angela’s confessor and scribe, likely the Franciscan friar known as Brother Arnaldo.</note> to whom I make my <term>confession<gloss>The religious practice of disclosing one's sins to a priest for spiritual healing and forgiveness.</gloss></term>, because this message was specifically for him.

<summary>Angela presents a parable of a father's disappointment in a lazy son to illustrate how God views those who squander divine enlightenment. She concludes by noting that this specific revelation was intended as a warning for her own confessor.</summary>

In another heavenly conversation was I told that there is a certain generation <note>In this context, "generation" refers to a specific type, class, or category of people.</note> which knoweth God only through the manifold goodness of the good things He hath done unto them, and that these know Him but little. And I was told that there is another generation of men who, albeit they know God through the aforesaid good things, do nevertheless know Him much better through His goodness which they experience in themselves.

In yet another conversation I heard a voice crying and saying, 

> “ Oh, how great are they ! How great are they ! Not the great readers of My **Scriptures**, but those who do them and fulfil them ; for the whole of the divine **Scriptures** are fulfilled in the example of the **Life of Christ Jesus**.”

Once when I was at prayer I said unto God—

> “ I know, O Lord, that Thou art my Father, for Thou art my God ; teach Thou me, therefore, that which Thou wouldst have me do, and instruct me in that which pleaseth Thee, for I am ready to obey Thee.”

Now reflecting upon these words from morning even until the hour of <term>tierce<gloss>The "third hour" of the day in the traditional liturgical calendar, roughly 9:00 AM.</gloss></term>, I beheld and heard, but that which I beheld and heard am I in no wise able to declare. But it was a most unspeakable abyss <note>A mystical term used to describe the profound, bottomless, and incomprehensible nature of God's presence.</note>, and it was shown me what it was, who dwelt therein and who dwelt not therein. And God said—

> “ Of a truth there is no other straight way than that which followeth My footsteps, for upon this road none are deceived.”

### THE LAST WRITING OF THE BLESSED ANGELA OF FOLIGNO

**T**his is the last writing and the last letter given by our Mother, the Blessed Angela of Foligno, before she fell sick unto death, and which she said was the last of her letters. She foresaw her happy passing away a long time before it happened, wherefore with great affection did she constrain her writer to write thus, saying—

> “ Oh my God, Jesus Christ, make me worthy that I may know Thy most high mystery, which hath been wrought by Thy most ardent charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to *caritas*, the Latin term for the highest form of selfless, divine love.</note>. That is to say, the most high and profound mystery of Thy most holy Incarnation <note>The "Incarnation" is the Christian belief that God took on human form in the person of Jesus.</note>, which Thou hast wrought for our sake and which hath been the beginning of our salvation, for it doeth two things unto us. Firstly, it filleth us with love; and secondly, it maketh us to be verily certain of our salvation.

> “ Oh, how unspeakable is this charity! Truly there is none greater than this, that my God, the Creator of all, should have become a creature in order that He might

fifthly, that hereby we may know also how the Son of God did make manifest the Father, by the obedience which He did observe throughout His whole life, even unto the bitter death of the Cross, and hath given <term>satisfaction</term> <note>In medieval theology, "satisfaction" refers to the idea that Christ’s suffering and death paid the "debt" or penalty for human sin, restoring the relationship between humanity and God.</note> for the whole human race. Make me worthy, oh Thou <term>God Uncreate</term> <note>God Uncreate: A theological term emphasizing that God is the eternal source of all things and was not Himself created by any other power.</note>, to know the depths of Thy love and the unfathomable extent of Thy ardent <term>charity</term> <note>original: *caritas*. Oh marvellous, indescribable, and joyful love, in Thee is all savour and sweetness and all delight, the contemplation whereof exalteth the soul above the world, making it able to stand alone in joy, peace and rest.

“ The fourth mystery is in the triumphant Resurrection, in the which two things chiefly are to be considered. The first is, that His resurrection giveth us hope of our own; and the second is, that it maketh us perfectly to know the spiritual resurrection which God worketh of His grace when He raiseth up a dead man to life again and restoreth the sick to health. Oh most high, unspeakable, unknown and <term>ineffable</term> <note>Ineffable: Something that is so great or sacred that it cannot be expressed in words.</note> mystery wherein Thou hast shown the fulfilment of our perfection, make me, oh Lord, worthy of knowing this most high mystery.

...sufficiencies which make souls proud, such as power, honour and preferment,—fly ye from them, for in them lieth great deception and danger. And greater deception still is there in <term>spiritual self-sufficiency</term> <note>A term describing a form of religious pride where one relies on their own knowledge, rituals, or feelings of holiness rather than on God's grace.</note> even than in the worldly—that is to say, in knowing how to speak of God, in understanding the Scriptures, in performing great penances and keeping the heart busied over spiritual things. For the spiritually self-sufficient do often fall into error and are more difficult to correct than those who have worldly self-sufficiency. Esteem yourselves, therefore, as nothing, as nothing known or nothing unknown. Of a truth, the soul can possess no better insight or knowledge than to perceive its own <term>nothingness</term> <note>Angela’s theology often focuses on the "double knowledge": knowing the "All" of God and the "nothingness" of the self.</note> and to remain within its own prison.

> “Strive ye, oh my sons, to possess <term>charity</term> <note>original: caritas. This refers to the highest form of Christian love—the selfless, divine love that unites the soul with God.</note>, without which there is neither salvation nor merit. Behold, God saith, ‘All My things are thine.’ <note>A reference to Luke 15:31, "Son, thou art ever with me, and all that I have is thine."</note> Who is there who deserveth this, that all God’s things should be his? Verily, there is naught else that deserveth this save the most ardent charity.

> “Oh my sons, fathers and brothers, see that ye love one another amongst yourselves and that ye have true charity and love; for if it hath this, the soul deserveth to inherit the divine possessions. In Angela's writing, this refers to the supernatural love of God that allows a person to love others with God's own heart.</note> which bringeth this about is not of this world.

“Oh my children, strive to possess this charity and judge not any man, even though ye should behold him commit a <term>mortal sin</term> <note>A "death-dealing" sin according to Church teaching; a grave action committed with full knowledge and consent that separates the soul from God.</note>. I do not say that sin should not be displeasing unto you and that ye should not hold it in the greatest horror, but I do say that ye should never judge the sinners, and moreover I say that ye should never despise them, for ye know not the judgments of our Lord God; and many are there who do appear unto men to be condemned unto hell, but who are saved in the sight of God, and many who appear unto men to be saved are <term>reprobate</term> <note>A theological term for those rejected by God and destined for damnation.</note> in the sight of God and condemned unto hell. I could tell you of some whom ye have despised, but of whom I have the sure hope that God will lead them back into His own Hand.

“I will make no other <term>testament</term> <note>A final will or legacy. Angela is modeling her final words on the "Testament" of St. Francis of Assisi.</note>, saving that I do commend unto you this mutual love and profound humility. And all mine heritage do I leave unto you, the which is likewise that of Christ Jesus, namely <term>poverty, suffering, and scorn</term> <note>This "triple heritage" is the core of Franciscan spirituality, mirroring the life of the crucified Christ.</note>. Your life is that of Jesus Christ. Those who shall possess this heritage of the Life of Christ shall be my children, because they are the children of God; wherefore is there no doubt that they shall possess the heritage of eternal life.”

### THE PASSING AWAY OF THE BLESSED ANGELA

**About** the Feast of the Nativity of our Lord Jesus Christ <note>Christmas Day. Angela died in early January 1309, during the traditional liturgical season of the Nativity.</note> (at which time she passed away unto Christ), as she lay very sick, she said, “The Word is made Flesh” <note>original: "Verbum caro factum est." This is a quote from the Gospel of John referring to the Incarnation, the mystery of God becoming human.</note>, and after a long silence, as though she were returning from some other place, she said, “Oh, every creature faileth, and the whole of the angelic understanding sufficeth not to comprehend this.” Then she added, “My soul is washed and cleansed in the blood of Jesus Christ, the which was as fresh and warm as though it had but this moment issued from the body of the <term>Crucified</term> <note>A title for Jesus Christ that emphasizes his suffering and death on the cross as a central point of meditation.</note>.”

After this she said: “Christ Jesus, the Son of God, hath now presented me unto the Father, and these words have been spoken unto me—

> “And he showed me the robe, even as the bridegroom showeth it unto the bride whom he hath loved a long time. It was neither of purple nor of scarlet, nor of <term>sendal</term> <note>A thin, rich silk fabric used in the Middle Ages for high-quality clothing and vestments.</note>, nor of <term>samite</term> <note>A heavy, luxurious silk fabric, often woven with gold or silver threads, associated with royalty and the wealthy.</note>, but it was a certain marvellous light which clothed the soul. Now when she was nigh unto the time of her passing away (being the day before it), she did often repeat,

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page where Christ, the Eternal Word, promised to lead Angela to her heavenly home. ### THE BLESSED ANGELA

"Now has an answer to those words been given to me, and it is this: ‘It is impossible that in death you should lose that which has been impressed upon your heart in life.’" Then we said to her, "Will you, then, depart and leave us?" And she answered—"A long time have I hidden it from you, but now will I conceal it no longer. I say to you that I must truly depart."

And upon that same day all those sufferings ceased with which for many days previously she had been grievously tormented in all her limbs, both within and without, and afflicted in many ways. And she did then lie in such repose of the body and cheerfulness of spirit that she appeared as though she already tasted something of the joy which had been promised to her.

Then we asked her whether that aforementioned joy had yet been granted to her, and she replied that the joy had commenced.

And in this repose of body and cheerfulness of spirit she lay very joyfully until the hour of <term>Compline</term> <note>The final prayer service of the day in the traditional Christian liturgy, held just before retiring for the night.</note> on the Saturday, surrounded by many of the brethren who administered the <term>Holy Mysteries</term> <note>A term for the Sacraments, specifically referring here to the final reception of the Eucharist and the Last Rites.</note> to her. And upon that same day—that is to say, upon the <term>Octave of the Innocents</term> <note>The eighth day following the Feast of the Holy Innocents on December 28th, marking the date as January 4th.</note>—at the last hour of the day, she gently fell asleep and rested in peace. And that most holy soul was set free from the flesh, and being drawn into the abyss of the divine infinitude, it received from Christ, its Bridegroom, the garment of innocence and immortality.

### *The End of the Testament and the Death of Blessed Angela.*

The venerable bride of Christ, <term>Angela of Foligno</term> <note>A renowned Italian mystic (c. 1248–1309) and member of the Secular Franciscan Order, known for her "Book of Visions and Instructions."</note>, passed from the shipwreck of this world <note>A common medieval metaphor comparing the dangers and temptations of earthly life to a perilous sea voyage.</note> unto the joys of heaven, which had long been promised unto her, in the year of our Lord’s <term>Incarnation</term> <note>The traditional Christian method of dating years from the birth of Jesus Christ.</note>, one thousand, three hundred and nine, on the fourth day of January, in the time of <term>Pope Clement the Fifth</term> <note>The Pope who reigned from 1305 to 1314 and is famous for moving the papal residence to Avignon, France.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the spiritual testament of Angela of Foligno with a final prayer for her followers and records the official date of her death on January 4, 1309.</summary>
<keywords>Angela of Foligno, death, 1309, Pope Clement V, spiritual testament, Franciscan mysticism</keywords>
